\title{%
  \textbf{Privacy-Preserving Vertical Federated Regression}\\[4pt]
  \large with Threshold Decryption and Differentially Private Releases\\[8pt]
  \normalsize Working draft --- protocol v3.1, honest-but-curious model
}
\date{\today}

\begin{document}

\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
We study two-party vertical federated least squares between a researcher $\Rp$, who holds a cohort with features $\bXR$, and an organization $\Op$ (for example a national statistical agency), which holds a register with features $\bXO$ and the response $\by$. Records are linked by labeled private set intersection with an oblivious pseudorandom function: $\Rp$ learns which of its cohort members are in $\Op$'s register and where, and $\Op$ learns only public sizes. The statistics that depend on $\Op$'s data are aggregated under two-party threshold CKKS. The secret key is split additively between the parties, so neither can decrypt alone, and $\Op$ never decrypts at all. Instead, $\Op$ adds the differential-privacy noise inside its share of each decryption, as an exact discrete Gaussian on the coefficient lattice that also floods the encryption error. $\Rp$ therefore receives only noisy statistics, and $\Op$ can enforce a privacy budget by withholding its share. $\Rp$'s own Gram block is computed locally and is never noised. Each run makes two releases. The first, of the Gram blocks only, chooses the ridge. The second, whose noise is independent of that choice, gives the estimate, a Wald interval for the ridge target from a parametric bootstrap of the mechanism, and a bias-aware interval for the regression coefficients. We give the exact replace-one sensitivity, zCDP accounting with a tight conversion, simulation-based output privacy against an honest-but-curious researcher, and the statistical guarantees of the two-stage procedure. We evaluate two settings of the protocol, each chosen on synthetic data alone by a pre-registered rule: setting I (inference) keeps the intervals and sizes the stage-1 radius by an exact Monte Carlo quantile; setting P (prediction) gives up the intervals for a smaller ridge, subject to its gate refusing at most $1\%$ of releases on both standardized and block-normalized synthetic features (a first choice, made on standardized features only, was refused on most block-normalized releases). On a synthetic population at $\delta = 10^{-9}$, the Wald intervals under setting I cover $94.5$--$96.6\%$ in every cell (target $95.5\%$), with and without the ridge, and $94.5$--$96.4\%$ on fixed cohorts of five real datasets with the calibrated noise of a real budget. Setting I switches the stage-1 ridge off $35\%$ sooner than the previous setting ($3.9$k to $53$k matched records for $\varepsilon = 8$ to $0.5$), and prediction comes within $10\%$ of non-private test error at $2.9$k to $38$k matched records under I and $2.1$k to $26$k under P. Once the ridge is off, I's coefficient error is slightly higher: it reaches twice that of non-private least squares at $8.0$k, $27$k and $88$k matched records for $\varepsilon = 8$, $4$ and $2$. On real data with each party normalizing its own block of every row, prediction comes within $10\%$ of non-private test error on protein at $5.0$k and $2.5$k matched records for $\varepsilon = 1$ and $2$ under setting P ($8.2$k and $4.1$k under I), and P's gate refuses at most $1\%$ of releases on bike, elevators, pol and protein. Against AdaSSP on the joined table at $\varepsilon = 1$, $\delta = 10^{-6}$, v3.1 with block normalization has higher test error on $20$ of $29$ datasets under either setting, with a median gap of $+14\%$ under P and $+25\%$ under I ($+28\%$ at the previous setting), because its ridge is still larger than that of AdaSSP under the same guarantee ($1.8$ times under P and $2.8$ times under I, with joint normalization). Against three further baselines, noising $\Rp$'s own block as well (full-record SSP) raises test error by a median of about $1\%$ on the 29 datasets; TukeyEM, which needs no bounds, fails its stability test and releases nothing on 25 of them at $\varepsilon \le 2$; and the parametric bootstrap of Ferrando et al., applied to our release, gives the same interval for the coefficients as ours once the ridge is off, and a finite one that covers, where ours is unbounded, from about a quarter of the ridge threshold on the synthetic population. End-to-end runs of a full OpenFHE implementation reproduce a plaintext simulation of the mechanism under every setting evaluated; in $450$ runs under settings I and P the decrypted noise has its calibrated law, with mean zero to within $0.001$ of its standard deviation over $14.7$ million values. At $10^6$ register rows and $20{,}000$ padded queries a full run takes $2.0$ minutes on a 4-core machine with $\Op$'s OPRF evaluation parallel ($\Op$: $91$\,s and $2.3$\,GB; $475$\,MB of traffic; $3.0$ minutes with it single-threaded), with the linkage exact in all $78$ large runs. On the protein dataset ($19{,}206$ matched records, previous setting), after each party normalizes its own block of every row, they come within $2$--$6\%$ of non-private test error at $\varepsilon \in \{1, 4\}$.
\end{abstract}

% ==============================================================
\section{Introduction}
% ==============================================================

Organizations such as Statistics Canada (StatCan) hold administrative and survey datasets that span the full Canadian population. University researchers, by contrast, often hold smaller cohort datasets enriched with domain-specific variables---clinical measurements, survey responses, or behavioural signals---that StatCan does not collect. Neither dataset alone is sufficient to answer certain research questions: a policy researcher studying labour-market outcomes might hold a cohort's health and education records, while StatCan holds their tax filing history, employment insurance claims, and income trajectories. The scientific value of joining these datasets is evident; the barrier to doing so is equally clear. Privacy law, institutional policy, and the practical realities of data governance make direct data sharing infeasible.

The Research Data Centre (RDC) model partially addresses this by letting researchers work with anonymized StatCan data inside a controlled environment. However, it does not permit a researcher to bring their own dataset and perform a record linkage against StatCan records in order to jointly train a statistical model---a process that typically requires six to eight weeks of vetting, human review, and institutional approvals. This overhead is a meaningful bottleneck on the pace and scope of Canadian policy research.

This document proposes a cryptographic protocol for \emph{vertical federated learning} (VFL) between a researcher and StatCan. The term ``vertical'' refers to the structure of the data: both parties hold overlapping sets of individuals (rows) but non-overlapping sets of variables (columns). The goal is to jointly fit a regression model over the combined column space without either party ever observing the other's raw data. The two datasets are linked by a common identifier, but the linkage itself must remain private: StatCan should not learn which of its records belong to the researcher's cohort.

\paragraph{Scope.} We treat the case in which \textbf{StatCan holds the response variable} $\by$ (e.g.\ tax-reported income, employment status, benefit receipt), and the researcher wishes to learn whether their cohort-specific covariates predict that outcome, using StatCan's covariates as controls. Both parties are honest-but-curious.

\paragraph{The design in one paragraph.} Linkage is labeled private set intersection (PSI) on oblivious-PRF values of the identifiers, after which $\Rp$ knows its matches and their positions in $\Op$'s secretly shuffled register. $\Rp$ computes its own Gram block exactly and uploads its features, aligned to $\Op$'s order and zero elsewhere, encrypted under its share of a two-party CKKS key. $\Op$ converts them to the joint key and multiplies them by its own plaintext columns, which gives encryptions of every statistic that involves its data. A release is a threshold decryption in which $\Op$ computes its share of the decryption, adds discrete-Gaussian noise to it, and sends it without the part of the ciphertext that would let $\Rp$ remove the noise. $\Op$ never decrypts anything, $\Rp$ never sees a noiseless statistic that depends on $\Op$'s data, and every decryption needs $\Op$'s cooperation, so $\Op$ can refuse once the budget is spent.

\paragraph{Contributions.} Each ingredient of the protocol has a precedent (Section~\ref{sec:related}): DP noise added at collective decryption, DP noise on partial decryptions as a defence for threshold approximate HE, noisy sufficient statistics for vertical linear regression, and a block that each data owner computes locally. What is new is their combination, made precise in the items below; Section~\ref{sec:related} says what we do not claim.
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep]
  \item \textbf{Protocol.} A two-party protocol that combines OPRF-based labeled PSI, a seeded upload under $\Rp$'s key share converted by $\Op$, plaintext--ciphertext aggregation, and a release in which $\Op$'s decryption share carries the DP noise (Section~\ref{sec:protocol}, Algorithms~\ref{alg:setup}--\ref{alg:sub}).
  \item \textbf{Privacy.} The exact replace-one sensitivity of the released statistics; a release whose single noise term, an exact lattice discrete Gaussian in $\Op$'s decryption share, is exactly $\rho_s$-zCDP and also floods every encryption error term against a worst-case bound; simulation-based output privacy; composition of runs, fixed or adaptively chosen, under a per-register ledger with a privacy filter; input and cohort privacy (Sections~\ref{sec:privacy} and~\ref{sec:input}).
  \item \textbf{Parameters.} The protocol and every theorem are stated for a parameter vector with explicit admissibility constraints; \textsc{Calibrate} derives the flooding parameter from the budget and the ledger cap, and the scale and the noise from both (Section~\ref{sec:params}).
  \item \textbf{Statistics.} A two-stage ridge whose choice is independent of the noise in the estimation release; two named estimands; a Wald interval from a parametric bootstrap of the mechanism, after Ferrando et al.~\cite{ferrando2022parametric}; a bias-aware interval for the regression coefficients, after Armstrong et al.~\cite{armstrong2020bias}; and the leading-order mechanism bias with an optional correction (Section~\ref{sec:stats} and Appendix~\ref{sec:bias}).
  \item \textbf{Preprocessing.} Per-party transforms with public constants that make typical records fill the clipping bounds, which no joint computation is needed for (Section~\ref{sec:protocol:preproc}).
  \item \textbf{Evaluation.} Exactness of the cryptographic layer, the calibration of the two-release protocol, interval coverage, end-to-end runs of a full implementation on real and synthetic data, coefficient accuracy against the intersection size, benchmarks against AdaSSP, vertical full-record SSP and TukeyEM on 29 UCI datasets, and a comparison with the parametric-bootstrap intervals of Ferrando et al., under two named settings of the protocol chosen on synthetic data by pre-registered rules: one for inference and one for prediction (Section~\ref{sec:eval}).
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Specification and proofs.} The specification of v3.1~\cite{vfrspec} and the companion proofs~\cite{vfrproofs} are in the repository, under \texttt{docs/}. References of the form ``proofs, Part~A'' are to the proofs document: Part~A~\cite{vfrproofsA}, Part~B~\cite{vfrproofsB} and the composition appendix~\cite{vfrproofsC}. Appendix~\ref{app:status} summarizes their status.

% ==============================================================
\section{Related work}
\label{sec:related}
% ==============================================================

\tocheck{The bibliographic details of the entries cited in this section are taken from the literature review's verified reference list, and the fields that list omits (some given names, volumes and pages) are omitted here too. Three are still unverified. (a) UnLynx~\cite{froelicher2017unlynx} is not in the verified list. Its authors, title, issue, pages and DOI were confirmed only through a search-engine summary of the PoPETs page, and the description of its noise protocol comes from the Drynx paper, through search-engine extracts; check it against the UnLynx text. (b) Ion et al.~\cite{ion2020deploying} is not in the verified list. The title of ePrint 2019/723 and its nine-author list conflict with Google Research's listing of the EuroS\&P~2020 paper, which has a different title and six authors; the bibliography gives the ePrint title and the first two authors only. Confirm the published title, the authors and the pages ($370$--$389$). (c) The later title and the venue of Hwang et al.~\cite{hwang2025insecurity}: see Check~(T1) in Section~\ref{sec:privacy:mech}.}

The protocol draws on secure regression for vertically partitioned data, differentially private regression and inference, threshold homomorphic encryption, and private set intersection. We review each and then state what is new.

\paragraph{Secure regression on vertically partitioned data.} The statistics literature on this problem predates federated learning. Sanil, Karr, Lin and Reiter~\cite{sanil2004privacy} compute exact global regression coefficients and goodness-of-fit measures for agencies that hold different attributes of the same records, without revealing the attributes. Karr, Lin, Sanil and Reiter~\cite{karr2009privacy} compute the off-diagonal blocks of the covariance matrix by secure matrix products between pairs of owners, and each owner computes its own diagonal block locally. They release coefficients, standard errors and diagnostics, use no third party, and assume that the records are already aligned. Our aggregation has the same block structure: $\Rp$ computes $\bA$ locally, and only the blocks that involve $\Op$'s data are computed jointly. Du, Han and Chen~\cite{du2004privacy} give two-party protocols for multivariate linear regression. Hall, Fienberg and Nardi~\cite{hall2011secure} compute linear and ridge regression under homomorphic encryption, release only the final result, and test the method on the Current Population Survey. In cryptography and machine learning, Nikolaenko et al.~\cite{nikolaenko2013privacy} combine linearly homomorphic encryption with garbled circuits for ridge regression on hundreds of millions of records, and Giacomelli et al.~\cite{giacomelli2018privacy} use linearly homomorphic encryption alone. Gasc\'on et al.~\cite{gascon2017privacy} solve vertical linear regression with garbled circuits, inner-product protocols and a fixed-point conjugate-gradient method. Hardy et al.~\cite{hardy2017private} link records by privacy-preserving entity resolution and train logistic regression under additively homomorphic encryption, with a coordinator as a third party. SecureML~\cite{mohassel2017secureml} trains linear and logistic regression on two servers, and the FATE platform~\cite{liu2021fate} includes PSI and two-party linear regression among its components. Yang et al.~\cite{yang2019federated} and Liu et al.~\cite{liu2024vertical} survey federated and vertical federated learning. All of these give the analyst the exact model or exact statistics. None gives the output a differential-privacy guarantee.

\paragraph{Differentially private linear regression.} Sufficient-statistics perturbation (SSP) adds noise to $\bX^\top\bX$, $\bX^\top\by$ and $\by^\top\by$ and solves from the noisy statistics. Vu and Slavkovi\'c~\cite{vu2009differential} add noise to sufficient statistics for tests on clinical-trial data. The functional mechanism of Zhang et al.~\cite{zhang2012functional} perturbs the coefficients of the polynomial objective, which for least squares is SSP with Laplace noise. Foulds et al.~\cite{foulds2016theory} use Laplace SSP for conjugate exponential-family posteriors. AdaSSP~\cite{wang2018revisiting} adds a ridge chosen from a private estimate of the smallest eigenvalue of the Gram matrix. Other estimators perturb the objective~\cite{chaudhuri2011differentially,kifer2012private}. TukeyEM~\cite{amin2023easy} fits least squares on disjoint subsets and selects among the fits by the exponential mechanism on approximate Tukey depth; it needs no bounds on the features or the labels. Insufficient statistics perturbation~\cite{brown2024insufficient} has error linear in the dimension and independent of the condition number for data of bounded leverage. All of these assume one curator who holds the joined table, and they protect the whole record. Our ridge rule borrows AdaSSP's idea of choosing $\lambda$ from a private eigenvalue bound, but takes the bound from a separate release, so that the estimation noise is independent of $\lambda$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:indep}).

\paragraph{Differentially private inference.} Sheffet~\cite{sheffet2017differentially} derives $t$-values and confidence intervals for differentially private ordinary least squares. Ferrando, Wang and Sheldon~\cite{ferrando2022parametric} form intervals by a parametric bootstrap of the mechanism, which accounts for sampling and privacy noise together, and prove consistency for a least-squares variant. Our Wald interval applies their idea to a ridge target with noise on only part of the statistics (Section~\ref{sec:stats:ci}). Evans, King, Schwenzfeier and Thakurta~\cite{evans2023statistically} give bias-corrected estimates with valid uncertainty from privacy-protected data. Brown et al.~\cite{brown2024private} derive instance-specific intervals from private gradient descent for least squares. Lin, Slavkovi\'c and Bhoomireddy~\cite{lin2026differentially} give a bias-corrected estimator for linear regression with asymptotic intervals under Gaussian DP. Barrientos, Reiter, Machanavajjhala and Chen~\cite{barrientos2019differentially} test whether a regression coefficient would be significant on the confidential data when the analyst sees only synthetic or protected outputs; the test adds Laplace noise to the mean of truncated $t$-statistics over subsamples. All of these are central methods. Our bias-aware interval for $\bbeta$ follows Armstrong, Koles\'ar and Kwon~\cite{armstrong2020bias}, with the ridge bias bounded through a public or a private bound on $\|\bbeta\|$.

\paragraph{Differential privacy on vertically partitioned data.} Dwork and Nissim~\cite{dwork2004privacy} extend noisy statistical databases to vertically partitioned databases. Xu, Yuan and Wu~\cite{xu2019achieving} apply the functional mechanism to vertically partitioned multiparty learning, including linear regression. A server splits the objective into single-party and cross-party terms, the parties perturb the polynomial coefficients, and one round of noise addition and secure aggregation gives the utility of the centralized functional mechanism. Differentially private vertical linear regression from noisy sufficient statistics is therefore not new. Wu et al.~\cite{wu2022differentially} let parties that hold disjoint attributes of the same subjects each release differentially private data. They show that a direct Gaussian mechanism suffers from small eigenvalues and give a release from which the regression converges to the non-private solution, without cryptography. Mohammed et al.~\cite{mohammed2014secure} release differentially private data from two parties with a two-party exponential mechanism. Heinze-Deml, McWilliams and Meinshausen~\cite{heinzedeml2017preserving} share perturbed random projections of each party's features. Other work adds noise to the intermediate results of iterative training~\cite{wang2020hybrid,dejong2022differentially}. None of these links records; they assume aligned rows. Krichene et al.~\cite{krichene2024private} treat some features as public and add noise only to the sufficient statistics that involve the private features or the labels. Our guarantee has this shape: $\Rp$'s features are fixed under the adjacency (Definition~\ref{def:adj}), so $\bA$ needs no noise.

\paragraph{Two-party and computational differential privacy.} McGregor et al.~\cite{mcgregor2010limits} show that information-theoretic two-party differentially private protocols lose accuracy on functions such as the inner product and the Hamming distance. Computational DP~\cite{mironov2009computational} asks for privacy only against computationally bounded adversaries. Our guarantee to $\Rp$ is SIM-CDP in this sense (Definition~\ref{def:simcdp}). It is also one-sided: DP protects $\Op$'s records from $\Rp$, while $\Rp$'s data are protected from $\Op$ by encryption alone (G1 and G2 in Table~\ref{tab:guarantees}).

\paragraph{Distributed noise and noise inside the encryption.} Dwork et al.~\cite{dwork2006our} let the parties generate Gaussian or exponential noise jointly, so that no trusted curator is needed. Shi et al.~\cite{shi2011privacy} let an aggregator decrypt only the sum of users' encrypted values, with the randomness distributed among the users so that the sum stays differentially private even if some users are compromised. Kairouz, Liu and Steinke~\cite{kairouz2021distributed} analyse sums of discrete Gaussians under secure aggregation. In these schemes the noise is split so that no single party knows all of it. In v3.1 all of it comes from $\Op$, the party whose records it protects; Remark~\ref{rem:whoknows} discusses what $\Op$'s knowledge of the noise implies. Stevens et al.~\cite{stevens2022efficient} use the LWE error of the encryption as DP noise in secure aggregation. Ogilvie~\cite{ogilvie2024differential} uses the CKKS error as DP noise, with ridge regression by gradient descent as the case study. Our release reuses noise in the other direction: the DP noise floods the encryption error.

\paragraph{Threshold homomorphic encryption with noise at decryption.} Threshold fully homomorphic encryption goes back to Asharov et al.~\cite{asharov2012multiparty}, and Boneh et al.~\cite{boneh2018threshold} smudge partial decryptions with noise. Mouchet et al.~\cite{mouchet2021multiparty} give $N$-out-of-$N$ multiparty HE from RLWE, with collective key generation and collective key switching; we use their key generation with two parties (Section~\ref{sec:blocks:he}). Froelicher, Hubaux and coauthors build federated-analytics systems that attach DP noise to the collective decryption. UnLynx~\cite{froelicher2017unlynx} and Drynx~\cite{froelicher2020drynx} encrypt under a collective EC-ElGamal key. For a differentially private answer, the computing nodes shuffle a list of encrypted Laplace noise values in turn and add the first entry of the shuffled list to the encrypted result, before the collective key switch to the querier. SPINDLE~\cite{froelicher2021scalable} trains generalized linear models under RLWE multiparty CKKS and states that collectively generated noise should be added to the result before the distributed key switch. FAMHE~\cite{froelicher2021truly} lets the parties choose whether the querier receives exact or differentially private results, without specifying a mechanism. In all four the data are horizontally partitioned, and the noise is added under encryption before the key switch, not inside a decryption share. Nor is it used to flood a decryption error; the ElGamal-based systems have none. Kluczniak and Santato~\cite{kluczniak2025circuit} supply the cryptographic half of our release. In threshold and multikey approximate HE, they apply a differentially private mechanism to the partial decryptions, with noise sized to the worst-case error growth, and obtain IND-CPA-style security against an adversary who also sees the partial decryptions. Our release uses noise of this kind. What we add is that the same noise is also the zCDP noise of the released statistic, with a simulation-based proof at a concrete flooding parameter (Section~\ref{sec:privacy:mech}).

\paragraph{Attacks on approximate homomorphic encryption.} Li and Micciancio~\cite{li2021security} recover the CKKS secret key from decryptions that the adversary sees (IND-CPA$^{D}$ security). Li, Micciancio, Schultz and Sorrell~\cite{li2022securing} add Gaussian noise after decryption to restore security, with nearly matching bounds on the noise needed. Guo et al.~\cite{guo2024key} recover the key in OpenFHE when the flooding noise is set from non-worst-case noise estimates. Checri et al.~\cite{checri2024practical} show that threshold BFV, BGV and CKKS are exposed to such attacks unless smudging noise is added after partial decryption. Hwang et al.~\cite{hwang2025insecurity} show that two-party CKKS protocols in which each party adds its own smudging noise meet only a relaxed simulation notion when both parties have inputs. Section~\ref{sec:privacy:mech} shows how v3.1 answers the first three: $\Op$'s share, the only partial decryption that is sent, carries noise calibrated to a worst-case bound. Remark~\ref{rem:whoknows} explains why the fourth does not affect the guarantee to $\Rp$ but does apply to $\Op$.

\paragraph{Private set intersection and computation on the intersection.} Labeled PSI from homomorphic encryption~\cite{chen2017fast,chen2018labeled,cong2021labeled} builds on PSI by oblivious polynomial evaluation~\cite{freedman2004efficient} and on cuckoo hashing~\cite{pagh2004cuckoo}. We key the items with the 2HashDH oblivious PRF~\cite{jarecki2014round}, specified in RFC~9497~\cite{rfc9497}. In these protocols the receiver learns the intersection. Circuit-PSI~\cite{pinkas2019efficient,rindal2021vole} gives the parties secret shares instead, and would hide the match set from $\Rp$ as well (Remark~\ref{rem:modeM}). Other protocols compute on the intersection without revealing it. The protocol of Ion et al.~\cite{ion2020deploying} reveals only the cardinality of the intersection and the sum of the values attached to it. Buddhavarapu et al.~\cite{buddhavarapu2020private} give private matching protocols whose outputs are secret-shared, for later computation. Lepoint et al.~\cite{lepoint2021private} compute inner products over the intersection from PIR with default, hiding even its size. In v3.1 $\Rp$ learns the match, which lets it compute $\bA$ exactly and locally. Lin, Paquette and Kolaczyk~\cite{lin2024differentially} study DP linear regression after probabilistic record linkage, under central DP, with error bounds that separate the linkage error, the estimation error and the cost of privacy. Our linkage is exact on identifiers, and linkage error is outside our model (Section~\ref{sec:ext}).

\paragraph{Statistical agencies.} Bogdanov et al.~\cite{bogdanov2016students} joined about ten million Estonian tax records with about half a million education records under secure multiparty computation for a statistical study. Dasylva et al.~\cite{dasylva2025linking} link trade data held by different national statistical offices, Statistics Canada among them, by PSI. Reiter, Oganian and Karr~\cite{reiter2009verification} propose verification servers, which tell an analyst how closely results from altered public-use data match those from the confidential data; the tests of Barrientos et al.~\cite{barrientos2019differentially} are a differentially private check of this kind. Barrientos, Williams, Snoke and Bowen~\cite{barrientos2024feasibility} evaluate DP summary statistics and regression on IRS tax data and the Current Population Survey, and report that accurate DP regression estimates and intervals were hard to obtain. The application setting is therefore not new. None of these works gives a DP release, under the agency's control, on a cohort that the researcher brings.

\paragraph{What is new.} Each ingredient has a precedent, and we do not claim them:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item DP noise attached to collective decryption (UnLynx, Drynx, SPINDLE, FAMHE);
  \item DP noise on partial decryptions as a defence for threshold approximate HE (Kluczniak and Santato);
  \item noisy sufficient statistics for vertical linear regression (Xu, Yuan and Wu);
  \item each owner's block computed locally (Karr et al.);
  \item noise only on the statistics that involve private data (Krichene et al.).
\end{itemize}
We found no prior protocol that combines them as follows:
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep, label=(\roman*)]
  \item vertically partitioned data linked by OPRF-based labeled PSI and aggregated under two-party threshold CKKS;
  \item a release in which a single party's decryption share carries an exactly sampled discrete Gaussian that is at once the zCDP noise for that party's records and the worst-case flooding noise for the CKKS error, with a simulation-based proof (Theorem~\ref{thm:output});
  \item $\Rp$'s block kept exact and local, with the adjacency and the noise covering $\Op$'s records only;
  \item a two-stage ridge whose choice is independent of the estimation noise, on which the validity of the Wald and bias-aware intervals rests (Proposition~\ref{prop:indep}, Section~\ref{sec:stats:ci});
  \item a per-register ledger that composes runs, fixed or chosen adaptively, under a privacy filter~\cite{rogers2016odometers}, with the flooding slack accounted next to the zCDP cost (Section~\ref{sec:privacy:ledger}).
\end{enumerate}

% ==============================================================
\section{Setting and notation}
\label{sec:setup}
% ==============================================================

$\Op$ restricts its register to a study scope $D_\Phi = \{j : \Phi(j)\}$, where the predicate $\Phi$ uses public attributes only (for example province and birth-year band), and pads $D_\Phi$ with dummy rows---fresh random identifiers that never match, with $\bx_O = \mathbf 0$ and $y = 0$---to a public size $\bar n_O$. $\Rp$ holds $n_R \le n_R^{\max}$ cohort records. Let $I$ be the cohort members that appear in $D_\Phi$ and $n = |I|$. Table~\ref{tab:notation} collects the notation.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering\small
\caption{Notation. $\rho$ always denotes a zCDP budget, $\Delta$ a sensitivity, $\Omega$ the CKKS scale and $\vartheta$ a validity ratio.}
\label{tab:notation}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Symbol} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\midrule
$\Phi$, $\bar n_O$, $n_R^{\max}$ & study scope (public attributes); padded register size; certified cohort cap \\
$n$, $I$, $\tau$, $\pi$ & matched records; $\Rp$'s matched cohort members; $\Op$'s secret shuffle of its padded rows; alignment $\pi(i) = \tau(j)$ for $i \in I$ \\
$d_R$, $d_O$, $p$ & features at $\Rp$ and at $\Op$; $p = d_R + 1 + d_O$, the $+1$ being $\Rp$'s intercept \\
$\bxd_i$ & $\Rp$'s row with the intercept, $(1, \bx_{R,i})$; $\dXR$ stacks these rows in $\tau$-order, zero off the match \\
$\bb \in \{0,1\}^{\bar n_O}$ & selection vector: $b_t = 1$ iff $t \in \pi(I)$ \\
$B_R$, $B_O$, $B_y$ & clipping bounds: $\|\bxd\| \le B_R$ (intercept included), $\|\bx_O\| \le B_O$, $|y| \le B_y$; $r, o, w = B_R^2, B_O^2, B_y^2$ \\
$\Delta$, $\Delta_{\neg yy}$, $\Delta_G$, $\Delta_y$ & replace-one $\ell_2$ sensitivities in the slot domain: full release with and without $\by^\top\by$; $(\triu\bB, \bC)$ only; $\by^\top\by$ alone ($= w$) \\
$(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}, \delta_{\mathrm{run}})$, $f_1$, $f_y$ & per-run budget; stage-1 share; share of a separate $\by^\top\by$ release \\
$\rho_s$, $\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$ & zCDP cost of release $s$; $\rho_{\mathrm{run}} = \sum_s \rho_s$ \\
$\gamma$, $\delta_{\mathrm{DP}}$, $\zeta$ & statistical slack of a run; the part of $\delta_{\mathrm{run}}$ left to the DP mechanism; the largest share of $\delta_{\mathrm{run}}$ the slack may take \\
$(\varepsilon_L, \delta_L)$, $\zeta_L$, $\xi_L$, $\delta'$; $\rho_L$, $E'_L$, $\Gamma_L$ & ledger cap of a base register, its slack share, its share reserved for smudging, and the per-release slack of smudging; the derived zCDP, smudging and slack capacities \\
$N$, $N_s = N/2$, $q_0$ & ring dimension, slots, last-level modulus \\
$\Omega_U$, $\Omega_u$, $\Omega_V$, $\Omega_f$ & encoding scales of $\Rp$'s vectors, of the one-hot masks and of $\Op$'s data; the exact final scale $\Omega_f = \Omega_U\Omega_V\Omega_u/(q_1q_2)$ \\
$\Ecd$, $\Dcd$ & encoding $\Ecd_{\Omega}(\mathbf z) = \lfloor \Omega\,\tau_{\mathrm{can}}^{-1}(\mathbf z) \rceil$ and decoding $\Dcd(v) = \tau_{\mathrm{can}}(v)/\Omega_f$, with $\tau_{\mathrm{can}}$ the canonical embedding \\
$\sigma_{c,s}$, $\sigma_s$ & noise scale on the coefficient lattice; the induced slot-domain s.d.\ $\sigma_s = \sigma_{c,s}\sqrt{N/2}/\Omega_f$ \\
$B_{\mathrm{tot},s}$, $\delta_B$, $\kappa$ & $\ell_2$ bound on all flooded error terms of release $s$, holding with probability $\ge 1 - \delta_B$; flooding parameter (statistical distance $2^{-\kappa}(1 + 2^{-80}) + 2^{-97}$ per release), derived by \textsc{Calibrate} \\
$\vartheta$, $\vartheta^\star$ & validity ratio $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG + \lambda\Psi)/(2\sigma_2\sqrt p)$; its target \\
$\mathcal E$, $\mathcal E_G$, $\mathcal E_2$ & released entries: all; $(\triu\bB, \bC)$; stage-2 entries ($\by^\top\by$ only in joint mode) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Parameters.} The protocol, its algorithms and its theorems are stated for a parameter vector $\mathcal P$: security parameters (ring dimension, modulus chain, $\delta_B$, the floor $\kappa_{\min}$ on the flooding parameter, the security level $\lambda_{\mathrm{sec}}$), privacy parameters (the budget, the splits $f_1$ and $f_y$, the slack share $\zeta$, the ledger cap), statistical parameters, the data bounds and preprocessing, the PSI parameters, and the encoding scales $\Omega_U$ and $\Omega_u$. Section~\ref{sec:params} lists every parameter with its role, the constraint it must satisfy and the value used in our evaluation, which we call the \emph{evaluation setting}; the evaluation settings are not part of the protocol. \textsc{Calibrate} derives the flooding parameter $\kappa$, the noise, the scales and the size of $q_0$ from $\mathcal P$, or refuses the run (Section~\ref{sec:privacy:calib}). Numbers in Sections~\ref{sec:protocol}--\ref{sec:stats} and Appendix~\ref{sec:bias} are evaluation settings or worked examples and are labelled as such.

The target is a ridge fit on the matched, column-joined data $\bX^I = [\dXR^{I} \mid \bXOI]$ with
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:gram}
  \bG := \bX^{I\top}\bX^I =
  \begin{bmatrix} \bA & \bC \\ \bC^{\top} & \bB \end{bmatrix}, \qquad
  \bc := \bX^{I\top}\by^{I} =
  \begin{bmatrix} \bcR \\ \bcO \end{bmatrix},
\end{equation}
where $\bA$ is $\Rp$'s own block and $\bB$, $\bC$, $\bcR$, $\bcO$ and $\by^\top\by$ depend on $\Op$'s data. We write $\PiR$ and $\PiO$ for the orthogonal projectors onto the two coordinate blocks, so $\PiR + \PiO = \bI_p$. The two estimands, the ridge target $\btheta_\lambda = (\bG + \lambda\Psi)^{-1}\bc$ and the regression coefficient $\bbeta$, are defined in Section~\ref{sec:stats:estimands}.

% ==============================================================
\section{Threat model and guarantees}
\label{sec:defs}
% ==============================================================

Both parties are \emph{honest-but-curious}. They follow the protocol, including key generation and $\Rp$'s preparation of its inputs (clipping, the selection vector), and they try to learn what they can from their views. Following the protocol includes drawing every secret, error and coin from its specified distribution with fresh randomness. In particular, the flooding bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:btot}, and with it Theorem~\ref{thm:output}, needs $\Rp$'s encryption and key-share errors to be sampled honestly: a researcher who inflated them would push the term ``$\Rp$'s errors times $\Op$'s data'' beyond the bound, and that term is correlated with $\Op$'s data (Remark~\ref{rem:whoknows}; robustness to such a researcher is an extension, Section~\ref{sec:ext}). $\Op$'s ledger is honest and admits runs one at a time (Section~\ref{sec:privacy:ledger}). Channels are private and authenticated. $\Rp$ is the analyst and receives the output. $\Op$ receives no output, and its guarantees G1 and G2 below concern its view of the protocol only.

\begin{definition}[Datasets and adjacency]
\label{def:adj}
A dataset is $\Op$'s padded scope register $D = \{(\mathrm{id}_j, \bx_{O,j}, y_j)\}_{j \le \bar n_O}$, with the cohort $(\mathrm{id}, \bXR)$, the identifiers and $\Phi$ held fixed. $D \sim D'$ if they differ in $(\bx_O, y)$ of exactly one row, matched or not (\emph{replace-one} adjacency).
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Zero-concentrated DP]
\label{def:dp}
A randomized mechanism $\mathcal M$ is $\rho$-zCDP if the R\'enyi divergence of order $a$ between $\mathcal M(D)$ and $\mathcal M(D')$ is at most $\rho a$ for all $a > 1$ and all $D \sim D'$~\cite{bun2016concentrated}. It is $(\varepsilon, \delta)$-DP if $\Pr[\mathcal M(D) \in S] \le e^{\varepsilon}\Pr[\mathcal M(D') \in S] + \delta$ for all $D \sim D'$ and measurable $S$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[SIM-CDP and IND-CDP, after~\cite{mironov2009computational}]
\label{def:simcdp}
A protocol is \emph{$(\varepsilon, \delta)$-SIM-CDP for $\Rp$ with statistical slack $\gamma$} if there exist a mechanism $\mathcal M$ that is $(\varepsilon, \delta)$-DP with respect to $\sim$ and a probabilistic polynomial-time simulator $\mathcal S$ such that, for every $D$ and every PPT distinguisher $\mathcal A$,
\[
  \bigl|\Pr[\mathcal A(\mathrm{view}_{\Rp}(D)) = 1] - \Pr[\mathcal A(\mathcal S(\mathcal M(D))) = 1]\bigr| \le \gamma + \mathrm{negl}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sec}}).
\]
It is \emph{$(\varepsilon, \delta)$-IND-CDP} if $\Pr[\mathcal A(\mathrm{view}_{\Rp}(D)) = 1] \le e^{\varepsilon}\Pr[\mathcal A(\mathrm{view}_{\Rp}(D')) = 1] + \delta + \mathrm{negl}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sec}})$ for all $D \sim D'$ and every PPT $\mathcal A$.
\end{definition}

Mironov et al.\ state these notions for pure $\varepsilon$; the extension to $(\varepsilon, \delta)$ is the standard one, and the slack $\gamma$ makes explicit the statistical terms that are not negligible in $\lambda_{\mathrm{sec}}$ at a concrete flooding parameter.

\begin{lemma}[From simulation to indistinguishability]
\label{lem:sim2ind}
$(\varepsilon, \delta)$-SIM-CDP with slack $\gamma$ implies $(\varepsilon, \delta + (1 + e^{\varepsilon})\gamma)$-IND-CDP.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Chain $\mathrm{view}(D) \approx \mathcal S(\mathcal M(D))$ (cost $\gamma$), the DP of $\mathcal S \circ \mathcal M$ (post-processing; factor $e^{\varepsilon}$, cost $\delta$), and $\mathcal S(\mathcal M(D')) \approx \mathrm{view}(D')$ (cost $e^{\varepsilon}\gamma$).
\end{proof}

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering\small
\caption{Guarantees of v3.1 against honest-but-curious parties. Correctness assumes honest computation.}
\label{tab:guarantees}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
 & Guarantee & Basis \\
\midrule
G1 & \textbf{$\Rp$-input privacy}: $\Op$'s view of the protocol can be simulated from $(n_R^{\max}, \bar n_O)$ and the public parameters. It does not cover what $\Rp$ later discloses, which is noiseless to $\Op$. & RLWE in $s_R$; security of the PSI. $\Op$ never receives a decryption share from $\Rp$, nor $\Rp$'s decrypted value $v_s$ (Section~\ref{sec:input}). \\
G2 & \textbf{Cohort privacy}: from its view of the protocol, $\Op$ learns nothing about $\Rp$'s cohort beyond $n_R^{\max}$. & Padded OPRF queries; labeled PSI. \\
G3 & \textbf{Output privacy}: $\Rp$'s view of a run is $(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}, \delta_{\mathrm{DP}})$-SIM-CDP for $\sim$ with slack $\gamma$, hence $(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}, \delta_{\mathrm{run}})$-IND-CDP. The joint view of all researchers over all runs on a base register, for runs chosen adaptively under the filter, is $(\varepsilon_L, \delta_L)$-IND-CDP for base-register adjacency (Definition~\ref{def:baseadj}). & Theorem~\ref{thm:output}; Proposition~\ref{prop:compose} (ledger). $\Rp$ samples its errors honestly. \\
G4 & \textbf{Disclosed}: $\Rp$ learns $I$ (which cohort members are in $D_\Phi$) and uniformly random positions $\tau(j)$, and nothing else about $\Op$'s register. & By design (Remark~\ref{rem:revealed}); OPRF-keyed items and labels and \textsc{SanitizeBFV} (Section~\ref{sec:input}). \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\begin{remark}[What is revealed by design]
\label{rem:revealed}
$\Rp$ learns which of its cohort appear in $\Op$'s scope, and $n$. When membership is itself sensitive (e.g.\ a benefits register, where membership \emph{is} the outcome), this must be disclosed to data stewards. DP does not protect membership or $n$, nor $\Rp$'s own cohort in a published model, since $\bA$ is exact. A membership-private variant is sketched in Remark~\ref{rem:modeM}.
\end{remark}

% ==============================================================
\section{Building blocks}
\label{sec:blocks}
% ==============================================================

\subsection{Two-party threshold CKKS}
\label{sec:blocks:he}

CKKS~\cite{cheon2017homomorphic} encrypts vectors of $N/2$ real numbers. A ciphertext is a pair $(c_0, c_1)$ of polynomials modulo $q$, and decryption with the secret key $s$ gives
\[
  c_0 + c_1 s = \Ecd(\mathbf z) + e \pmod q,
\]
the encoded vector plus a small error. In the threshold variant the key is split additively, $s = s_R + s_O$, so decryption splits as $(c_0 + c_1 s_O) + c_1 s_R$: the first term needs $\Op$ and the second needs $\Rp$.

\paragraph{Key generation.} Following Mouchet et al.~\cite{mouchet2021multiparty}, from a common random polynomial $a$ each party draws its share and an error and publishes one polynomial,
\[
  p_{0,R} = -a s_R + e_R, \qquad p_{0,O} = -a s_O + e_O, \qquad \pk = \bigl(p_{0,R} + p_{0,O},\ a\bigr) = (-a s + e,\ a).
\]
Rotation keys for the slot shifts $2^i$ are generated the same way: $\Rp$ sends its rotation-key shares to $\Op$, which adds its own and keeps the joint rotation keys, since only $\Op$ evaluates. $\Op$ sends $\Rp$ only its public-key share $p_{0,O}$. The circuit has depth two and no ciphertext--ciphertext products, so no relinearization or conjugation key is needed.

\paragraph{Upload at half size.} $\Rp$ encrypts with its own share symmetrically, using a random $a$ expanded from a short seed: $c_0 = -a s_R + e + \Ecd(\mathbf v)$, $a = \mathrm{PRG}(\varsigma)$. It sends $(c_0, \varsigma)$. $\Op$ regenerates $a$ and sets $c_0 \gets c_0 - a s_O$, after which $c_0 + a s = \Ecd(\mathbf v) + e$: a ciphertext under the joint key.

\paragraph{What $\Op$ computes.} $\Op$ adds ciphertexts, multiplies them by its own plaintext columns, and rotates slots to sum a vector. None of this needs a key share.

\subsection{Oblivious PRF}
\label{sec:blocks:oprf}

$\Rp$ obtains $F_k(x) = H_2(x, H_1(x)^k)$ under $\Op$'s key $k$ without $\Op$ seeing $x$ and without $\Rp$ learning $k$ (2HashDH~\cite{jarecki2014round,rfc9497}). $\Rp$ sends $\alpha = H_1(x)^r$ for a random $r$; $\Op$ returns $\beta = \alpha^k$; $\Rp$ unblinds $\beta^{1/r} = H_1(x)^k$ and hashes. $\alpha$ is uniform in the group, so $\Op$ learns nothing about $x$. The output is split into an item part $F_k(x)$, used for matching, and a key part $F'_k(x)$, used to open labels. The OPRF makes every membership test an online interaction with $\Op$, which matters because identifiers such as social insurance numbers are a small, guessable space.

\subsection{Labeled PSI}
\label{sec:blocks:psi}

In labeled PSI~\cite{chen2017fast,chen2018labeled,cong2021labeled} the receiver learns which of its items are in the sender's set and, for each match, a label attached by the sender. $\Op$ hashes its items into bins, interpolates per bin a membership polynomial whose roots are the items and a label polynomial that maps each item to its encrypted label, and evaluates both on $\Rp$'s encrypted, cuckoo-hashed items. Here the label is the row's position $\tau(j)$ in $\Op$'s shuffled register. Appendix~\ref{app:psi} gives the details.

\subsection{zCDP and the discrete Gaussian}
\label{sec:blocks:dp}

A mechanism that adds Gaussian noise of s.d.\ $\sigma$ to a statistic with $\ell_2$ sensitivity $\Delta$ is exactly $\Delta^2/(2\sigma^2)$-zCDP~\cite{bun2016concentrated}. The multivariate discrete Gaussian on $\Z^N$ is exactly $\Delta^2/(2\sigma^2)$-zCDP for a statistic with values in $\Z^N$, that is, for integral shifts only~\cite{canonne2020discrete}: if two centres differed by a non-integral vector, the two outputs would lie on disjoint cosets of $\Z^N$. The release of Section~\ref{sec:privacy:mech} is centred at an integral vector, so its shifts are integral. zCDP costs add under composition. A $\rho$-zCDP mechanism is $(\varepsilon, \delta)$-DP with $\varepsilon = \rho + 2\sqrt{\rho\ln(1/\delta)}$; the tight conversion of Canonne, Kamath and Steinke~\cite{canonne2020discrete} allows a larger $\rho$ at the same $(\varepsilon, \delta)$ (about $27\%$ larger at $\varepsilon = 1$, $\delta = 10^{-9}$). Floating-point samplers leak through their low-order bits~\cite{mironov2012significance}; we use an exact integer sampler.

% ==============================================================
\section{The protocol}
\label{sec:protocol}
% ==============================================================

Figure~\ref{fig:protocol} shows the message flow and Algorithms~\ref{alg:setup}--\ref{alg:sub} give the pseudocode.

\begin{figure}[p]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.9\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figures/protocol_v31.pdf}
  \caption{Message flow of protocol v3.1. Blue arrows go from $\Rp$ to $\Op$ and orange arrows from $\Op$ to $\Rp$. $\Op$ never receives a decryption share, and $\Rp$ never receives $c_0'$.}
  \label{fig:protocol}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Phase 0: setup (Algorithm~\ref{alg:setup}).} The parties agree on the parameter vector $\mathcal P$ (Section~\ref{sec:params}): the scope, the caps, the budget, the ledger cap of the base register, the statistical constants, the PSI parameters and the encoding scales. Each party preprocesses its own columns with public constants (Section~\ref{sec:protocol:preproc}), and the clipping bounds are chosen on the preprocessed public reference data or from domain knowledge. The sensitivities follow from the bounds alone (Section~\ref{sec:privacy:sens}). $\Op$ runs \textsc{Calibrate} (Algorithm~\ref{alg:sub}), which derives the flooding parameter, the CKKS scale and the noise from $\mathcal P$ and refuses the run if $\mathcal P$ is not admissible; $\Op$ also refuses if the run's full cost would exceed its ledger's cap, and otherwise charges that cost at once (Section~\ref{sec:privacy:ledger}). $\Rp$ repeats \textsc{Calibrate}, whose outputs depend on public values only, and checks $\Op$'s. The parties run threshold key generation, and $\Op$ draws a fresh OPRF key, a fresh shuffle $\tau$, and prepares its PSI polynomials.

\textsc{ChooseBounds} (Algorithm~\ref{alg:sub}) takes the $99.5\%$ quantile of each block's norm, $\|\bxd\|$, $\|\bx_O\|$ and $|y|$, on the preprocessed public reference data; this is the evaluation setting. Choosing the quantile levels to minimize the first-order coefficient MSE plus the squared clipping bias is future work.

\paragraph{Phase 1: link (Algorithm~\ref{alg:link}).} $\Rp$ pads its identifiers to $n_R^{\max}$, obtains their OPRF values, and runs labeled PSI. It learns $I$ and the positions $\pi(i) = \tau(j)$, and computes its own block
\[
  \bA = \sum_{i \in I} \bxd_i\bxd_i^\top
\]
exactly and locally. $\bA$ is never transmitted and never noised: it is a function of $(I, \bXR)$, both known to $\Rp$, and it does not change between adjacent datasets.

\paragraph{Phase 2: upload (Algorithm~\ref{alg:upload}).} $\Rp$ writes the selection vector $\bb$ and its features $\dXR$ over all $\bar n_O$ positions, in $\tau$-order and zero off the match, encrypts them under $s_R$ with seeded randomness, and sends them in chunks of at most $N/2$ slots. $\Op$ converts each ciphertext to the joint key.

\paragraph{Phase 3: aggregate (Algorithm~\ref{alg:aggregate}).} For each released entry $\Op$ multiplies one of $\Rp$'s encrypted vectors by one of its plaintext columns and sums:
\begin{align}
  \Enc{B_{jk}} &= \textstyle\sum_{t} \Enc{b_{t}}\,X_{O,tj}X_{O,tk}, &
  \Enc{C_{jk}} &= \textstyle\sum_{t} \Enc{\dot{X}_{R,tj}}\,X_{O,tk}, \label{eq:BC}\\
  \Enc{c_{O,k}} &= \textstyle\sum_{t} \Enc{b_{t}}\,X_{O,tk}\,y_{t}, &
  \Enc{c_{R,j}} &= \textstyle\sum_{t} \Enc{\dot{X}_{R,tj}}\,y_{t}, \label{eq:cOcR}
\end{align}
and $\Enc{\by^\top\by} = \sum_t \Enc{b_t}\,y_t^2$. Unmatched positions contribute zero to the values, because $\bb$ and $\dXR$ are zero there. $\Op$ then packs the results so that each released entry sits alone in its own slot: $M_1$ holds only $\triu\bB$ and $\bC$, for stage~1, and $M_2$ holds every stage-2 entry. A separate $M_y$ holds $\by^\top\by$ when it is not free (Section~\ref{sec:privacy:sens}).

\paragraph{Phase 4: release (Algorithm~\ref{alg:release}).} For each packed ciphertext $M = (c_0, c_1)$, $\Op$ re-randomizes it under the joint public key with fresh $(u, e_0, e_1)$, computes its noisy decryption share
\[
  h_O = c_0' + c_1' s_O + \eta, \qquad \eta \sim \DG(0, \sigma_c^2)^N \text{ fresh},
\]
and sends $(c_1', h_O)$; the release was paid for when the run was admitted. It never sends $c_0'$. $\Rp$ computes $v = h_O + c_1' s_R = \Ecd(\mathbf z) + e + \eta$ and decodes. Stage~1 releases $M_1$ with s.d.\ $\sigma_1$, stage~2 releases $M_2$ with s.d.\ $\sigma_2$, and the releases use independent randomness.

\paragraph{Phase 5: solve and infer (Algorithm~\ref{alg:solve}).} From stage~1 and its exact $\bA$, $\Rp$ chooses the ridge $(\lambda, \Psi)$. After stage~2 it checks the gate $\hat\vartheta \ge 1$, solves $\tilde\btheta = (\hat\bG_2 + \lambda\Psi)^{-1}\hat\bc_2$, and computes both intervals (Section~\ref{sec:stats}). All of this is post-processing.

\input{algorithms}

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering\small
\caption{What crosses between the parties. All messages travel over a mutually authenticated channel with structured (non-executable) serialization.}
\label{tab:ledger}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
$\Rp \to \Op$ & key-generation shares ($p_{0,R}$ and rotation-key shares); $n_R^{\max}$ blinded OPRF elements; BFV encryptions of windowed powers under $\Rp$'s own BFV key; seeded uploads $(c_0, \varsigma)$ of $\bb$ and the columns of $\dXR$ \\
$\Op \to \Rp$ & public parameters: the agreed $\mathcal P$ (including $B_O, B_y, d_O, \bar n_O$) and \textsc{Calibrate}'s outputs $(\kappa, \Omega_V, \Omega_f, q_0, \sigma_{c,s})$, PSI sizes; the public-key share $p_{0,O}$ (not its rotation-key shares); OPRF responses; masked, sanitized PSI replies; per release, $(c_1', h_O)$ \\
never transmitted & $s_R$, $s_O$; $\bA$; raw $\bXR$, $\bXO$, $\by$; $\tau$; $k$; $c_0'$, $u$, $e_0$, $e_1$ and $\eta$ of every release; $\Rp$'s reconstruction $v_s = h_{O,s} + c'_{1,s}s_R$ of every release (with $h_{O,s}$ and $c'_{1,s}$ it would reveal $s_R$) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\begin{remark}[Changes relative to earlier drafts]
\label{rem:changes}
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep, label=(\roman*)]
  \item \textbf{Keys.} $\Rp$ no longer holds the CKKS secret key. The key is shared, $\Op$ adds the noise inside its decryption share, and $c_0'$ is never transmitted, which closes a leak of $\Op$'s key share.
  \item \textbf{Flooding.} The DP noise $\eta$ itself floods every error term that depends on $\Op$'s data or secrets, statistically and in $\ell_2$. The earlier separately calibrated ``error channel'' and its cost $\rho_{\mathrm{err}}$ are gone.
  \item \textbf{Two releases.} The ridge is chosen from a first release instead of from a committed public eigenvalue bound, so it adapts to the data while staying independent of the estimation noise.
  \item \textbf{Accounting.} zCDP with the tight conversion replaces the analytic Gaussian calibration of a single release.
  \item \textbf{Linkage.} An OPRF with a fresh key per run replaces a keyed hash with a key known to $\Rp$, and OPRF queries are padded and counted against a quota.
  \item \textbf{Input checks.} The homomorphic input checks of v3 are removed: they addressed only a malicious researcher, and one could be bypassed. Robustness to a deviating researcher is an extension (Section~\ref{sec:ext}).
  \item \textbf{Bias correction.} The zero-budget correction of Appendix~\ref{sec:bias} is now an option, off in our evaluation.
  \item \textbf{Preprocessing.} Each party transforms its own columns in Setup, with public constants (Section~\ref{sec:protocol:preproc}).
  \item \textbf{Parameters.} The protocol is stated for a parameter vector with explicit admissibility constraints (Section~\ref{sec:params}). The flooding parameter is derived from the budget and the ledger cap, the PSI replies are sanitized with their own flooding parameter, and the ledger has a specified composition rule, which charges a run's full cost at admission.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Per-party preprocessing}
\label{sec:protocol:preproc}

The noise is sized to the clipping bounds, so every record whose norm is well below its bound wastes part of the budget. After standardization, real features are often heavy-tailed. On the protein dataset of Section~\ref{sec:eval:e2e}, the $99.5\%$ quantile of the squared norm of $\Rp$'s block (with the intercept) is $9.5$ times its median, and that of $\Op$'s block $19$ times. Bounds at these quantiles leave typical records using a small part of the budget. Setup therefore includes a preprocessing step, \textsc{Preprocess} in Algorithm~\ref{alg:sub}, in which each party transforms its own columns:
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep, label=(\roman*)]
  \item standardize each column with public constants, from public reference data or domain knowledge;
  \item optionally, a per-column tail transform with public constants: winsorizing at $|z| \le c$, a rank-Gaussian transform from public reference quantiles, or a log transform of a skewed variable;
  \item optionally, \emph{block normalization}: each party scales its own block of every row to unit norm ($\Rp$ excludes the intercept, so $\|\bxd\|^2 = 2$; $\Op$ gets $\|\bx_O\| = 1$). A clipped log of the block norm may be appended as an extra feature to keep the magnitude.
\end{enumerate}
$\Op$ standardizes $y$, and may winsorize it, in the same way. The bounds are then chosen on the transformed reference data. For block normalization they are exact: $B_R^2 = 2$, $B_O = 1$.

\begin{proposition}[Preprocessing preserves the guarantees]
\label{prop:preproc}
Let each party's transform be a fixed function of its own record and of public constants. Then the protocol run on the transformed data, with bounds that hold on the transformed domain, has the guarantees of Table~\ref{tab:guarantees} with respect to replace-one adjacency on the raw data.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$\Op$'s transform maps one raw record to one transformed record, so changing one raw record $(\bx_O, y)$ changes exactly one transformed record, which still satisfies the bounds; $\Rp$'s records are fixed under $\sim$. The sensitivity of Section~\ref{sec:privacy:sens} therefore bounds the change, and the rest of the analysis is unchanged. The constants are data-independent, as \textsc{Calibrate}'s outputs must be. $\Rp$ may standardize with its own constants, since its data are not protected against itself, but it commits to $B_R$.
\end{proof}

The estimand is the regression on the transformed features. Winsorizing and log transforms keep the coefficients interpretable. Block normalization changes their meaning: each block contributes through its direction only, unless the log-norm feature is appended. It is therefore the choice for prediction rather than for interpreting coefficients.

\paragraph{Why not joint row normalization.} Wang's preprocessing for AdaSSP normalizes each whole row $(\bx_R, \bx_O)$, which needs both parties' columns. We considered two ways to do this here.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{Exact joint clipping} computes the scale factor $\min\{1, B/\|\bx\|\}$ under encryption from $\Rp$'s encrypted row norms and $\Op$'s plaintext ones. This needs a high-degree polynomial approximation and ciphertext--ciphertext products. Worse, scaling $\Rp$'s part by a factor that depends on $\Op$'s data makes $\bA$ depend on $\Op$'s data, so $\bA$ could no longer be exact.
  \item \emph{Clipping only $\Op$'s part to the remaining budget} $B^2 - \|\bxd\|^2$ keeps $\bA$ exact and the circuit linear. $\Rp$ assigns each matched record to one of $K$ norm classes and uploads its selection vector and features split by class, still encrypted, and $\Op$ pre-clips its columns once per class in plaintext, at about $K$ times the upload and aggregation cost. On protein it does not pay. A joint budget alone raises $\Delta$ from $87.3$ to $102$, because the sensitivity is dominated by $\Op$'s own norm, which enters twice, and records with a small $\Rp$-norm may then use more of it. Keeping $\Op$'s separate cap as well lowers $\Delta$ only to $82$, about $12\%$ fewer records.
\end{itemize}
The gap is caused by heavy tails under loose bounds, not by the lack of a joint bound, and per-party transforms close most of it. In end-to-end runs on protein, block normalization brings private test error within $2$--$6\%$ of non-private, against $12$--$21\%$ with standardization alone (Table~\ref{tab:e2e_protein}).

Block normalization is the evaluation's recommended per-party preprocessing (Sections~\ref{sec:eval:e2e} and~\ref{sec:eval:adassp}). Its coefficients describe the regression on the block directions, as noted above. The appended log-norm feature and winsorizing $y$ were not evaluated.

\paragraph{Optional feature screening.} Setup may include a screening module, which runs before \textsc{Preprocess} (Algorithm~\ref{alg:setup}). On public reference data that carries both parties' schemas, the parties look for features that are nearly collinear, within a block or across the two blocks, and drop one of each such pair. A collinearity across the blocks is invisible to each party in its own data, and it keeps $\lambda_{\min}$ of the joint design small, which delays the switch-off of the ridge (Section~\ref{sec:stats:noff}). The module uses only public reference data, so it costs no budget; it needs reference data that carries both schemas, and it helps only as far as that data represents the matched records. On protein, $\Rp$'s feature $x_{R,1}$ is nearly collinear with $\Op$'s feature $x_{O,5}$ (correlation $0.997$ after standardization). Dropping $x_{R,1}$ lowers $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ from $691$k to $102$k at $\varepsilon = 1$, with block normalization at the previous setting and $\delta = 10^{-9}$ (Table~\ref{tab:noff_drop}).

\clearpage  % place the algorithm pages before Section 7

% ==============================================================
\section{Output privacy}
\label{sec:privacy}
% ==============================================================

\subsection{Sensitivity under replace-one adjacency}
\label{sec:privacy:sens}

Stage~2 releases the stacked statistic
\begin{equation}
  \mathbf z_2(D) = \bigl(\triu(\bB),\; \bC,\; \bcR,\; \bcO,\; \by^{\top}\by\bigr),
  \label{eq:release}
\end{equation}
without $\by^\top\by$ in separate mode, and stage~1 releases $\mathbf z_1(D) = (\triu\bB, \bC)$. An unmatched row contributes nothing to these values. Replacing one matched row's $(\bx, y)$ by $(\bx', y')$, with $\Rp$'s row $\bxd$ fixed, changes $\mathbf z_2$ by
\[
  \bigl(\triu(\bx\bx^{\top} - \bx'\bx'^{\top}),\;
  \bxd(\bx - \bx')^{\top},\;
  \bxd(y - y'),\;
  \bx y - \bx'y',\;
  y^{2} - y'^{2}\bigr).
\]
Every entry of a release gets noise of the same s.d., so the relevant sensitivity is the $\ell_2$ norm of this change, maximized over the bounds.

\begin{proposition}[Replace-one sensitivity]
\label{prop:sens}
With $r = B_R^2$, $o = B_O^2$, $w = B_y^2$ and $\Delta_{\mathrm{add}}^2 = (o + r)(o + w) + w^2$ the add/remove bound, $\Delta_{\mathrm{add}} \le \Delta \le 2\Delta_{\mathrm{add}}$, and
\begin{equation}
  \Delta^{2} =
  \begin{cases}
    \tfrac12(2o + r + w)^{2} + 2rw & \text{if } |r - w| \le 2o,\\[2pt]
    4r(o + w) & \text{if } r - w \ge 2o,\\[2pt]
    \max_{t \in [-B_{y}, B_{y}]} F\bigl(c^{\ast}(t), t\bigr) & \text{if } w - r > 2o,
  \end{cases}
  \label{eq:sens_closed}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align*}
  F(c,t) &= 2o^{2}(1 - c^{2}) + 2ro(1 - c) + r(B_{y} - t)^{2} + o(w + t^{2}) - 2ocB_{y}t + (w - t^{2})^{2},\\
  c^{\ast}(t) &= \min\bigl\{1, \max\{-1, -(r + B_{y}t)/(2o)\}\bigr\}.
\end{align*}
For stage~1, $\Delta_G = (2o + r)/\sqrt2$ if $r \le 2o$ and $2\sqrt{ro}$ otherwise. These are exact suprema for $d_O \ge 2$ and valid, conservative bounds for $d_O = 1$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
The lower bound replaces $(\bx, y)$ with $(\mathbf 0, 0)$, the upper bound passes through that dataset with the triangle inequality. At the optimum $\|\bxd\| = B_R$ and $\|\bx\| = \|\bx'\| = B_O$; the remaining dependence is on $c = \cos\angle(\bx, \bx')$ and $t = y'$, and maximizing the concave quadratic in $c$ gives $c^\ast(t)$. In regime~1 the worst case is $y' = -y$ with $c = -(r - w)/(2o)$ and a diagonal change in $\bB$; in regime~2 it is $(\bx, y) \mapsto (-\bx, -y)$. Regime~3 reduces to the roots of three cubics. The full proof is in the companion proofs document (Part~B, Theorem~S1).
\end{proof}

\textsc{DeltaRep} (Algorithm~\ref{alg:sub}) computes regime~3 by exact enumeration: it evaluates $F(c^\ast(t), t)$ at $t = \pm B_y$, at the boundaries of the pieces of $c^\ast$, and at the real roots of the three cubics $\partial_t F(c^\ast(t), t) = 0$, one per piece, and takes the largest value. It rounds the result up by a relative $10^{-12}$ only as a safeguard against floating-point error. The specification's one-dimensional maximization is replaced. The implementation's enumeration agrees with a direct multistart maximization over the vectors ($d_O = 2$) to a relative $10^{-12}$ in regime~3, for example $\Delta = 26.07$ at $(B_R, B_O, B_y) = (1, 1, 5)$.

\begin{remark}[When $\by^\top\by$ is free]
\label{rem:yyfree}
Releasing $\by^\top\by$ costs nothing exactly when $\Delta = \Delta_{\neg yy}$. This holds whenever $w \le 2(r + o)$ and never when $w > 3(r + o)$; in between it is decided numerically. It is false in general: for $(B_R, B_O, B_y) = (1, 1, 5)$, $\Delta = 26.07$ with $\by^\top\by$ against $14.14$ without. Algorithm~\ref{alg:setup} therefore releases $\by^\top\by$ in stage~2 only when it is free, and otherwise in its own release with sensitivity $\Delta_y = w$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Upper triangle or full matrix]
\label{rem:triu}
For $d_O \ge 2$ the supremum is the same whether the change in $\bB$ is measured on its upper triangle or by the full Frobenius norm: the $\triu$ maximum is attained only when the change is diagonal. The protocol releases the upper triangle only, which keeps the release and the traffic smaller. Releasing both triangles with independent noise would cost the same worst-case $\rho_2$ and halve the variance of the averaged off-diagonal entries, at $d_O(d_O - 1)/2$ more released entries per release.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The release mechanism}
\label{sec:privacy:mech}

For release $s$, let $\mathbf z_s(D) \in \R^{N_s}$ hold the released entries in their slots and zeros elsewhere.

\begin{lemma}[Sensitivity of the encoded statistic]
\label{lem:enc}
$\|\tau_{\mathrm{can}}^{-1}(\mathbf z)\|_2 = \sqrt{2/N}\,\|\mathbf z\|_2$. Hence $\sup_{D \sim D'} \|\Ecd_{\Omega}(\mathbf z_s(D)) - \Ecd_{\Omega}(\mathbf z_s(D'))\|_2 \le \sqrt{2/N}\,\Omega\,\Delta_s + \sqrt N$, where $\sqrt N$ bounds the difference of the two coordinate-wise roundings.
\end{lemma}

\paragraph{The centre.} The decrypted value differs from $\Ecd_{\Omega_f}(\mathbf z_s(D))$ also by deterministic, data-dependent terms, chiefly the spill of the one-hot masks. These terms are part of the query, not hints about secrets, so the mechanism is centred at the noiseless output of the circuit,
\[
  \hat{\mathbf z}_s(D) = \sum_{e \in \mathcal E_s}\frac{1}{q_1q_2}\,\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl(\sum_{h \le m} m_{U_e,h}\,m_{V_e,h}\Bigr)m_{u_e}, \qquad
  f_s(D) = \lfloor \hat{\mathbf z}_s(D) \rceil \in \Z^N,
\]
rounded to the coefficient lattice so that the shift $f_s(D) - f_s(D')$ between adjacent datasets is integral, as the discrete Gaussian needs (Section~\ref{sec:blocks:dp}), where $m_{U_e,h}$, $m_{V_e,h}$ and $m_{u_e}$ are the encodings of $\Rp$'s vectors at $\Omega_U$, of $\Op$'s vectors at $\Omega_V$ and of the mask at $\Omega_u$, and $\mathrm{Tr}$ is the trace that \textsc{EvalSum} computes. $f_s(D)$ depends on the data and the public parameters only, not on any key, error or coin. Its sensitivity is, for any encoding scales and for neighbours that differ in a matched or an unmatched row (proofs, Part~A, Lemma~4.3; Corollary~3.13 there is the equal-scale case)
\begin{equation}
  \sup_{D \sim D'}\|f_s(D) - f_s(D')\|_2 \le \widehat\Delta_{\mathrm{enc},s} := \sqrt{2/N}\,\Omega_f\Delta_s + \Delta_{x,s} + \sqrt N,
  \label{eq:denc}
\end{equation}
where $\Delta_{x,s}$, an explicit function of $N$, the encoding scales, $q_1$, $q_2$, $|\mathcal E_s|$, the bounds and $\Delta_s$, bounds the change of the rounding and spill terms. $\Omega_f$ is the exact final scale, tracked from the primes and the encoding scales and never a nominal power of two, and $\Rp$ decodes with it. At the evaluation settings $\Delta_{x,2} \approx 2^{27.6}$ against $\sqrt{2/N}\,\Omega_f\Delta_2 \approx 2^{62.7}$, so centring changes $\rho_s$ by a relative $2^{-34}$. Flooding the mask spill instead would need $\log_2\Omega_f$ of about $67$--$71$ rather than $61.5$ at the proofs' example ($\bar n_O = 10^6$, $\varepsilon = 1$).

\paragraph{Error decomposition.} The value $\Rp$ reconstructs in a release is
\[
  v_s = h_O + c_1' s_R = c_0' + c_1' s + \eta_s = f_s(D) + e_{\mathrm{tot},s} + \eta_s \pmod{q_0}.
\]
$e_{\mathrm{tot},s} \in \Z^N$ collects every term that depends on a key, an error or a coin: $\Rp$'s encryption errors multiplied by $\Op$'s data, the rescaling and ModDown roundings, the key-switching error (which involves the errors of $\Op$'s key shares), the mask spill of these errors, the rounding of the centre, and the fresh-encryption error $e\,u + e_0 + e_1 s$ of the re-randomization.

\begin{lemma}[Smudging]
\label{lem:smudge}
For $e \in \Z^N$ and $\eta \sim \mathcal D_{\Z^N, \sigma_c}$, $\mathrm{SD}(\eta + e, \eta) \le \|e\|_2/(2\sigma_c)$, and, for $\sigma_c$ above the smoothing parameter, $\mathrm{SD}(\eta + e, \eta) \le \|e\|_2/(\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma_c) + \mathrm{negl}$. If $\sigma_c \ge \max\{2^{\kappa}B_{\mathrm{tot}}/\sqrt{2\pi},\ 2^{42}\}$ and $\|e\|_2 \le B_{\mathrm{tot}}$, the distance is at most $2^{-\kappa}(1 + 2^{-80}) + 2^{-97}$, also given any side information independent of $\eta$.
\end{lemma}

The floor $2^{42}$ is what the factor $1 + 2^{-80}$ needs, which holds for $\sigma_c \ge 2.1 \cdot 2^{40}$; earlier drafts stated $2^{20}$, at which the proof gives only $1 + 2^{-37.9}$ (proofs, composition appendix, Remark~2.6). The floor never binds at an admissible evaluation setting, where $2^{\kappa}B_{\mathrm{tot}}/\sqrt{2\pi} \ge 2^{55}$.

\begin{theorem}[Worst-case error bound]
\label{thm:btot}
For the circuit of Algorithm~\ref{alg:aggregate} there is an explicit bound $B_{\mathrm{tot},s}$, a function of public parameters only ($N$, $\bar n_O$, $d_R$, $d_O$, $B_R$, $B_O$, $B_y$, the primes and the key-switching gadget, $\Omega_U$, $\Omega_V$, $\Omega_u$, $\delta_B$ and the error distribution), such that $\Pr[\|e_{\mathrm{tot},s}\|_2 \le B_{\mathrm{tot},s}] \ge 1 - \delta_B$ for every dataset in the clipped domain, the probability being over keys and fresh randomness only (proofs, Part~A, Theorem~O1). The bound assumes, as model (M5) of the proofs, that every secret and error is drawn independently and honestly from its specified distribution, the errors from a mean-zero $\sigma_e^2$-subgaussian distribution with $\sigma_e = 3.2$.
\end{theorem}

At the proofs' example ($N = 2^{14}$, $\bar n_O = 10^6$, the bounds of the simulation population, all scales and both rescaling primes $2^{52.5}$, centred key switching, $\delta_B = 2^{-50}$), $B_{\mathrm{tot},2} \approx 2^{25.3}$. The error measured in OpenFHE runs at that setting stays $6$--$8$ bits below it.

With OpenFHE's key switching and the evaluation settings of Section~\ref{sec:params}, $B_{\mathrm{tot},2} \approx 2^{26.6}$. Two properties of OpenFHE~1.5.1, read from its source, change the proofs' centred constants:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item the fast base conversion in ModUp returns digits in $[0, k_j\tilde Q_j)$, so the digit factor becomes $\varsigma_\uparrow = 2k_j$;
  \item ModDown's rounding lies in $(-k_P, 0]$, so the rounding factor becomes $\varsigma_\downarrow = 2k_P$.
\end{itemize}
Here $k_j$ is the number of primes in digit $j$ and $k_P$ the number of special primes. The dominant terms are the key-switching gadget term ($2^{25.3}$) and the ModDown roundings ($2^{25.7}$). These two properties, and OpenFHE's order of operations (it switches the key before it applies the automorphism), define an abstract model of its key switching, for which the bound is proved, with every other term unchanged (proofs, Part~A, Lemma~4.1). That OpenFHE~1.5.1 realizes the model was checked by reading its source again at tag v1.5.1 (\texttt{ApproxSwitchCRTBasis}, \texttt{ApproxModDown}, the hybrid key switch and \texttt{EvalAutomorphism}); this is a statement about a program and is not proved.

Every term of $B_{\mathrm{tot}}$ is an analytic bound; none is an estimate or a measurement. The implementation draws its errors with OpenFHE's inversion-table sampler at $\sigma = 3.19$, whose support is $|x| \le 27$. From the sampler's table we checked numerically that it is subgaussian with variance proxy $3.19^2 \le 3.2^2$, as (M5) requires; this is a check, not a proof. Neither party uses OpenFHE's noise estimation, its \texttt{NOISE\_FLOODING\_DECRYPT} mode or its multiparty decryption. $\Op$'s share is computed directly on the polynomials, and $\eta$ is the only noise added.

As a sanity check, not part of the guarantee, we measured the whole error $e_{\mathrm{tot},s}$ of releases of the implementation against the exactly computed centre $f_s(D)$. Over $12$ runs ($24$ releases) at $\bar n_O \in \{4096, 16384\}$, on the synthetic population and on data at the clipping bounds, its $\ell_2$ norm was $2^{17.8}$--$2^{18.8}$, between $7.8$ and $8.7$ bits below $B_{\mathrm{tot},s}$. The re-randomization term contributed almost nothing, and data at the clipping bounds did not increase the error. The deterministic part that the centring removes, $\|f_s(D) - \Ecd_{\Omega_f}(\mathbf z_s(D))\|_2$, was $2^{21.3}$--$2^{29.6}$; on the extreme data it exceeds $B_{\mathrm{tot},s}$, which is why the mechanism must be centred.

\tocheck{(T4) Model (M5) assumes mean-zero subgaussian errors. OpenFHE's floating-point inversion-table sampler was checked to be $3.2^2$-subgaussian only numerically. The check is tight: its table gives the variance proxy $10.1761 = 3.19^2$, perturbing every probability by $+2^{-53}$, the granularity of its uniform generator, gives $10.2392 \le 10.24 = 3.2^2$, and a perturbation of $2^{-52}$ would fail it ($10.32$). Its mean is zero only to about $2^{-53}$, which changes the bound by a relative term below $2^{-38}$ at the evaluation sizes. Add the mean term and the numerical check to the proofs, or draw the errors with an exact sampler.}

\textsc{Calibrate} must use this worst-case, data-independent bound. A bound estimated from the actual ciphertexts would depend on the data, leak through $\sigma_{c,s}$, and not be a worst-case bound. Recent attacks make the point concrete. Guo, Nabokov, Suvanto and Johansson~\cite{guo2024key} recover the secret key from a single shared decryption in OpenFHE when the flooding noise is set from non-worst-case noise estimates. Checri, Sirdey, Boudguiga and Bultel~\cite{checri2024practical} show that threshold BFV, BGV and CKKS are exposed to key recovery unless smudging noise is added after partial decryption. In v3.1 the only partial decryption that leaves its owner is $\Op$'s; it carries $\eta$, calibrated to the worst-case bound, and $\Rp$'s is never sent. Kluczniak and Santato~\cite{kluczniak2025circuit} show that Gaussian noise exponential in the security parameter, added to the partial decryptions of threshold and multikey approximate HE, gives indistinguishability-based security, and that this noise level is tight. Our release uses noise of the same kind. It differs in two ways: the same noise is the zCDP noise of the released statistic, and we prove a simulation-based guarantee for $\Rp$'s whole view, with an explicit statistical slack at a concrete $\kappa$.

\tocheck{(T2) Kluczniak and Santato (CiC 2(1), 2025): the description (indistinguishability-based circuit privacy, a KL-divergence analysis, noise exponential in the security parameter, tightness, partial decryptions of multikey and threshold schemes) comes from search-engine extracts. Check the theorem statements and the exact noise condition, and whether their tightness result says anything about a statistical flooding parameter $\kappa = 40$ accounted for as an explicit slack.}

\tocheck{(T3) Guo et al.\ (USENIX Security 2024) and Checri et al.\ (CRYPTO 2024): the claims are from abstracts and search-engine extracts. Check which OpenFHE modes they attacked. In OpenFHE 1.5.1, multiparty decryption adds a Gaussian with $\sigma = 2^{20}$ by default, estimate-based flooding under \texttt{NOISE\_FLOODING\_DECRYPT}, and uniform noise under \texttt{NOISE\_FLOODING\_MULTIPARTY}. The given names of Checri et al.\ and the page numbers of Guo et al.\ ($7447$--$7461$, from an extract) are not verified and are not in the bibliography.}

\begin{theorem}[Output privacy against an honest-but-curious $\Rp$]
\label{thm:output}
Let the parameter vector satisfy the constraints of Section~\ref{sec:params}, and let \textup{\textsc{Calibrate}}'s outputs, which depend on public values only, satisfy for every release $s$
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep, label=(\roman*)]
  \item $\sigma_{c,s} \ge \max\{2^{\kappa}B_{\mathrm{tot},s}/\sqrt{2\pi},\ 2^{42}\}$, with $B_{\mathrm{tot},s}$ from Theorem~\ref{thm:btot};
  \item $\rho_s = \widehat\Delta_{\mathrm{enc},s}^2/(2\sigma_{c,s}^2)$ and $\sum_s\rho_s \le \rho_{\mathrm{run}}$, where $\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$ is the largest $\rho$ whose tight conversion gives $(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}, \delta_{\mathrm{DP}})$, with
  \[
    \gamma = n_{\mathrm{rel}}\bigl(\delta_B + 2^{-\kappa}(1 + 2^{-80}) + 2^{-97}\bigr), \qquad \delta_{\mathrm{DP}} = \delta_{\mathrm{run}} - (1 + e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}})\gamma > 0 .
  \]
\end{enumerate}
Assume RLWE for the re-randomizer (one sample per release, modulus $q_0$) and for $\Op$'s public-key share $p_{0,O}$ (one sample; $\Op$'s rotation-key shares are never sent), at ring dimension $N$ and modulus $QP$ with $\log_2(QP) \le L_{\max}(N, \lambda_{\mathrm{sec}})$, and that $\Rp$ samples its errors honestly (Section~\ref{sec:defs}). Then $\Rp$'s view of one run is $(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}, \delta_{\mathrm{DP}})$-SIM-CDP for $\sim$ with statistical slack $\gamma$, and therefore $(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}, \delta_{\mathrm{run}})$-IND-CDP. The ideal mechanism $\mathcal M(D) = (f_s(D) + \eta_s)_s$ is a composition of lattice discrete Gaussian mechanisms and is $\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$-zCDP. PSI security is not needed for this guarantee: the identifiers are fixed under $\sim$, so the PSI transcript does not depend on $(\bx_O, y)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
A sequence of hybrids. H0 is the real view: setup messages, PSI messages, and $\{(c'_{1,s}, h_{O,s})\}_s$. H1 rewrites $h_{O,s} = v_s - c'_{1,s}s_R$, which is identical. H2 replaces $v_s$ by $f_s(D) + \eta_s$; by Lemma~\ref{lem:smudge} with side information and Theorem~\ref{thm:btot} this costs at most $\delta_B + 2^{-\kappa}(1 + 2^{-80}) + 2^{-97}$ per release, $\gamma$ in total. The side information includes $\Rp$'s random tape, whose errors and key share enter $e_{\mathrm{tot},s}$; Theorem~\ref{thm:btot} needs those errors to be honestly sampled; and $\gamma$ is a statistical slack, not negligible in $\lambda_{\mathrm{sec}}$. After H2, $(u_s, e_{1,s})$ appear only through $c'_{1,s} = c_{1,s} + a u_s + e_{1,s}$, because $c'_{0,s}$ is never sent. H3 replaces $c'_{1,s}$ by a uniform element (RLWE with secret $u_s$; fresh $u_s$ per release). H4 simulates the setup messages by plain RLWE for $\Op$'s public-key share, the only key material $\Op$ sends. The simulator, given $\mathcal M(D)$ and $s_R$, samples a uniform $c_1^\ast$ per release and outputs $h^\ast = \mathcal M(D)_s - c_1^\ast s_R$. The shifts $f_s(D) - f_s(D')$ are integral, so the multivariate discrete Gaussian with sensitivity $\widehat\Delta_{\mathrm{enc},s}$ is exactly $\rho_s$-zCDP~\cite{canonne2020discrete}; costs add, and the tight conversion gives $(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}, \delta_{\mathrm{DP}})$-DP. Lemma~\ref{lem:sim2ind} turns the slack into $\delta_{\mathrm{run}} = \delta_{\mathrm{DP}} + (1 + e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}})\gamma$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why the release format matters]
\label{rem:format}
If $\Op$ sent $c_0'$, or a share $c_1' s_O$ without $\eta$, $\Rp$ would learn $c_1' s_O$ exactly and could recover $s_O$, since $c_1'$ is invertible with overwhelming probability. Re-randomization matters too. The evaluated $c_1$ is computed from public values and $\Op$'s plaintext columns, and $\Rp$ chose the seed of $a$, so without it $c_1$ could reveal $\Op$'s data. In a real OpenFHE run of an earlier specification that added the noise as a plaintext scalar, without re-randomizing, $\Rp$ identified the true dataset in 20 of 20 trials, for a matched and for an unmatched record, where an $\varepsilon = 1$ guarantee caps the success rate at $e/(1 + e) \approx 73\%$. Reusing $\eta$ or $u$ across releases would reveal $v_2 - v_1$ exactly, so both are fresh every time. Stage~1 uses its own packed ciphertext $M_1$, which depends only on $\triu\bB$ and $\bC$, so its error cannot depend on entries released only in stage~2.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Who knows the noise]
\label{rem:whoknows}
Hwang et al.~\cite{hwang2025insecurity} show that two-party CKKS protocols in which each party adds its own smudging noise to a shared output are not secure in the standard simulation sense when both parties have inputs. The party that sampled a share of the noise knows it, so its view yields a closer approximation of the exact result than the ideal output reveals. Such protocols meet only a relaxed (``liberal'') notion, in which the simulator also receives the exact result. Their remedy is to sample the noise jointly, so that no party knows it.

v3.1 avoids this obstruction for the analyst, at the price of an explicit statistical slack. $\Rp$ adds no noise: its decryption share is never sent, so it needs no flooding of its own, and $\eta$ is the only noise in the output. $\Rp$'s coins still enter $e_{\mathrm{tot},s}$, through its encryption errors (multiplied by $\Op$'s data), its key-share errors and $s_R$. All of this is bounded by $B_{\mathrm{tot},s} \le 2^{-\kappa}\sqrt{2\pi}\,\sigma_{c,s}$ and is absorbed by Lemma~\ref{lem:smudge}, which allows any side information independent of $\eta$, including $\Rp$'s random tape. The simulator of Theorem~\ref{thm:output} therefore needs only $\mathcal M(D)$, never the exact $f_s(D)$.
