It is also output-consistent, so $\Rp$'s view jointly with its output is simulated in the standard sense, not only the liberal one, when the ideal output is the centred residue of $f_s(D) + \eta_s$ modulo $q_0$ (proofs, Part~A, Corollary~9.3). The price is the slack $\gamma$, which is statistical rather than negligible in $\lambda_{\mathrm{sec}}$. The deterministic CKKS terms are part of the functionality $f_s(D) + \eta$, not of its noise. Their effect on privacy is accounted for by $\Delta_{x,s}$, so the ideal output is $f_s(D) + \eta$ rather than $\Ecd(\mathbf z_s(D)) + \eta$.

Two qualifications follow.
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep, label=(\roman*)]
  \item The flooding argument needs $\Rp$'s errors to be sampled honestly. A researcher who inflated its encryption errors would push the term ``$\Rp$'s errors times $\Op$'s data'' beyond $B_{\mathrm{tot}}$, and that term is correlated with $\Op$'s data. This lies outside the honest-but-curious model (Section~\ref{sec:ext}).
  \item The obstruction applies unchanged to $\Op$, which knows $\eta$. G1 and G2 are statements about $\Op$'s view of the protocol, in which $\Op$ has no output, and they do not extend to $\Rp$'s outputs (Section~\ref{sec:input}).
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\tocheck{(T1) Hwang et al.\ (ePrint 2025/395; the later version's title, used in the bibliography, is ``On the (In)security of Approximate Computation Protocols from CKKS'', listed as accepted at ASIACRYPT 2026): the definitions and the two-party obstruction were checked against the authors' FHE.org 2026 poster only, and the abstract, the later title and the venue only through search-engine extracts. Confirm against the full text the exact standard and liberal definitions; that the obstruction needs the corrupted party's noise share to be non-negligible next to the rest of the output noise; and the statement for the case of one party with input. Fix the venue in the bibliography once confirmed.}

\paragraph{Release timing.} The exact sampler is not constant time, and $\Rp$ observes when each release arrives. $\Op$ therefore holds every release until a deadline $D$ after it takes up the request, and sends it only then. $D$ is fixed before the first release, from public parameters and the machine alone. In the implementation it is twice the worst of three timed exact samplings of $N$ values at the run's largest $\sigma_c^2$, drawn from an independent generator and discarded, plus one re-randomized partial decryption. A release whose computation overruns $D$ is refused, never sent late, and the run's cost stays charged. Outside an overrun, $\Rp$'s observed latency is $D$ plus scheduling jitter, which does not depend on $\eta$, so the timing channel adds at most the overrun probability to the statistical distance of the simulation of Theorem~\ref{thm:output}. That probability is measured, not bounded. At the largest $\sigma_c$ of the $10^6$-row runs ($2^{68.5}$), $D = 0.49$\,s; on the idle machine $1{,}000$ releases took at most $0.68\,D$, with no overrun (overrun probability at most $0.3\%$ at $95\%$ confidence), but with four CPU-bound processes competing for the four cores $4$ of $200$ overran. The flooding samples of \textsc{SanitizeBFV} in the PSI replies are not padded.

\tocheck{(T8) Release timing: the padding makes R's observed release latency independent of eta except when a release overruns its deadline. The overrun probability is only measured (0 of 1,000 releases on the idle machine, 4 of 200 under CPU contention), not bounded, and it is not charged to delta. Bound it, for example by a tail bound on the sampler's running time on an isolated core, or charge it to the slack; and pad the flooding of the PSI replies as well.}

\subsection{Calibration}
\label{sec:privacy:calib}

\textsc{Calibrate} (Algorithm~\ref{alg:sub}) derives every quantity the release needs from the parameter vector, using public values only, in five steps.
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep, label=(\arabic*)]
  \item \emph{Mode and sensitivities.} $\Delta$, $\Delta_{\neg yy}$, $\Delta_G$ and $\Delta_y$ from the bounds (Proposition~\ref{prop:sens}). $\by^\top\by$ joins stage~2 iff it is free (Remark~\ref{rem:yyfree}), so the number of releases is $n_{\mathrm{rel}} = 2$ (joint mode) or $3$ (separate mode).
  \item \emph{Flooding parameter and slack.} $\kappa$ from~\eqref{eq:kappa} below, unless the parameters fix it; $\gamma = n_{\mathrm{rel}}(\delta_B + 2^{-\kappa}(1 + 2^{-80}) + 2^{-97})$ and $\delta_{\mathrm{DP}} = \delta_{\mathrm{run}} - (1 + e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}})\gamma$.
  \item \emph{Budget.} $\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$, the largest $\rho$ whose tight conversion gives $(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}, \delta_{\mathrm{DP}})$, split as $\rho_1 = f_1\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$, $\rho_y = f_y\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$ in separate mode, and $\rho_2$ the rest.
  \item \emph{Scale and noise.} The smallest admissible final scale $\Omega_f$ (that is, the smallest $\Omega_V$, given $\Omega_U$ and $\Omega_u$) at which $\sigma_{c,s} \ge \max\{2^{\kappa}B_{\mathrm{tot},s}(\Omega_f)/\sqrt{2\pi},\ 2^{42}\}$ for every release, with $\sigma_{c,s}^2 = \lceil \widehat\Delta_{\mathrm{enc},s}^2/(2\rho_s) \rceil$ an integer, as the exact sampler needs. Rounding up only lowers the cost below $\rho_s$.
  \item \emph{Modulus and security.} $q_0 > 2(\Omega_f S_{\max} + 2^{\kappa}B_{\mathrm{tot},\max} + t_\eta\,\sigma_{c,\max})$ with $S_{\max} = \bar n_O\max(r, o, w, \sqrt{ro}, \sqrt{rw}, \sqrt{ow})$, so that honest inputs never wrap around, and $\log_2(QP) \le L_{\max}(N, \lambda_{\mathrm{sec}})$, the largest modulus at which RLWE with ring dimension $N$ and ternary secrets has security $\lambda_{\mathrm{sec}}$~\cite{albrecht2018he}.
\end{enumerate}
\textsc{Calibrate} refuses the run when a step has no solution: no admissible $\kappa$, $\delta_{\mathrm{DP}} \le 0$, no scale that floods, $q_0$ too small, or a chain above the security bound. When it does not refuse, its outputs are functions of public values only and satisfy hypotheses (i) and (ii) of Theorem~\ref{thm:output}, the no-wraparound condition of Section~\ref{sec:input} and, under a ledger cap, the per-run shares of Section~\ref{sec:privacy:ledger} (proofs, Part~A, Proposition~9.1).

\paragraph{The noise.} The induced slot-domain noise is, up to a relative term $(\Delta_{x,s} + \sqrt N)\sqrt{N/2}/(\Omega_f\Delta_s)$ that is below $10^{-10}$ at the evaluation settings,
\begin{equation}
  \sigma_1 = \frac{\Delta_G}{\sqrt{2 f_1\rho_{\mathrm{run}}}}, \qquad
  \sigma_2 = \frac{\Delta}{\sqrt{2(1 - f_1)\rho_{\mathrm{run}}}}, \qquad
  \sqrt{\rho_{\mathrm{run}}} \gtrsim \sqrt{L + \varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}} - \sqrt L, \quad L = \ln(1/\delta_{\mathrm{DP}}),
  \label{eq:sigmas}
\end{equation}
in joint mode ($\Delta_{\neg yy}$ and $1 - f_1 - f_y$ replace $\Delta$ and $1 - f_1$ in separate mode). The last inequality, from the simple conversion, gives a planning formula; over $\varepsilon \in [0.1, 8]$ and $\delta \in [10^{-9}, 10^{-6}]$ it is conservative by $6.1$--$33.4\%$ in $\sigma_2$ (Section~\ref{sec:eval:calib}).

\paragraph{The flooding parameter.} The same $\eta$ is the DP noise and floods $e_{\mathrm{tot},s}$, and the flooding enters the guarantee only through the slack $\gamma$, which Lemma~\ref{lem:sim2ind} converts with the factor $1 + e^{\varepsilon}$. The flooding parameter is therefore a function of the budget, not a constant. Unless the parameters fix it, \textsc{Calibrate} sets
\begin{equation}
  \kappa = \max\{\kappa_{\min},\ \kappa_{\mathrm{run}},\ \kappa_L\},
  \label{eq:kappa}
\end{equation}
where $\kappa_{\mathrm{run}}$ is the smallest integer with $(1 + e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}})\gamma \le \zeta\,\delta_{\mathrm{run}}$, and $\kappa_L$ is the smallest integer with $n_{\mathrm{rel}}\varepsilon'(\kappa) \le (\tilde\rho_{\mathrm{run}}/\rho_L)\,E'_L$, this run's share of the ledger's smudging budget, with $\varepsilon'(\kappa)$ the smudging cost of one release (Section~\ref{sec:privacy:ledger}, Remark~\ref{rem:sharperslack}); $\tilde\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$ is the budget at $\delta_{\mathrm{DP}} = (1 - \zeta)\delta_{\mathrm{run}}$, a lower bound on $\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$ once $\kappa \ge \kappa_{\mathrm{run}}$. The three terms have separate jobs.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item $\kappa_{\mathrm{run}}$ makes the per-run statement hold with the slack taking at most a share $\zeta$ of $\delta_{\mathrm{run}}$. $\gamma$ falls with $\kappa$ to the floor $n_{\mathrm{rel}}(\delta_B + 2^{-97})$, so $\kappa_{\mathrm{run}}$ exists iff $n_{\mathrm{rel}}(1 + e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}})(\delta_B + 2^{-97}) < \zeta\delta_{\mathrm{run}}$; otherwise $\delta_B$ must be lowered.
  \item $\kappa_L$ makes the ledger run out of zCDP budget before it runs out of smudging budget, whatever the order and the sizes of the runs, provided every run on the register uses $\kappa \ge \kappa_L$. Since the smudging step is accounted in hockey-stick divergence, its $2^{-\kappa}$ part is not multiplied by $e^{\varepsilon_L}$, and $\kappa_L$ is far below $\kappa_{\min}$ at the evaluation settings.
  \item $\kappa_{\min}$ is a statistical security floor. $\gamma$ also bounds what $\Rp$ can learn beyond $\mathcal M(D)$, including about $\Op$'s key share, so it should not grow with a generous $\delta_{\mathrm{run}}$.
\end{itemize}
$\zeta$ trades budget for bits. Shrinking $\delta_{\mathrm{DP}}$ from $\delta_{\mathrm{run}}$ to $(1 - \zeta)\delta_{\mathrm{run}}$ lowers $\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$ by a relative $\zeta/\sqrt{L(L + \varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}})}$ to first order (simple conversion, $L = \ln(1/\delta_{\mathrm{run}})$), so $\sigma_2$ rises by half that, while each unit of $\kappa$ adds one bit to $\Omega_f$ and to $q_0$. At $\delta_{\mathrm{run}} = 10^{-9}$ and $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}} \in \{1, 4, 8\}$ the tight conversion loses $0.46$--$0.59\%$ of $\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$ at $\zeta = 0.1$ and $0.04$--$0.06\%$ at $\zeta = 0.01$, and $\zeta = 0.01$ raises $\kappa$ by $3$ bits at $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}} = 4$ and $5$ at $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}} = 8$ (by at most $1$ where the floor $\kappa_{\min} = 40$ binds). Table~\ref{tab:kappa} gives $\kappa$ and the resulting scale at the previous setting ($f_1 = 0.1$); at $f_1 = 0.25$ (setting I) the scale is about $0.13$ bits lower and $\kappa$ is the same.

\begin{table}[ht]
  \centering\small
  \caption{Example at the previous setting ($f_1 = 0.1$): the flooding parameter from~\eqref{eq:kappa} and the final scale \textsc{Calibrate} chooses, simulation population ($p = 11$, joint mode, $n_{\mathrm{rel}} = 2$), $\delta_{\mathrm{run}} = 10^{-9}$, $\zeta = 0.1$, $\kappa_{\min} = 40$, $\Omega_U = 2^{68}$, $N = 2^{14}$, $q_0 \approx 2^{119}$, $\log_2(QP) = 357 \le 438$. Left: a single-run ledger. Right: ledger cap $(\varepsilon_L, \delta_L) = (10, 10^{-6})$ with $\zeta_L = 0.1$, $\xi_L = 10^{-3}$ and $\delta' = 2^{-100}$; $\kappa_L$ is below $\kappa_{\min}$, so $\kappa$ and the scale equal the single-run values (the statistical-distance accounting would give $\kappa_L = 48, 46, 44, 42, 40$). All values were computed by the implementation's \textsc{Calibrate}. Every row is feasible. At a fixed $\kappa = 40$, $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}} = 8$ is not: the slack exceeds $\delta_{\mathrm{run}}$.}

  \begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrrrrrrr@{}}

    \midrule
    0.5 & 36 & 40 & 0.48 & 61.78 & 61.78 & 88.0 & 22 & 40 & 61.78 \\

    2 & 38 & 40 & 1.53 & 63.69 & 63.69 & 89.9 & 19 & 40 & 63.69 \\

\paragraph{The scale.} Since $\sigma_{c,s} = \sigma_s\Omega_f\sqrt{2/N}$ and $\sigma_s$ is fixed by the budget, $\Op$ meets the flooding condition by raising $\Omega_f$; stage~2 binds:
\[
  \log_2\Omega_f \approx \kappa + \log_2 B_{\mathrm{tot},2} + \log_2\sqrt{N/2} - \log_2\sqrt{2\pi} - \log_2\sigma_2 .
\]
Example: for the simulation population at $\varepsilon = 1$, $\delta = 10^{-9}$, $N = 2^{14}$ and $\kappa = 40$, the proofs' bound gives about $40 + 25.3 + 6.5 - 1.3 - 9.0 \approx 61.5$, and the bound for OpenFHE's key switching $62.75$ (Table~\ref{tab:kappa}).

\paragraph{Encoding scales.} We use the circuit as written (Algorithm~\ref{alg:aggregate}) with separate encoding scales: $\Omega_U$ and $\Omega_u$ are parameters, \textsc{Calibrate} chooses $\Omega_V$, and $\Omega_f = \Omega_U\Omega_V\Omega_u/(q_1q_2)$ follows and is tracked exactly. The final scale is therefore not tied to the size of a rescaling prime, which a 64-bit implementation keeps below $2^{60}$. The constraint on $\Omega_U$ comes from $B_{\mathrm{tot}}$: the term from $\Rp$'s encryption errors grows like $(\Omega_f/\Omega_U)\sqrt{\bar n_O}$. With $\Omega_U \ge \Omega_f$ it stays below the other terms at every register size; with $\Omega_U < \Omega_f$ the required scale grows with $\bar n_O$, and for large enough $\kappa$ no scale floods, because $B_{\mathrm{tot}}$ then grows as fast as $\Omega_f$, and \textsc{Calibrate} refuses. In the evaluation $\Omega_U = 2^{68}$ and $\Omega_u = q_1$. The required scale is then independent of $\bar n_O \in [4096, 10^6]$ to within $0.05$ bits for $\varepsilon \le 4$, and grows by $0.4$ bits at $\varepsilon = 8$, where $\Omega_f > \Omega_U$ (Table~\ref{tab:kappa}). With $\Omega_U = q_2 \approx 2^{59}$ instead, the required scale reaches $2^{70.3}$ at $\bar n_O = 10^6$, $\varepsilon = 4$ and $\kappa = 40$. The proof review's restructured circuit, which needs about $2^{53.7}$, is not used: its proof is incomplete, and with decoupled scales the larger scale costs nothing.

\subsection{The ledger and composition across runs}
\label{sec:privacy:ledger}

$\Op$ keeps one ledger per base register, shared across all researchers and scopes. It admits a run only if the run's full cost fits under the cap, and it produces decryption shares only within admitted runs. Because no decryption is possible without $\Op$'s share, the cap is enforced technically, not by policy. A refused stage-2 gate (Section~\ref{sec:stats:stage2}) still spends the budget. The OPRF quota limits membership look-ups across sessions as a matter of policy.

\paragraph{Parameters and state.} The ledger's parameters are four:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item a cap $(\varepsilon_L, \delta_L)$;
  \item the share $\zeta_L \in (0, 1)$ of $\delta_L$ reserved for statistical slack;
  \item the share $\xi_L \in (0, 1)$ of $\varepsilon_L$ reserved for the smudging of the encryption error;
  \item a per-release constant $\delta' \in (0, 1)$.
\end{itemize}
From them,
\[
  E'_L = \xi_L\varepsilon_L/2, \qquad
  \rho_L = \max\{\rho : \delta(\rho, (1 - \xi_L)\varepsilon_L) \le e^{-E'_L}(1 - \zeta_L)\delta_L\}, \qquad
  \Gamma_L = \frac{\zeta_L\delta_L}{1 + e^{\varepsilon_L - E'_L}} ,
\]
where $\delta(\rho, \varepsilon)$ is the tight conversion. A run with $n_{\mathrm{rel}}$ releases at flooding parameter $\kappa$ costs three amounts:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item its zCDP cost $\rho_{\mathrm{run}}$;
  \item a smudging cost $\varepsilon'_{\mathrm{run}} = n_{\mathrm{rel}}\varepsilon'(\kappa)$, where $\varepsilon'(\kappa)$ is the smallest $\varepsilon$ with $\delta(\pi 4^{-\kappa}, \varepsilon) \le \delta'$, at most $2\sqrt{\pi\ln(1/\delta')}\,2^{-\kappa} + \pi 4^{-\kappa}$;
  \item a slack $\gamma_L = n_{\mathrm{rel}}(\delta_B + \delta')$.
\end{itemize}
The three costs come from the hockey-stick form of the smudging step (Remark~\ref{rem:sharperslack}). The state is their three sums $(\rho_\Sigma, E_\Sigma, \Gamma_\Sigma)$ over the runs admitted on the register. A run is charged its full cost, for all its planned releases, at the moment it is admitted, and it is not refunded if it is aborted or a release is never requested. Refunding the zCDP cost and the $2^{-\kappa}$-dependent smudging cost of releases not made would be sound, but the $\delta_B$ part must stay charged, because $\Rp$'s decision to continue may depend on its own key share and encryption errors, which enter $e_{\mathrm{tot}}$ (proofs, composition appendix, Remark~4.3); the composition statement below is proved for the rule without refunds. Requests are processed one at a time, and a run's cost is reserved atomically when it is admitted, so concurrent runs cannot jointly exceed the cap. The capacities are rounded down and the costs up. The evaluation uses $\xi_L = 10^{-3}$ and $\delta' = 2^{-100}$.

\paragraph{Admission.} Write $\rho^+ = \rho_\Sigma + \rho_{\mathrm{run}}$, $E^+ = E_\Sigma + \varepsilon'_{\mathrm{run}}$ and $\Gamma^+ = \Gamma_\Sigma + \gamma_L$.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{Runs fixed in advance}, with the parameters and inputs of every run fixed before the first run and independent of any output: admit iff $e^{E^+}\delta(\rho^+, \varepsilon_L - 2E^+) + (1 + e^{\varepsilon_L - E^+})\Gamma^+ \le \delta_L$.
  \item \emph{Runs chosen after seeing earlier results}: admit iff $\rho^+ \le \rho_L$, $E^+ \le E'_L$ and $\Gamma^+ \le \Gamma_L$. This is a privacy filter in the sense of Rogers et al.~\cite{rogers2016odometers}, and it is the rule for a shared register, where later runs are rarely fixed in advance. With $\kappa_L$ in~\eqref{eq:kappa} for every run, the smudging condition holds whenever the zCDP condition does. The slack condition does too, provided $n_{\mathrm{rel}}(\delta_B + \delta') \le (\tilde\rho_{\mathrm{run}}/\rho_L)\Gamma_L$, a constraint on $\delta_B$ that does not involve $\kappa$.
\end{itemize}
Either rule makes the joint view $(\varepsilon_L, \delta_L)$-IND-CDP (Proposition~\ref{prop:compose}; proofs, composition appendix, Corollary~5.4). A register on which a single run is allowed is covered by Theorem~\ref{thm:output} alone, with cap $(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}, \delta_{\mathrm{run}})$, which that run fills exactly; $\kappa_L$ plays no role there. The earlier accounting, which measures the smudging step in statistical distance, has two sums, $\Gamma_L = \zeta_L\delta_L/(1 + e^{\varepsilon_L})$ and the raw slack $\gamma = n_{\mathrm{rel}}(\delta_B + 2^{-\kappa}(1 + 2^{-80}) + 2^{-97})$ per run; it is also proved (Theorem~4.2 there) and remains an option of the implementation, but it multiplies the $2^{-\kappa}$ part by $e^{\varepsilon_L}$.

\begin{definition}[Base-register adjacency]
\label{def:baseadj}
A base register is $\mathfrak D = \{(\mathrm{id}_j, \mathbf a_j, \bx_j, y_j)\}_j$, with identifiers, public attributes, all of $\Op$'s feature columns and the response. $\mathfrak D \sim_{\mathrm b} \mathfrak D'$ if they have the same identifiers and public attributes and differ in $(\bx_j, y_j)$ of exactly one row. Scopes use public attributes only, and preprocessing acts row by row with public constants. So for every run, the padded scope registers of $\mathfrak D$ and $\mathfrak D'$ either coincide or are adjacent in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:adj}, whatever the scope, the model and the cohort (proofs, composition appendix, Lemma~1.3).
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Composition across runs]
\label{prop:compose}
Let all researchers using one base register act as one coalition, possibly colluding. The coalition chooses each run's parameters (scope, model, budget, flooding parameter, ring parameters from a fixed finite menu) and each cohort as a function of the joint view of earlier runs. It follows the protocol within each run, and it may interleave and abort runs. Assume that:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item every admitted run draws fresh CKKS key material and fresh release coins, and satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:output};
  \item the register is static;
  \item RLWE holds, as in Theorem~\ref{thm:output}, for every ring parameter set of the menu, against non-uniform adversaries;
  \item $\Op$'s ledger is honest, admits runs one at a time, and charges a run's full cost at admission.
\end{itemize}
Then the joint view of all researchers is $(\varepsilon_L, \delta_L)$-IND-CDP for $\sim_{\mathrm b}$ in two cases: under the second (filter) rule, for every PPT coalition; and under the first rule, when the runs are fixed in advance.

More precisely, the joint view is simulated, with statistical slack $\E[\sum_{k\ \mathrm{admitted}}\gamma_k] + \mathrm{negl}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sec}})$, by an ideal experiment. This ideal experiment is a post-processing of an adaptive $\rho^*$-zCDP mechanism whenever $\sum_{k\ \mathrm{admitted}}\rho_k \le \rho^*$ in every execution (proofs, composition appendix, Theorem~4.2). With the smudging step measured in hockey-stick divergence, the same holds with an $(\varepsilon'(\kappa), \delta')$ step per release in place of the slack $2^{-\kappa}(1 + 2^{-80}) + 2^{-97}$ (Theorem~5.2 there), which gives the ledger of this section (Corollary~5.4 there).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
The full proof is in the composition appendix of the proofs (Theorem~4.2). It has four steps.

\emph{Statistical hybrid.} Write $\Op$'s share as $v - c_1's_R$ with $v = f_s(D) + e_{\mathrm{tot},s} + \eta_s$. Remove $e_{\mathrm{tot},s}$ from every release of every run in one hybrid, before any computational step. A hybrid lemma for adaptive processes bounds the statistical distance by a single expectation under the real experiment: of $\sum_{\mathrm{made}}2^{-\kappa}(1 + 2^{-80}) + 2^{-97}$ plus $\delta_B$ for every planned release of every admitted run. That expectation is at most $\E[\sum_{k\ \mathrm{admitted}}\gamma_k]$, which each admission rule bounds pointwise.

\emph{Computational hybrids, run by run.} In admission order, replace each run's re-randomized $c_1'$ and then $\Op$'s key shares by uniform elements. The reduction for a step has $D$ as advice. It plays earlier runs ideally and later runs honestly, and it computes the adaptively chosen parameters by running the coalition, which is PPT, together with \textsc{Calibrate} and the ledger, which are deterministic and efficient. It announces the step's ring parameters before receiving its RLWE challenge, at a loss of the menu's size. The number of steps is at most $(n_{\mathrm{rel}} + 1)$ times the coalition's running time; under the filter it is also at most $(n_{\mathrm{rel}} + 1)\Gamma_L/\min_k\gamma_k$.

\emph{Ideal composition.} The ideal experiment releases $f_s(D) + \eta_s$ through lattice Gaussian steps whose dimension, centre and $\sigma$ depend on the history, interleaved with public steps of cost $0$: refusals, coins, the PSI, and uniform key shares and $c_1'$. A R\'enyi filter, proved there by the supermartingale argument behind the filters of Rogers et al.~\cite{rogers2016odometers} and Feldman and Zrnic~\cite{feldman2021individual}, makes it $(a, a\rho^*)$-R\'enyi DP for every $a > 1$ when $\sum\rho_k \le \rho^*$ pointwise. Refusals and the stage-2 gate are coordinates of the view, not events conditioned on.

\emph{Conversion.} Chaining as in Lemma~\ref{lem:sim2ind} gives $(\varepsilon, \delta(\rho^*, \varepsilon) + (1 + e^{\varepsilon})\Gamma^*)$ for every $\varepsilon$, where $\Gamma^*$ bounds $\sum_{\mathrm{admitted}}\gamma_k$ pointwise. Under the statistical-distance filter $(\rho^*, \Gamma^*) = (\rho_L, \Gamma_L)$ with $\Gamma_L = \zeta_L\delta_L/(1 + e^{\varepsilon_L})$, and the $\delta$ budget is used exactly. For runs fixed in advance, the admitted set is deterministic, and the first rule at the last admission gives the same bound. With the hockey-stick ledger, the statistical step is the $(\varepsilon'(\kappa), \delta')$ step of Remark~\ref{rem:sharperslack}, applied before every computational step so that the computational losses only add, and the conversion is Corollary~5.4 there: $\varepsilon = (1 - \xi_L)\varepsilon_L$, $E^* = E'_L$, and $e^{E'_L}\delta(\rho_L, \varepsilon) + (1 + e^{\varepsilon_L - E'_L})\Gamma_L \le \delta_L$.
\end{proof}

\tocheck{Composition: the proof, including the adaptive hybrid and the hockey-stick ledger, is written out in the composition appendix of the proofs (Theorems~4.2 and~5.2, Corollary~5.4), with a self-contained R\'enyi filter, so the cited filter results are attributions only. Not proved there: that the first rule (runs fixed in advance) is valid when the requests are chosen adaptively, where the proof gives only $\delta(\sup\rho_\Sigma, \varepsilon_L) + (1 + e^{\varepsilon_L})\sup\Gamma_\Sigma$, which can approach $2\delta_L$; and the cut-off argument for the expected-polynomial-time exact sampler inside the reductions, which is stated but not written out. The per-run results it takes from Part~A of the proofs were not re-verified. The bibliographic details of the filter literature were checked only through search-engine extracts: Feldman and Zrnic's theorem number in the NeurIPS version, the pages of Rogers et al.\ (1921--1929), and the volume and pages of Whitehouse et al.\ (PMLR~202, 36990--37007).}

Two consequences. First, a statement over many runs multiplies the slack by $1 + e^{\varepsilon_L - E'_L}$, not $1 + e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}}$. The smudging step's $2^{-\kappa}$ part, however, enters only through the small budget $E'_L$. At $(\varepsilon_L, \delta_L, \zeta_L, \xi_L) = (10, 10^{-6}, 0.1, 10^{-3})$, for example, $\kappa_L$ is $22$, $21$, $19$, $17$ and $15$ for $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}} = 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8$. That is below $\kappa_{\min} = 40$, so the ledger does not raise $\kappa$ (Table~\ref{tab:kappa}). The statistical-distance accounting would give $48$, $46$, $44$, $42$ and $40$, which cost $8.1$, $6.0$, $4.0$, $1.0$ and $0$ bits of $\Omega_f$ at $\bar n_O = 10^6$. The reserved share costs $0.2\%$ of $\rho_L$. $\delta_B$ is still multiplied by $e^{\varepsilon_L - E'_L}$: at $\varepsilon_L = 20$ the floor $n_{\mathrm{rel}}\delta_B$ alone exceeds every run's share unless $\delta_B$ is lowered well below $2^{-50}$. Second, the filter loses nothing against non-adaptive composition, because zCDP composes by summing a single parameter. For $(\varepsilon, \delta)$-DP, filters that match advanced composition, and a filter for approximate zCDP, are given by Whitehouse et al.~\cite{whitehouse2023fully}.

\begin{remark}[The smudging step in hockey-stick divergence]
\label{rem:sharperslack}
Let $e \in \Z^N$ with $\|e\|_2 \le B_{\mathrm{tot}}$ and $\sigma_c \ge 2^{\kappa}B_{\mathrm{tot}}/\sqrt{2\pi}$. Then $\eta + e$ and $\eta$ are $(\varepsilon'(\kappa), \delta')$-indistinguishable, with $\varepsilon'(\kappa) \le 2\sqrt{\pi\ln(1/\delta')}\,2^{-\kappa} + \pi 4^{-\kappa}$. For $\delta' = 2^{-100}$ this is at most $29.52 \cdot 2^{-\kappa} + \pi 4^{-\kappa}$, or $2.7 \cdot 10^{-11}$ at $\kappa = 40$, where the tight value is $2.0 \cdot 10^{-11}$. The bound follows from the R\'enyi bound for integer shifts and the conversion, and it needs no smoothing condition and no floor on $\sigma_c$ (proofs, composition appendix, Lemma~5.1). The ledger uses this form across runs (Theorem~5.2 there). Theorem~\ref{thm:output} keeps the statistical-distance form for a single run. With the hockey-stick form there too, $\kappa_{\mathrm{run}}$ would be $18$, $17$, $16$, $15$ and $14$ at a reserved share $\xi = 10^{-3}$ of $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{run}}$, and $\kappa = \kappa_{\min}$ in every row of Table~\ref{tab:kappa}.
\end{remark}

% ==============================================================
\section{Input privacy, cohort privacy and correctness}
\label{sec:input}
% ==============================================================

\paragraph{G1.} $\Op$ sees $\Rp$'s seeded uploads $c_0 = -a s_R + e + \Ecd(\mathbf v)$, which are pseudorandom under RLWE in $s_R$ (with circular security covering $\Rp$'s rotation-key shares, and the PRG modelled as a random oracle); the PSI messages, which are BFV ciphertexts under $\Rp$'s own key and blinded OPRF elements; and $\Rp$'s key-generation shares, which are RLWE samples. $\Op$ never receives a decryption share from $\Rp$, so its view can be simulated from $(n_R^{\max}, \bar n_O)$, up to a slack $p_{\mathrm{cuckoo}} + p_{\mathrm{false}}$: the failure probability of cuckoo hashing (at most $2^{-49.1}$ with $n_R^{\max} = 2{,}000$ items, $h = 3$ hash functions and $2^{14}$ bins, and at most $2^{-40}$ up to a load of $0.64$, for an insertion that finds an assignment whenever one exists, as the implementation's random walk with an augmenting-path fallback does; proofs, Part~A, Lemma~7.5; above $10{,}472$ items $\Rp$ splits its queries into the fewest balanced tables whose summed bound is at most $\delta_{\mathrm{psi}}$: two tables and $2^{-43.4}$ at $20{,}000$, ten and $2^{-41.1}$ at $100{,}000$), and the probability of a false match, most of which $\Rp$ detects and answers with an abort (Appendix~\ref{app:psi}). $\Op$ observes the abort, and its probability can depend on the cohort, so it belongs in the slack.

G1 and G2 concern $\Op$'s view of the protocol. $\Op$ receives no output, and its view includes $\eta$, so the guarantees do not extend to anything $\Rp$ later discloses (Remark~\ref{rem:whoknows}).
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{Disclosed results are noiseless to $\Op$.} A statistic or model that $\Rp$ computes from the releases and publishes is, to $\Op$, a function of $f_s(D) + e_{\mathrm{tot},s}$. The DP noise protects $\Op$'s records from $\Rp$ and from third parties; it never protects $\Rp$'s data from $\Op$. This matters beyond what Remark~\ref{rem:revealed} already discloses: $\bA$ is exact in a published model, and $\bC$ and $\bcR$ become exact to $\Op$.
  \item \emph{$v_s$ must stay with $\Rp$.} $\Op$ knows $h_{O,s}$ and $c'_{1,s}$, so $v_s$ would give it $c'_{1,s}s_R$. Since $c'_{1,s}$ is invertible with overwhelming probability, that gives $s_R$, and with it $\Rp$'s uploads: G1 and G2 would both fail. Disclosing $v_s$ is equivalent to sending $\Rp$'s decryption share, which would need flooding by $\Rp$ (the client-side noise of the protocols analysed by Hwang et al.~\cite{hwang2025insecurity}). $v_s$ is therefore never transmitted (Table~\ref{tab:ledger}).
  \item \emph{Decoded values at double precision are not a key leak.} Rounding $\Dcd(v_s)$ to double precision leaves an error of about $2^{19}$ per slot, or about $2^{12}$ per coefficient of $v_s$, because $|\tau(\eta)_k| \approx \sqrt N\sigma_c$. Even all slots at that precision would therefore give $\Op$ only a rounding-type RLWE sample of $s_R$ with that error at modulus $q_0 \approx 2^{119}$, far inside the secure region for $N = 2^{14}$. Only $v_s$ itself, or a decoding of equal precision, is dangerous. $\Rp$ still rounds every published value (Remark~\ref{rem:publish}); rounding is free.
\end{itemize}
A guarantee for $\Op$ in the standard sense, jointly with $\Rp$'s output, would need noise that $\Op$ does not know either. Hwang et al.\ do this by sampling the noise jointly; here $\Rp$ must not know it either, because of DP. That is a protocol change we do not make.

\begin{remark}[Publishing guidance]
\label{rem:publish}
Before $\Rp$ publishes any decoded value of release $s$, it rounds $\Dcd(v_s)$ to a fixed precision relative to $\sigma_s$, for example to a multiple of $10^{\lfloor\log_{10}\sigma_s\rfloor - 2}$, two decimal digits below the leading digit of $\sigma_s$ (Algorithm~\ref{alg:release}). Rounding is post-processing and costs no budget. $\Rp$ never publishes $v_s$. The implementation's \texttt{run\_R} returns the full decoded slot vectors for local use only; \texttt{round\_published} in \texttt{implementation/vfr/party\_r.py} does the rounding.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{G2.} $\Op$ evaluates exactly $n_R^{\max}$ OPRF calls on blinded inputs and answers padded PSI queries, so it learns nothing about the cohort or $n_R$, up to the same slack $p_{\mathrm{cuckoo}} + p_{\mathrm{false}}$ for $\Rp$'s observable aborts.

\paragraph{G4.} $\Rp$ learns $I$ and $\pi|_I$ from linkage. That it learns nothing else about $\Op$'s register needs the labeled PSI to be secure against a semi-honest receiver. Chen, Laine and Rindal prove their PSI from homomorphic encryption secure in the semi-honest model assuming the scheme is IND-CPA secure and circuit private, and note that statistical function privacy, obtained in the leveled setting by re-randomization and noise flooding, suffices~\cite{chen2017fast}. Chen, Huang, Laine and Rindal add OPRF preprocessing, which strengthens the security model~\cite{chen2018labeled}. Two layers of the protocol address what the noise of a reply could reveal.
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep, label=(\alph*)]
  \item \emph{OPRF-keyed items and labels.} Every item $\Op$ inserts is $F_k(\mathrm{id}_j)$ under a fresh key, and every label is $\tau(j)$ masked by a key derived from $F'_k(\mathrm{id}_j)$. $\Rp$ learns $F_k$ only at its $n_R^{\max}$ queries, so the item and the label of every row outside $I$ are pseudorandom to it; $\tau$ is a uniform permutation, and the masks $\mathbf r_1$, $\mathbf r_2$ are fresh. A reply, noise included, is a deterministic function of $\Rp$'s ciphertexts and of these values. A simulator that knows $(I, \pi|_I)$, the public sizes and $\Rp$'s view of the OPRF can therefore run $\Op$'s evaluation on fresh random items and labels outside $I$ and obtain replies indistinguishable from the real ones. In the random-oracle model for $H_1$, $H_2$ and the derived hashes, this holds under the computational Diffie--Hellman assumption in the OPRF group, with distinguishing advantage at most $q_{H_2}\mathrm{Adv}_{\mathrm{CDH}}$ plus a negligible collision term, for every input (proofs, Part~A, Theorem~7.3). Even without circuit privacy, then, what the noise reveals about $\Op$'s register beyond $(I, \pi|_I)$ is pseudorandom. When a bin has more than one partition ($\alpha > 1$; $\alpha = 5$ at $\bar n_O = 10^6$ with the PSI parameters of Section~\ref{sec:params}), $\Rp$ also learns which partition flags its match. That index depends on the loads and the insertion order of $\Op$'s items in the bin, and the simulator reproduces it by running $\Op$'s preprocessing, partition placement included, on the random items.
  \item \textsc{SanitizeBFV} (Algorithm~\ref{alg:sub}) is part of the protocol nevertheless, so that G4 does not rest on the pseudorandomness of the evaluation's inputs alone. It switches each reply and a fresh encryption of zero under $\Rp$'s BFV key to the reply modulus $q'$, adds them, and floods the noise with an exact discrete Gaussian $\mathcal D_{\Z^{N_B}, \sigma_\psi}$. After the switch, the error of a reply with plaintext $m$ is $\varphi(m) + w$, where $\varphi(m)$ is an integral function of $m$ with $\|\varphi(m)\|_\infty \le t/2 + 1$, from the change of scale $\lfloor q_B/t \rfloor \to \lfloor q'/t \rfloor$, and $w$ collects the rounding terms of the switches and the scaled evaluation and fresh-encryption noise. Let $B_\psi$ bound $\|w\|_2$ with probability at least $1 - \delta_{B,\psi}$, for every input of $\Op$. The requirements are
  \[
    \sigma_\psi \ge \max\{2^{\kappa_\psi}B_\psi/\sqrt{2\pi},\ 2^{42}\} \ \text{(flooding)}, \qquad
    q'/(2t) > t_\eta\,\sigma_\psi + B_\psi + t \ \text{(exact decryption)},
  \]
  where the term $t$ covers $\varphi(m)$. Then, as in hybrids H2 and H3 of Theorem~\ref{thm:output}, each reply ciphertext is within statistical distance $\delta_{B,\psi} + 2^{-\kappa_\psi}(1 + 2^{-80}) + 2^{-97}$, plus a computational term (RLWE in the randomness of the fresh encryption), of a ciphertext computed from its plaintext and fresh randomness alone. Over the $n_\psi$ reply ciphertexts of a run the distances add. The plaintexts themselves are $0$ or uniform, and a label or uniform, because of the masks.

  The argument holds for either order of the switch and the addition. Flooding needs only a worst-case bound on $w$: adding the fresh encryption before the switch leaves one rounding term, and switching both and then adding leaves two, which $B_\psi$ must then cover. Re-randomization needs the switched $c_1'$ to be uniform: under RLWE the fresh part $a_Bu' + e_1'$ is uniform modulo $q_B$, and rounding from $\Z_{q_B}$ to $\Z_{q'}$ maps the uniform distribution to the uniform distribution because $q' \mid q_B$, whether it is switched alone or together with the reply. Algorithm~\ref{alg:sub} uses the implementation's order, switching both and then adding.
\end{enumerate}
Neither layer is charged to $\delta$: the PSI transcript does not depend on $(\bx_O, y)$, so it lies outside the DP guarantee, and $\kappa_\psi$ is a security parameter of G4 alone. A modulus switch alone, as in the specification, is heuristic. Flooding protects against a semi-honest receiver, whose ciphertexts are fresh; a malicious receiver could send noisier ones, which is outside our model.

At the evaluation settings the implementation uses $\kappa_\psi = 40$ and $\sigma_\psi = 2^{\kappa_\psi}B_{\mathrm{err}} \approx 2^{61}$, with $B_{\mathrm{err}} = 2(\sqrt{N_B}/2 + N_B^{3/2}/2) + 1 \approx 2^{21}$: the worst-case rounding terms of the two switches for a ternary key, plus $1$. This $\sigma_\psi$ meets the flooding requirement for every $B_\psi \le \sqrt{2\pi}\,B_{\mathrm{err}} \approx 2^{22.3}$, which covers the rounding terms in the worst case, the rounding of $\varphi(m)$ included. With $q'$ of two 60-bit limbs, $q'/(2t) \approx 2^{87}$ against $t_\eta\sigma_\psi + B_\psi + t \approx 2^{64.3}$ at $t_\eta = 10$. In the textbook BFV model a worst-case bound covers the switched evaluation noise as well: for every input of $\Op$ its $\ell_2$ norm is at most $2^{-31}$ after the switch (product trees of depth $3$, $65$ coefficients per polynomial, $q_B$ of seven limbs and $q'$ of two; proofs, Part~A, Proposition~7.7 and Corollary~7.8), against $2^{-72}$ measured. Whether OpenFHE's BFVrns, whose multiplication and scaling are RNS approximations, satisfies that model was not checked, so for the implementation the $+1$ still rests on the measurement.

\tocheck{G4, second layer: check that OpenFHE~1.5.1's BFVrns (its RNS multiplication, scaling and relinearization) satisfies the BFV model of the proofs' bound on the evaluation noise (Part~A, Proposition~7.7), or bound that noise directly for it. The circuit-privacy statements of CLR17 and the role of OPRF preprocessing in CHLR18 were checked only through search-engine extracts; confirm against the full texts, including whether CHLR18 state that OPRF preprocessing removes the need for circuit privacy.}

\paragraph{Correctness.} With $q_0$ from \textsc{Calibrate}, honest inputs never wrap around; the failure probability is at most $\delta_B + 2Ne^{-t_\eta^2/2}$ per release, a correctness event that is not charged to $\delta$. No condition is needed at the earlier levels of the circuit: the error decomposition is an identity of congruences whatever the size of the intermediate plaintexts, so only the final reduction modulo $q_0$ can wrap around (proofs, Part~A, Proposition~8.1). The deterministic offset of the centre from $\Omega_f\tau_{\mathrm{can}}^{-1}(\mathbf z_s(D))$ has a proved public bound, $2^{30.3}$ in the coefficient domain at $\bar n_O = 10^6$ with $n_R^{\max} = 2{,}000$ (Proposition~4.5 there), far inside the slack of \textsc{Calibrate}'s condition. With the noise switched off, decryption reproduces the plaintext statistics up to the CKKS approximation error (Section~\ref{sec:eval:crypto}).

% ==============================================================
\section{The statistical procedure}
\label{sec:stats}
% ==============================================================

\subsection{Estimands}
\label{sec:stats:estimands}

The protocol targets two quantities.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item The \textbf{ridge target} $\btheta_\lambda = (\bG + \lambda\Psi)^{-1}\bc$, a fixed function of the matched data once $(\lambda, \Psi)$ is chosen. Only the mechanism noise enters its uncertainty.
  \item The \textbf{regression coefficient} $\bbeta$: the coefficient of the working linear model for the clipped data, $\E[\tilde\by \mid \tilde\bX] = \tilde\bX\bbeta$ with conditionally independent, homoskedastic errors, where $\tilde\bX$ and $\tilde\by$ are the clipped design and response. Its sample analogue is $\hat\bbeta = \bG^{-1}\bc$. Its uncertainty includes sampling variance, and $\btheta_\lambda$ is biased for it when $\lambda > 0$.
\end{itemize}
The working model holds for the clipped data when the bounds are true bounds, so that clipping never binds.

\begin{remark}[The projection coefficient]
\label{rem:projcoef}
Without the working model, the natural target is the projection coefficient $\bbeta^\ast = \bG^{-1}\tilde\bX^\top\E[\tilde\by \mid \tilde\bX]$ of the clipped data, which equals $\bbeta$ when the model holds. The bound on $\|\bbeta\|$ that the bias-aware interval uses holds for $\bbeta^\ast$ with no model assumed (Table~\ref{tab:status}), and the Wald interval for $\btheta_\lambda$ does not involve $\bbeta$ at all. The homoskedastic sampling variance of the bias-aware interval, however, does not hold for $\bbeta^\ast$ in general.
\end{remark}

When clipping binds, the clipped errors are heteroskedastic even if the errors before clipping are homoskedastic, and the homoskedastic sampling variance of the bias-aware interval (Section~\ref{sec:stats:ci}) need not hold. The interval stays valid for the projection coefficient, to the same order, if $\tilde s^2$ is replaced by a public bound $\bar\sigma^2$ on the conditional variances of the clipped response: $B_y^2$ always, or any bound on the error variance before clipping when only $\by$ is clipped, since clipping is $1$-Lipschitz and cannot increase a variance (proofs, Part~B, Proposition~S10b). On the synthetic population $B_y^2 = 8.35$ widens the sampling part of the interval by a factor of about $4$.

\tocheck{With the data-driven residual variance the bias-aware interval is not guaranteed when clipping binds: $\tilde s^2$ estimates an average of the conditional variances, not their maximum, and a sandwich variance would need a further release. Decide whether to report the interval with a public variance bound.}

\subsection{Stage 1: choosing the ridge}
\label{sec:stats:stage1}

Stage~1 releases $\hat\bB_1$ and $\hat\bC_1$ with s.d.\ $\sigma_1$, and $\Rp$ forms $\hat\bG_1$ with its exact $\bA$. The rule needs a radius $r_1$ with
\[
  \Pr\bigl[\lambda_{\max}(\hat\bG_1 - \bG) > r_1\bigr] \le \alpha_L .
\]
Then $\hat L = \max\{0, \lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1) - r_1\} \le \lambda_{\min}(\bG)$ with probability at least $1 - \alpha_L$. This one-sided event is the only one the procedure uses. The protocol allows two radii.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{Union bound:} $r_1 = \sigma_1(2\sqrt p + 2\sqrt{\ln(1/\alpha_L)})$. The name is historical: the radius comes from a Gaussian comparison bound, $\E\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf E_1) \le 2\sigma_1\sqrt p$, plus concentration of $\lambda_{\max}$ around its mean, not from a union bound. It holds for sub-Gaussian noise, and it is the radius the proofs cover. At $p = 11$ and $\alpha_L = 0.0025$ it is about $1.55$ times conservative.
  \item \emph{Exact quantile:} $r_1 = \sigma_1 q_{1-\alpha_L}$, where $q_{1-\alpha_L}$ is the $(1 - \alpha_L)$ quantile of $\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf E)$ and $\mathbf E$ is the stage-1 noise matrix with unit s.d.: symmetric, zero on $\bA$, and independent $\mathcal N(0, 1)$ on the released entries of $\triu\bB$ and $\bC$. Its law depends only on $(d_R, d_O)$, so $q_{1-\alpha_L}$ is computed offline by Monte Carlo from public quantities and costs no privacy. The implementation takes an upper 99\% confidence bound on the quantile from draws with at least $2{,}500$ expected exceedances ($10^6$ draws at $\alpha_L = 0.0025$). At $p = 11$ this gives $q_{0.9975} = 7.42$, against $11.53$ for the union bound.
\end{itemize}
A two-sided statement needs $\ln(2/\alpha_L)$ in the union bound and the $(1 - \alpha_L/2)$ quantile in the exact radius. The Monte Carlo quantile is computed for Gaussian slot noise with the calibrated s.d., while the encrypted noise is a discrete Gaussian on the coefficient lattice, each slot value being a sum of $N$ independent terms of it. A coupling settles the difference. Each release's lattice noise $\eta$ can be coupled with a continuous Gaussian $\mathbf Z$ of s.d.\ $\sigma_{c,s}$ so that $\eta = \lfloor \mathbf Z \rceil$, except with probability at most $N\,\mathrm{TV}_1(\sigma_{c,s})$, about $10^{-37}$ at the evaluation setting ($N = 2^{14}$, $\varepsilon = 1$, previous setting), where the total-variation constant $\mathrm{TV}_1(\sigma) \le 0.0202\,\sigma^{-2} + 0.0315\,\sigma^{-3} + e^{-2\pi^2\sigma^2}$ of one coordinate is proved (proofs, Part~B, Lemma~S0); the image of $\mathbf Z$ in the slots is exactly Gaussian with s.d.\ $\sigma_s$. On that event, and on the events that $\|e_{\mathrm{tot},s}\|_2 \le B_{\mathrm{tot},s}$ and that nothing wraps around, the slot noise differs from the Gaussian one by a vector of $\ell_2$ norm at most $\bar w_s$, which collects the rounding of $\mathbf Z$, the flooded error and the decoding offset, whose public bound is proved (proofs, Part~A, Proposition~4.5; Part~B, Proposition~S7). $\bar w_s$ is between about $2 \times 10^{-8}$ ($\bar n_O = 4{,}096$) and $5 \times 10^{-6}$ ($\bar n_O = 10^6$), against $\sigma_1 \ge 128$ in every setting of Table~\ref{tab:noff_sim}. The exact radius therefore holds for the protocol with $r_1$ enlarged by $\sqrt2\,\bar w_1$ and the failure probability enlarged by the probability of those events; the implementation uses the unenlarged $r_1$, a relative difference below $10^{-8}$. With Monte Carlo draws independent of the release, the order-statistic radius also has an exact level: $(M - j)/(M + 1) = 0.002385$ at $p = 11$, with $M = 10^6$ draws and the order statistic $j + 1 = 997{,}616$.

With the target $g^\star = 2\vartheta^\star\sigma_2\sqrt p$, the full ridge
\[
  \lambda_I = \max\{0,\ g^\star + r_1 - \lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)\}
\]
guarantees $\lambda_{\min}(\bG + \lambda_I\bI) \ge g^\star$ on that event. The $\Op$-block ridge $\lambda_O$ is the smallest $\lambda \ge 0$ with $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1 + \lambda\PiO) \ge \gamma$, where $\gamma = g^\star + r_1$. It is finite iff $\lambda_{\min}(\bA) > \gamma$, and then it has the closed form
\[
  \lambda_O = \max\bigl\{0,\ \gamma - \lambda_{\min}(\bS_\gamma)\bigr\}, \qquad \bS_\gamma = \hat\bB_1 - \hat\bC_1^\top(\bA - \gamma\bI)^{-1}\hat\bC_1 ,
\]
because $\hat\bG_1 + \lambda\PiO - \gamma\bI \succeq \mathbf 0$ iff its Schur complement $\bS_\gamma + (\lambda - \gamma)\bI$ is positive semidefinite, the block $\bA - \gamma\bI$ being positive definite. No bisection is needed. The $\PiO$ ridge can never certify a validity ratio above $\lambda_{\min}(\bA)/(2\sigma_2\sqrt p)$. The rule takes $(\lambda, \Psi) = (\lambda_O, \PiO)$ if $\lambda_O \le \kappa_\Psi\lambda_I$, for a parameter $\kappa_\Psi \ge 0$, and $(\lambda_I, \bI)$ otherwise. Shrinking only $\Op$'s block leaves $\Rp$'s coefficients unshrunk when $\Rp$'s own block is well conditioned.

\begin{proposition}[Independence]
\label{prop:indep}
$(\lambda, \Psi)$ is a function of the stage-1 release and of $\bA$ only, and is therefore independent of the stage-2 noise.
\end{proposition}

This is what makes the bias theory of Appendix~\ref{sec:bias} and the intervals below valid conditionally on $(\lambda, \Psi)$. Choosing $\lambda$ from the same release that is used for estimation would make $\lambda$ a function of the noise; Appendix~\ref{sec:bias:lambda} shows what goes wrong.

Setting I keeps $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$ and $\kappa_\Psi = 2$ from simulations of earlier variants of the rule, and setting P keeps $\kappa_\Psi = 2$ as well. Neither value was re-tuned under the two-stage rule with the exact radius; this is a limitation of both settings (Section~\ref{sec:params}).

\subsection{Stage 2: estimate and gate}
\label{sec:stats:stage2}

Stage~2 releases every entry with s.d.\ $\sigma_2$. $\Rp$ assembles $\hat\bG_2$ and $\hat\bc_2$, computes the validity ratio $\hat\vartheta = \lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_2 + \lambda\Psi)/(2\sigma_2\sqrt p)$, and releases nothing if $\hat\vartheta < 1$. Otherwise
\[
  \tilde\btheta = \tilde\bP\hat\bc_2, \qquad \tilde\bP = (\hat\bG_2 + \lambda\Psi)^{-1}.
\]
The gate is post-processing; a refused release still spends the budget. Coverage is unconditional: every coverage statement in this paper bounds the probability, over all releases, that $\Rp$ reports an interval that misses its target. A refused release reports no estimate and no interval, so it makes no claim and cannot miss, and it still spends the budget. For $\vartheta^\star > 2$ the probability of refusal is at most $\alpha_L + \exp(-p(\vartheta^\star - 2)^2)$ (proofs, Part~B, Lemma~S3). For $\vartheta^\star \le 2$, as in the prediction setting P of Section~\ref{sec:params} ($\vartheta^\star = 1.5$), there is no such bound. With $\Psi = \bI$ the ridge sets $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1 + \lambda\bI) = g^\star + r_1$, so the gate passes iff
\[
  r_1 + \bigl[\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_2) - \lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)\bigr] \ge (1 - \vartheta^\star)\, 2\sigma_2\sqrt p .
\]
The bracket is the difference of the downward shifts that the two noise levels cause in $\lambda_{\min}$; on average it is negative when $\sigma_2 > \sigma_1$. With $\vartheta^\star < 1$ the right side is positive, so the gate passes only when $r_1$ is large against $\sigma_2\sqrt p$, which depends on $\Delta_G/\Delta$ and $f_1$ through~\eqref{eq:sigmas}. Block normalization makes $\Delta_G$ small against $\Delta$, and a first choice of the prediction setting with $\vartheta^\star = 0.5$ was refused on most block-normalized releases (Sections~\ref{sec:eval:adassp} and~\ref{sec:eval:sweep}). With $\vartheta^\star > 1$ the right side is negative: at $\vartheta^\star = 1.5$ the gate refuses only if the stage-2 noise lowers $\lambda_{\min}$ by more than $r_1 + \sigma_2\sqrt p$ beyond what the stage-1 noise did. Setting P was chosen under an explicit bound on the refusal rate. It refuses at most $1\%$ of releases in every simulated cell, including $\sigma_1 = 0.46\,\sigma_2$, and at most $7.2\%$ on any of 29 real datasets (Sections~\ref{sec:eval:sim} and~\ref{sec:eval:adassp}).

\subsection{Intervals}
\label{sec:stats:ci}

\paragraph{Mechanism variance.} $\Rp$ knows the noise distribution, so it estimates the covariance of $\tilde\btheta$ due to the mechanism by a parametric bootstrap around its own release: symmetric noise $\mathbf E^\ast$ with s.d.\ $\sigma_2$ on the $\Op$-dependent entries of $\hat\bG_2$ and zero on $\bA$, noise $\mathbf f^\ast \sim \mathcal N(0, \sigma_2^2\bI)$ on $\hat\bc_2$, and $\btheta^\ast_b = (\hat\bG_2 + \mathbf E^\ast_b + \lambda\Psi)^{-1}(\hat\bc_2 + \mathbf f^\ast_b)$. A draw is kept only if $\|\tilde\bP^{1/2}\mathbf E^\ast\tilde\bP^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \eta_B$, for a truncation parameter $\eta_B \in (0, 1)$; without truncation the bootstrap moments do not exist. With $B$ draws the Monte Carlo error of $\bV_{\mathrm{mech}}$ is of relative order $B^{-1/2}$. Antithetic pairs $(\pm\mathbf E^\ast, \pm\mathbf f^\ast)$ reduce Monte Carlo error. $\bV_{\mathrm{mech}}$ is the sample covariance of the kept draws. To first order, $\tilde\btheta - \btheta_\lambda \approx \tilde\bP(\bff - \bE\btheta_\lambda)$, so
\[
  \bV_{\mathrm{mech}} \approx \sigma_2^2\,\tilde\bP\bigl(\bI + K(\tilde\btheta)\bigr)\tilde\bP, \qquad
  K(\btheta)_{ii} = \textstyle\sum_j \chi_{ij}\theta_j^2, \quad K(\btheta)_{ik} = \chi_{ik}\theta_i\theta_k \ (i \ne k),
\]
with $\chi_{ij} = 0$ iff $i$ and $j$ both lie in $\Rp$'s block. $\Rp$ may use this closed form instead of the bootstrap.

\paragraph{Wald interval for $\btheta_\lambda$.} The error level is split as $\alpha = \alpha_{\mathrm{CI}} + \alpha_L + \alpha_U$, a parameter. Setting I uses $\alpha = 0.05$ with $\alpha_L = \alpha_U = \alpha/20$, so $\alpha_{\mathrm{CI}} = 0.045$ and $z_{1 - \alpha_{\mathrm{CI}}/2} = 2.005$. Setting P computes no intervals; its $\alpha_L = 0.75$ only sizes $r_1$. Both intervals are computed only for releases that pass the gate, and their coverage is unconditional in the sense of Section~\ref{sec:stats:stage2}: a refused release makes no claim. The interval is
\[
  \tilde\theta_j \pm z_{1-\alpha_{\mathrm{CI}}/2}\sqrt{(\bV_{\mathrm{mech}})_{jj}} .
\]

\paragraph{Residual scale.} $\widetilde{\mathrm{RSS}} = \widehat{yy} - \hat\bc_2^\top\tilde\btheta - \lambda\tilde\btheta^\top\Psi\tilde\btheta$ is biased by the noise at order $\sigma_2^2$. The same bootstrap gives $\mathrm{Corr} = \overline{\mathrm{RSS}^\ast} - \widetilde{\mathrm{RSS}}$ and $\tilde s^2 = \max\{\widetilde{\mathrm{RSS}} - \mathrm{Corr}, 0\}/(n - p)$, which removes the $O(\sigma_2^2)$ bias up to $O(\sigma_2^4)$.

\paragraph{Bias-aware interval for $\bbeta$.} The total variance adds the sampling term, $\bV = \bV_{\mathrm{mech}} + \tilde s^2\,\tilde\bP\hat\bG_2\tilde\bP$. The ridge bias of coordinate $j$ is at most $\bar b_j = \lambda\|\Psi\tilde\bP\mathbf e_j\|_2\,\hat B_\beta$, where $\hat B_\beta = \min\{B_\beta^{\mathrm{pub}}, \sqrt{U_{yy}/\hat L}\}$ bounds $\|\bbeta\|$ from a public a-priori bound and a private one,
\[
  U_{yy} = \min\Bigl\{\widehat{yy} + z_{1-\alpha_U/2}\,\sigma_{yy} + B_y^2\sqrt{n\ln(2/\alpha_U)/2},\ nB_y^2\Bigr\},
\]
with $\sigma_{yy}$ the s.d.\ of the release that carries $\by^\top\by$. If the $y_i$ are conditionally independent given the design, $U_{yy} \ge \E[\by^\top\by \mid \bX]$ with probability at least $1 - \alpha_U$: Hoeffding's inequality bounds the sampling deviation of $\by^\top\by$, and the Gaussian tail the release noise, each at level $\alpha_U/2$, and the cap holds deterministically. (The specification's form spent $\alpha_U$ twice and left $\sigma_{yy}$ undefined.) The interval, following Armstrong, Kolesár and Kwon~\cite{armstrong2020bias}, is
\[
  \tilde\theta_j \pm \mathrm{cv}_{\alpha_{\mathrm{CI}}}\bigl(\bar b_j/\sqrt{V_{jj}}\bigr)\sqrt{V_{jj}},
\]
where $\mathrm{cv}_\alpha(t)$ is the $(1 - \alpha)$ quantile of $|Z + t|$ for $Z \sim \mathcal N(0, 1)$. The private bound is usually infinite while the ridge is on ($\hat L = 0$), so without a public $B_\beta^{\mathrm{pub}}$ the interval for $\bbeta$ is unbounded exactly when the ridge is needed. This is the honest answer: the data cannot rule out large coefficients in the directions the ridge suppresses.

\begin{remark}[How accurate is first order?]
\label{rem:firstorder}
The validity ratio controls convergence of the Neumann series, but the relative size of the neglected mechanism bias in the Wald interval is of order $\sigma_2\tr(\bP_\lambda) \le \sqrt p/(2\vartheta_0)$, which at $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$ and $p = 11$ (setting I) can be $0.66$. Coverage claims are therefore asymptotic as $\vartheta_0 \to \infty$ at fixed $p$. Algorithm~\ref{alg:solve} reports $\sigma_2\tr(\tilde\bP)$ as a diagnostic.
\end{remark}

The intervals are centred at $\tilde\btheta$, not at the bootstrap bias-corrected estimate $2\tilde\btheta - \overline{\btheta^\ast}$, because the squared mechanism bias is at most $0.19\%$ of the mean squared error under setting I (cell average; Section~\ref{sec:eval:bias}).

\subsection{When can the ridge be dropped?}
\label{sec:stats:noff}

The stage-1 rule is explicit, so the intersection size from which it stops adding a ridge can be read off the protocol parameters and one property of the data.

\paragraph{The threshold.} Let $\Sigma = \E[\bx\bx^\top]$ be the per-record second-moment matrix of the preprocessed, clipped design, intercept included, and $\ell = \lambda_{\min}(\Sigma)$ its weakest direction. With $n$ matched records $\bG \approx n\Sigma$ and $\lambda_{\min}(\bG) \approx n\ell$, up to a relative fluctuation of order $n^{-1/2}$. Ignoring the stage-1 noise in $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)$, the full ridge is $\lambda_I(n) \approx \max\{0,\ g^\star + r_1 - n\ell\}$, which falls linearly in $n$ and vanishes at
\begin{equation}
  n_{\mathrm{off}} = \frac{g^\star + r_1}{\ell}
  = \frac{\sqrt p}{\ell\sqrt{2\rho_{\mathrm{run}}}}\left[\frac{2\vartheta^\star\Delta}{\sqrt{1 - f_1}} + \frac{q}{\sqrt p}\,\frac{\Delta_G}{\sqrt{f_1}}\right],
  \label{eq:noff}
\end{equation}
by~\eqref{eq:sigmas}, where $q = r_1/\sigma_1$ is $2\sqrt p + 2\sqrt{\ln(1/\alpha_L)}$ for the union bound and $q_{1-\alpha_L}$ for the exact radius (Section~\ref{sec:stats:stage1}). With $p = 11$, setting I ($\vartheta^\star = 2.5$, $f_1 = 0.25$, exact radius, $q = 7.42$) gives
\[
  n_{\mathrm{off}} \approx \frac{19.1\,\Delta + 14.8\,\Delta_G}{\ell\sqrt{2\rho_{\mathrm{run}}}},
\]
against $(17.5\,\Delta + 36.5\,\Delta_G)/(\ell\sqrt{2\rho_{\mathrm{run}}})$ at the previous setting ($f_1 = 0.1$, union bound) and $(11.5\,\Delta + 8.9\,\Delta_G)/(\ell\sqrt{2\rho_{\mathrm{run}}})$ at setting P ($\vartheta^\star = 1.5$, $f_1 = 0.25$, $q_{0.25} = 4.44$). Here $\sqrt{2\rho_{\mathrm{run}}} = 0.089$, $0.173$, $0.335$, $0.642$ and $1.21$ for $\varepsilon = 0.5$, $1$, $2$, $4$ and $8$ at $\delta = 10^{-9}$. Since $\sqrt{\rho_{\mathrm{run}}}$ grows almost linearly in $\varepsilon$ at fixed $\delta$, $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ scales like the sensitivity divided by $\ell\varepsilon$. The two terms have different origins. $g^\star/\ell$ is the threshold if $\ell$ were known in advance; $r_1/\ell$ is the price of learning it from stage~1. With $\Delta_G \approx 0.9\Delta$, as for the simulation population, $r_1 \approx 0.69\,g^\star$ under setting I, so learning $\ell$ multiplies the threshold by about $1.7$; at the previous setting $r_1 \approx 1.9\,g^\star$ and the factor was $2.9$. The private bound $\hat L$ on $\lambda_{\min}(\bG)$, which makes the interval for $\bbeta$ finite without a public bound on $\|\bbeta\|$, becomes positive earlier, from $n \approx r_1/\ell$: about $0.41\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ under setting I and $0.65\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ at the previous setting.

\paragraph{Below the threshold.} With $\Psi = \bI$, the component of $\btheta_\lambda$ along an eigenvector of $\bG$ with eigenvalue $n\ell_k$ keeps the fraction $n\ell_k/(n\ell_k + \lambda)$ of the least-squares coefficient. In the weakest direction $n\ell + \lambda \approx g^\star + r_1 = n_{\mathrm{off}}\ell$, so the fraction kept is $n/n_{\mathrm{off}}$: the shrinkage there falls linearly in $n$ and is gone at $n_{\mathrm{off}}$. Directions with $\ell_k \gg \ell$ are barely shrunk. A weak direction carries little of the variation in $\bx$, so shrinking it costs little in prediction error but a lot in the coefficients that load on it; this is why prediction recovers before the coefficients do (Section~\ref{sec:eval:e2e}).

\paragraph{The transition.} The noise lowers $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)$ on average, the more so when several eigenvalues of $\Sigma$ are close to $\ell$, so the rule chooses a larger $\lambda$ than the formula and switches off after $n_{\mathrm{off}}$. Table~\ref{tab:noff_sim} gives the threshold for the simulation population of Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim} ($\ell = 0.599$, $\Delta = 86.2$, $\Delta_G = 77.2$, $p = 11$), and Table~\ref{tab:noff_check_v31} simulates the stage-1 rule at multiples of it.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item Setting I: the ridge is never off at $0.8\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$, off in $3$--$4\%$ of releases at $n_{\mathrm{off}}$, and off in $95$--$99.5\%$ at $1.2\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$. The previous setting gave $3$--$5\%$ and $94$--$99\%$. We take $1.2\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ as the size from which the ridge is off with high probability.
  \item Setting P: the transition is wider. At most $1\%$ of releases are off at $n_{\mathrm{off}}$, $46$--$63\%$ at $1.2\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ and $98$--$99\%$ at $1.4\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$. Its $g^\star + r_1$ is about $0.6$ times that of setting I in units of $\sigma_2$ (smaller $\vartheta^\star$, radius $q_{0.25} = 4.44$), so the downward bias of $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)$, which the formula ignores, is a larger share of it. The first choice P$_0$ ($\vartheta^\star = 0.5$, Section~\ref{sec:params}) switched off later still: no release off at $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ and $56$--$70\%$ at $1.4\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$.
\end{itemize}
Table~\ref{tab:calib_example} uses the committed bound $\ell = 0.494$ instead of the population value, so its thresholds are $21\%$ higher. Under the earlier mechanism, which used that committed bound instead of a stage-1 release, the ridge switched off at exactly $g^\star/\ell$ for that mechanism's $\sigma$ and $\vartheta^\star = 2$.

\paragraph{Check on real data.} On protein (Section~\ref{sec:eval:e2e}, $19{,}206$ matched records, $p = 10$), $\ell$ computed from the matched design predicts the ridge the encrypted runs chose (Table~\ref{tab:noff_protein}). Those runs used the previous setting ($f_1 = 0.1$, union-bound radius). The runs chose $1$--$19\%$ more than predicted, always more, as the downward bias of $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)$ implies; the gap is largest where $\sigma_1$ is largest.

\paragraph{What sets $\ell$.} $\ell$ is a property of the joint design after preprocessing, so near-collinearity decides $n_{\mathrm{off}}$, and it may lie within either party's block or across them. On protein, $\ell$ is about $0.002$ under every preprocessing, which keeps the ridge on to between $186$k and $13$M records at the previous setting. Setting I lowers these thresholds by a factor of $0.63$ (standardized) to $0.80$ (block-normalized), for example to $8.3$M and $555$k at $\varepsilon = 1$. The source of the small $\ell$ differs.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item Standardized: the weakest direction is $0.77\,x_{R,1} - 0.62\,x_{O,5}$; the two features have correlation $0.997$, so the two parties hold almost the same variable. $\Rp$'s own block is much better conditioned, $\lambda_{\min}(\bA/n) = 0.010$. Neither party can see this collinearity in its own data.
  \item Block-normalized: the weakest direction lies inside $\Rp$'s block, $-0.66\,x_{R,1} + 0.69\,x_{R,2} - 0.27\,x_{R,3}$, and $\ell \approx \lambda_{\min}(\bA/n) = 0.0021$: normalizing a block of strongly correlated features leaves it close to degenerate.
\end{itemize}
Table~\ref{tab:noff_drop} repeats the full preprocessing with one feature removed. Removing $x_{R,1}$ removes both weak directions: with block normalization $\ell$ rises to $0.013$ and $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ falls from $691$k to $102$k at $\varepsilon = 1$ and from $186$k to $27$k at $\varepsilon = 4$. Removing $x_{O,5}$ instead helps only the standardized design.

Two diagnostics cost no budget. First, $\bA$ is a principal submatrix of $\bG$, so $\lambda_{\min}(\bG) \le \lambda_{\min}(\bA)$ by Cauchy interlacing, and after linkage $\Rp$ obtains from its exact $\bA$ the lower bound $n_{\mathrm{off}} \ge n(g^\star + r_1)/\lambda_{\min}(\bA)$ before any release; $\Op$ can bound $\ell$ the same way from its own block on the register. Second, a collinearity across the parties is invisible to both but visible on a public reference set that carries both schemas, where one exists; screening features on it is the optional Setup module of Section~\ref{sec:protocol:preproc}.

\paragraph{Levers.} Table~\ref{tab:noff_levers} lists what lowers $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ at $\varepsilon = 1$ on the simulation population, where the previous setting gives $41.7$k. Setting I combines the exact radius with $f_1 = 0.25$, the value the pre-registered rule of Section~\ref{sec:params} chose. $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ alone would favour $f_1 \approx 0.35$, but it is flat over $0.25$--$0.45$, and the larger $\sigma_2$ of a larger $f_1$ costs accuracy once the ridge is off.

Below $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ the ridge is not a tuning choice: it is what certifies the validity ratio $\vartheta^\star$ on which the expansion and the intervals rest (Remark~\ref{rem:validity}). The levers lower the threshold; none of them makes the ridge unnecessary below it while the intervals are wanted. Setting P lowers the target itself and gives up the intervals. A study can be planned by evaluating~\eqref{eq:noff} at the intended $\varepsilon$, with $\ell$ estimated on public reference data or bounded through $\lambda_{\min}(\bA)$.

\paragraph{A rigorous bracket.} Two results bound the transition without simulation.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{The noise biases $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)$ downward.} For a symmetric $\bG$ and a zero-mean symmetric noise matrix $\bE$ with $\E\|\bE\|_{\mathrm{op}} < \infty$, $\E\lambda_{\min}(\bG + \bE) \le \lambda_{\min}(\bG)$, since $\lambda_{\min}(\bG + \bE) \le \mathbf v^\top(\bG + \bE)\mathbf v$ for a unit bottom eigenvector $\mathbf v$ of $\bG$. The gap grows with the number of eigenvalues of $\bG$ near $\lambda_{\min}(\bG)$. This is the bias that~\eqref{eq:noff} ignores.
  \item \emph{The switch-off is bracketed.} Condition on the data and take Gaussian slot noise. The rule returns $\lambda = 0$ with probability at least $1 - \alpha'$ once $\lambda_{\min}(\bG) \ge g^\star + r_1 + \sigma_1 q'_{1-\alpha'}$, where $q'_{1-\alpha'}$ is the $(1 - \alpha')$ quantile of $\lambda_{\max}$ of the unit stage-1 noise matrix, by Weyl's inequality. It returns $\lambda > 0$ with probability at least $1 - \alpha'$ while $\lambda_{\min}(\bG) \le g^\star + r_1 - \sqrt2\,\sigma_1 z_{1-\alpha'}$, because $\mathbf v^\top\bE_1\mathbf v$ is Gaussian with variance at most $2\sigma_1^2$. With $\lambda_{\min}(\bG) \approx n\ell$, the switch-off therefore lies between $(1 - \sqrt2\,\sigma_1 z_{1-\alpha'}/(g^\star + r_1))\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ and $(1 + \sigma_1 q'_{1-\alpha'}/(g^\star + r_1))\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$, with probability at least $1 - \alpha'$ at each end. The ratio $\sigma_1/(g^\star + r_1)$ does not depend on $\varepsilon$. The upper end cannot be improved in terms of $\lambda_{\min}(\bG)$ alone: for $\bG$ proportional to the identity it is attained exactly (proofs, Part~B, Proposition~S8).
\end{itemize}
On the simulation population at $\alpha' = 0.05$ ($q'_{0.95} = 6.303$ at $p = 11$) the bracket is $[0.87, 1.35]\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ under setting I and $[0.79, 1.58]\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ under setting P, at every $\varepsilon$. The simulated transitions, from $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ to $1.2\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ under I and to $1.4\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ under P, lie inside them.

A comparison bound gives the size of the bias for any spectrum: $\E\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1) \ge \E\min_{\|\mathbf u\| = 1}(\mathbf u^\top\bG\mathbf u - 2\sigma_1\mathbf g^\top\mathbf u)$ with $\mathbf g$ standard normal, by the Sudakov--Fernique inequality, and $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)$ concentrates with variance proxy $2\sigma_1^2$ (proofs, Part~B, Lemma~S8$'$). On the simulation population at $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ under setting I it bounds the downward bias by $3.2\,\sigma_1$, against $1.7\,\sigma_1$ in simulation, which is too loose to improve on the bracket.

\tocheck{The switch-off factors 1.2 (setting I and the previous setting) and 1.4 (setting P) remain empirical. A bound needs a tail bound for lambda-min of the noisy stage-1 Gram that uses the spectrum and is much sharper than the comparison bound; at n-off the three smallest eigenvalues of the simulation population's Gram lie within about 2.4 sigma-1 of each other, too close for second-order perturbation.}

\subsection{Mechanism bias}
\label{sec:stats:bias}

The noise in $\hat\bG_2$ biases $\tilde\btheta$ at order $\sigma_2^2$. Appendix~\ref{sec:bias} gives the leading term, $\sigma_2^2 M(\bP_\lambda)\btheta_\lambda$ conditionally on a high-probability event, with explicit constants (Theorems~\ref{thm:bias} and~\ref{thm:ridge}), and a zero-budget correction that subtracts its plug-in estimate. The correction is an option of the protocol and is off in our evaluation: the squared mechanism bias is at most $0.19\%$ of the mean squared error under setting I (cell average; Section~\ref{sec:eval:bias}).

\subsection{Status of the statistical guarantees}

The companion proofs (Part~B) establish: the union-bound radius $r_1$ for the one-sided event (exact); the independence and closed forms of the two-stage rule; the bound on $\|\bbeta\|$ for the projection coefficient with no model assumed; the Wald remainder exactly, with the plug-in variance to first order and normality of the sampling term asymptotically; and the bootstrap RSS correction to first order, without explicit constants. The exact-quantile radius of settings I and P has an exact level for Gaussian slot noise with fresh Monte Carlo draws, and the coupling of Section~\ref{sec:stats:stage1} transfers it to the lattice noise at a cost of about $10^{-37}$ in probability and a relative $10^{-8}$ in the radius; the total-variation constant of the coupling and the decoding offset have proved bounds (Part~B, Lemma~S0 and Proposition~S7; Part~A, Proposition~4.5). The bracket on the switch-off of Section~\ref{sec:stats:noff} is proved for Gaussian slot noise and transfers to the protocol by the same coupling; its upper end is attained by Gram matrices proportional to the identity, and the size of the downward bias of $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)$ is bounded only loosely (Part~B, Section~S8). The mechanism bias of Appendix~\ref{sec:bias} is proved with explicit constants (Part~B, Theorem~S9). When clipping binds, the bias-aware interval stays valid with a public bound on the conditional variance of the clipped response in place of $\tilde s^2$ (Part~B, Proposition~S10b); a guarantee with $\tilde s^2$ itself is open.

% ==============================================================
\section{Parameters}
\label{sec:params}
% ==============================================================

The protocol is defined for a parameter vector $\mathcal P$. Table~\ref{tab:params} lists every parameter: its role, the constraint it must satisfy, and the value used in this paper's evaluation. The last column is an \emph{evaluation setting}, one admissible choice among many; it is not part of the protocol.

The constraints are of three kinds. Some are hypotheses that $\mathcal P$ itself must meet, such as the ranges of the shares and the fact that the bounds and the preprocessing constants are public and fixed in advance; the parties check them in Setup. Some involve quantities that \textsc{Calibrate} derives: the existence of $\kappa$, $\delta_{\mathrm{DP}} > 0$, a scale that floods, the size of $q_0$ and the security bound on the chain. \textsc{Calibrate} checks these and refuses the run otherwise (Section~\ref{sec:privacy:calib}). The rest, such as $f_1$, $\vartheta^\star$ and $\kappa_\Psi$, affect only utility or cost and need only lie in their ranges. We call $\mathcal P$ \emph{admissible} if all constraints of Table~\ref{tab:params} hold. Theorem~\ref{thm:output}, Proposition~\ref{prop:compose} and the input-privacy and correctness statements of Section~\ref{sec:input} hold for every admissible $\mathcal P$, with the constants they state; none of them uses a particular value. The statistical results of Section~\ref{sec:stats} need in addition $\vartheta^\star > 1$ and the intervals on ($\alpha_{\mathrm{CI}} > 0$).

\paragraph{Two evaluation settings.} Sections~\ref{sec:eval:sim}--\ref{sec:eval:lessons} use two named settings of the same protocol. They differ only in the statistical parameters (Table~\ref{tab:settings}).
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \textbf{Setting I (inference)} keeps the interval guarantee. It uses the exact-quantile radius (Section~\ref{sec:stats:stage1}), $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$, $\kappa_\Psi = 2$, $\alpha = 0.05$ with $\alpha_L = \alpha_U = \alpha/20$, and $f_1 = 0.25$.
  \item \textbf{Setting P (prediction)} is tuned for test error only. It uses the exact-quantile radius, $\vartheta^\star = 1.5$, $\alpha_L = 0.75$, $\kappa_\Psi = 2$ and $f_1 = 0.25$. Its intervals are off and $\alpha_L > \alpha$, so the statistical results of Section~\ref{sec:stats} do not apply to it, and $\Rp$ reports the estimate without intervals. The interval guarantee holds only under setting I. P replaces a first choice, P$_0$ ($\vartheta^\star = 0.5$, $\alpha_L = 0.5$, $f_1 = 0.2$), whose gate refused most releases on block-normalized real data. P$_0$'s results are kept for reference.
\end{itemize}
Both keep the gate $\hat\vartheta \ge 1$, which is part of the protocol, and both have the same privacy guarantee: the noise depends only on $(\varepsilon, \delta, f_1)$, while $r_1$, $\vartheta^\star$, $\kappa_\Psi$ and $\alpha_L$ act on released statistics. The previous setting ($f_1 = 0.1$, union-bound radius, $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$, $\kappa_\Psi = 2$, $\alpha_L = \alpha_U = \alpha/20$), which Sections~\ref{sec:eval:crypto}--\ref{sec:eval:e2e} use, is kept as a reference.

\paragraph{How the settings were chosen.} On the synthetic population of Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim} only, by a rule written down before any selection or real-data run (\nolinkurl{reports/v31_settings/settings_choice.md} in the repository): $\delta = 10^{-9}$, $\varepsilon \in \{0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8\}$, $14$ values of $n$ from $100$ to $100{,}000$ with $200$ cohorts each (seeds independent of the validation runs), $R^2 = 0.5$, and common random numbers across candidates.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item I takes the $f_1 \in \{0.20, 0.25, \dots, 0.50\}$ that minimizes the mean, over the $70$ $(n, \varepsilon)$ cells, of $\log(\mathrm{RMSE}_{\mathrm{DP}}/\mathrm{RMSE}_{\mathrm{OLS}})$ of the slopes against the true coefficients. $f_1 = 0.25$ wins. The criterion is flat between $0.2$ and $0.3$, and $R^2 = 0.3$ and $0.9$ give the same choice.
  \item P$_0$, the first choice of P, took the candidate in $\vartheta^\star \in \{0.5, 1, 1.5, 2.5\} \times \alpha_L \in \{0.0025, 0.25, 0.5\} \times f_1 \in \{0.1, 0.2, 0.35\}$ that minimized the mean ratio of test MSE to that of non-private least squares on the same cohorts, with $\btheta = 0$ for a refused release. $(0.5, 0.5, 0.2)$ won. It lay on the edge of the grid in $\vartheta^\star$ and $\alpha_L$, and as pre-registered the grid was not extended. No candidate's gate refused more than $2\%$ of releases in any cell on this population. On block-normalized real data, however, P$_0$'s gate refused most releases (Section~\ref{sec:eval:adassp}).
  \item P was then re-chosen by a second rule, also written down before any run of it (\nolinkurl{reports/v31_P2/settings_choice_P2.md}). It uses two populations: the one above, and the same cohorts with each party's block of every row scaled to unit norm ($B_R^2 = 2$, $B_O = 1$, $B_y = 2.89$). At $f_1 = 0.25$ these have $\sigma_1 = 1.55\,\sigma_2$ and $0.46\,\sigma_2$. On Wang's 29 datasets block normalization gives $0.25$--$0.83$ (median $0.56$), with 8 below $0.46$. The candidates are $\vartheta^\star \in \{0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.25, 1.5, 2, 2.5\} \times \alpha_L \in \{0.0025, 0.05, 0.25, 0.5\} \times f_1 \in \{0.1, 0.2, 0.25, 0.35\}$, with the exact radius and the intervals off. P minimizes the same criterion as P$_0$, averaged over $140$ cells (both populations, five $\varepsilon$, $14$ values of $n$, $200$ cohorts each, $R^2 = 0.5$), among the candidates whose gate refuses at most $1\%$ of releases in every cell. Ties within $0.1\%$ go to the larger $\vartheta^\star$. The grid choice $(1.5, 0.5, 0.25)$ lay on the edge in $\alpha_L$, so as pre-registered $\alpha_L = 0.75$ was added once. $(1.5, 0.75, 0.25)$ wins, with a criterion of $1.241$ against $1.274$ for I and $1.286$ for P$_0$. It lies on the edge of the extended grid and, as pre-registered, was kept. The constraint binds: six candidates with $\vartheta^\star = 1.25$ do up to $0.3\%$ better but refuse $2$--$15\%$ of releases in some block-normalized cells, and no candidate with $\vartheta^\star = 0.5$ meets it. At $R^2 = 0.3$ and $0.9$ P is also first by the criterion.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Limitations of the settings.} Four limitations apply.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item The selection re-chose only $f_1$ for setting I, and $(\vartheta^\star, \alpha_L, f_1)$ for setting P. Setting I keeps $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$ and $\kappa_\Psi = 2$, and setting P keeps $\kappa_\Psi = 2$; these values come from simulations of earlier variants of the rule and were not re-tuned under the two-stage rule with the exact radius.
  \item Setting I's criterion averages over $n$, so it trades accuracy after the switch-off for accuracy before it. Once the ridge is off, $f_1 = 0.25$ leaves $9.5\%$ more noise s.d.\ in stage~2 than the previous setting, and the coefficient error reaches twice the sampling floor $9$--$13\%$ later at $R^2 = 0.5$ (Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim}). A criterion that weights the ridge-off regime more, or an $f_1$ that depends on the planned $n$ against $n_{\mathrm{off}}$, was not evaluated.
  \item Setting P lies on the edge of its extended grid, at $\alpha_L = 0.75$, so its stage-1 radius is the $25\%$ quantile of $\lambda_{\max}$ of the unit noise. Its refusal constraint binds: the six candidates with $\vartheta^\star = 1.25$ that do up to $0.3\%$ better on the selection populations refuse $2$--$15\%$ of releases in some block-normalized cells. P is therefore the best candidate of this grid under this bound, not an optimum; a wider grid or another bound could choose differently, and as pre-registered the grid was extended only once.
  \item Setting P was chosen after its first choice P$_0$ failed on block-normalized real data, and its selection populations cover $\sigma_1/\sigma_2$ down to $0.46$ at $f_1 = 0.25$ only. Under block normalization, 8 of Wang's 29 datasets have a lower ratio, down to $0.25$ (solar), and there its gate refuses more often than the $1\%$ it was chosen under: up to $7.2\%$ of releases on solar at $\varepsilon = 1$ (Section~\ref{sec:eval:adassp}). P is not validated below the selection's noise ratio or on response distributions heavier-tailed than those of the 29 datasets. The ratio is public before any release, so a prediction setting that depends on it is possible; it was not evaluated.
\end{itemize}
