\paragraph{The chain in the evaluation.} The implementation uses $N = 2^{14}$, a ciphertext modulus of four primes of $60$, $59$, $59$ and $59$ bits ($q_0$ is the product of the first two, about $2^{119}$; $q_1$ and $q_2$ are the others), two 60-bit special primes and decomposition number $2$, for $\log_2(QP) = 357$, below the 128-bit limit of $438$ for $N = 2^{14}$. A 64-bit build of OpenFHE keeps every prime below $2^{60}$. Honest inputs need $q_0$ of $80$--$92$ bits over $\bar n_O \in [4{,}096, 10^6]$ and $\varepsilon \le 4$, and $98$ bits at $\varepsilon = 8$ (Table~\ref{tab:kappa}); the specification's ``70--73 bits'' is too small. $N = 2^{15}$ brings no gain: it makes the aggregation at $10^6$ rows about $40\%$ slower.

\FloatBarrier

% ==============================================================
\section{Empirical evaluation}
\label{sec:eval}
% ==============================================================

All results in this section are for protocol v3.1: its calibration, the full OpenFHE implementation (Sections~\ref{sec:eval:crypto} and~\ref{sec:eval:e2e}), and plaintext simulations of its release and solve steps, which reproduce the encrypted runs (Sections~\ref{sec:eval:e2e} and~\ref{sec:eval:sim}). Each subsection states its settings. They are evaluation settings (Section~\ref{sec:params}), not part of the protocol. Sections~\ref{sec:eval:crypto}--\ref{sec:eval:e2e} use the previous setting: $f_1 = 0.1$, the union-bound radius, $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$, $\kappa_\Psi = 2$ and $\alpha = 0.05$ with $\alpha_L = \alpha_U = \alpha/20$. Sections~\ref{sec:eval:sim}--\ref{sec:eval:lessons} use the two named settings of Table~\ref{tab:settings}: I (inference) and P (prediction), with the previous setting as a reference. Unless stated otherwise $\delta = 10^{-9}$ and the bias correction is off. Every study fixed the flooding parameter in advance instead of deriving it by~\eqref{eq:kappa}; each subsection states its values. The simulation studies, the benchmarks and the encrypted checks of settings I and P use $\kappa = 40$, $40$, $40$, $44$ and $48$ for $\varepsilon = 0.5$, $1$, $2$, $4$ and $8$. The rule gives $40$, $40$, $40$, $41$ and $46$ at $\delta = 10^{-9}$ and $40$ at every $\varepsilon$ at $\delta = 10^{-6}$. Using it would change $\sigma_1$ and $\sigma_2$ by less than $0.2\%$ (at most $0.15\%$, at $\varepsilon = 8$ and $\delta = 10^{-9}$), because $\kappa$ enters the noise only through the slack. Where a subsection compares with the earlier single-release mechanism of the previous version of this paper (one analytic-Gaussian release, a ridge set from a committed eigenvalue bound, and the bias correction applied), it says so. The scripts that produce every number are in the \texttt{experiments/} directory of the project repository.

\subsection{The cryptographic layer is exact}
\label{sec:eval:crypto}

A prototype of the linkage and aggregation steps was run end to end over sockets, on six cohorts ($n = 200$ to $64{,}000$ matched records out of $n_O = 2{,}000$ to $80{,}000$) with a plaintext backend, and on one cohort ($n = 400$ out of $n_O = 4{,}000$) with OpenFHE~1.5.1 (BFV, $N = 16{,}384$, for the linkage; CKKS with scale $2^{50}$ for the aggregation). Labeled PSI recovered exactly the true match set in every run, with no misaligned rows (count, set and row-by-row alignment checked). With the noise switched off, the regression agreed with its plaintext target to $2.9 \times 10^{-10}$ with the plaintext backend and $1.7 \times 10^{-10}$ with OpenFHE. The OpenFHE run took about 13~s end to end. The prototype hashes identifiers with SHA-256 instead of an OPRF, uses a single CKKS key, and has no release step.

\paragraph{Full implementation of v3.1.} The \texttt{implementation/} directory of the repository implements every phase of v3.1 on OpenFHE~1.5.1, with $\Rp$ and $\Op$ as separate processes and a transcript of every message: RFC~9497 OPRF (ristretto255), labeled PSI under $\Rp$'s BFV key with flooded replies, threshold key generation, the seeded upload and key conversion, the aggregation circuit, and the release with exact discrete-Gaussian noise inside $\Op$'s share. A small C++ layer provides the polynomial-level access that the release needs. On the toy example and on a $4{,}096$-row synthetic register it recovers exactly the true match set and alignment; with the noise switched off by a test-only flag the decrypted statistics match their plaintext values to $10^{-11}$ relative; with the noise on, the measured noise s.d.\ is $0.996\,\sigma_2$ and $0.976\,\sigma_1$; the randomness-dependent encryption error stays about 8 bits below the worst-case $B_{\mathrm{tot}}$; and $\Rp$ never receives $c_0'$ while $\Op$ never receives anything that could serve as a decryption share of $\Rp$. Its deviations from the specification are listed in \texttt{implementation/DEVIATIONS.md}.

\subsection{Calibration under v3.1}
\label{sec:eval:calib}

Table~\ref{tab:calib_sigma} gives $\sigma_2/\Delta$ for two releases with $f_1 = 0.1$ (the previous setting) and $\by^\top\by$ free; $\sigma_2$ scales as $1/\sqrt{1 - f_1}$, so it is $1.095$ times larger at setting I ($f_1 = 0.25$) and at setting P, which has the same $f_1$; Table~\ref{tab:calib_ratio} compares it with the single analytic-Gaussian release the earlier experiments simulated. The planning formula of~\eqref{eq:sigmas} is conservative by $6.1$--$33.4\%$ over $\varepsilon \in [0.1, 8]$ and $\delta \in [10^{-9}, 10^{-6}]$: $6.1\%$ at $(\varepsilon, \delta) = (8, 10^{-9})$ and $33.4\%$ at $(0.1, 10^{-6})$.

    $10^{-6}$ & 1.15 & 1.14 & 1.14 & 1.13 & 1.13 & 1.12 & 1.11 \\

\end{table}

Table~\ref{tab:calib_example} works the calibration through for the simulation population of Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim} at $\delta = 10^{-9}$. Learning the ridge from stage~1 costs data: at $\varepsilon = 1$ the ridge stays on until about $51$k matched records at the previous setting ($33$k at setting I), against $16$k for a single release with the eigenvalue bound known in advance.

\subsection{Interval coverage under v3.1}
\label{sec:eval:coverage}

Coverage here is unconditional (Section~\ref{sec:stats:stage2}): it is counted over all releases, and a refused release makes no claim, while it still spends the budget. In the simulations of setting I (Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim}) and in the real-data check of Table~\ref{tab:coverage_real}, the gate refused no release, so conditional and unconditional coverage coincide there. Table~\ref{tab:coverage} simulates the release and solve steps of Algorithm~\ref{alg:solve} (2{,}000 replications per scenario, $p = 11$), in scenarios chosen to exercise each branch of the ridge rule. The Wald interval hits its target with or without the ridge. The bias-aware interval for $\bbeta$ is conservative, because it uses the worst-case bias and a cautious variance, and it is unbounded when the ridge is on and no public bound on $\|\bbeta\|$ is given. For contrast, a naive Wald interval around a ridge estimate, read as an interval for $\bbeta$, covered only $42$--$81\%$ in the earlier simulations when no eigenvalue bound was available.

\begin{table}[ht]
  \centering\small
  \caption{Coverage of both intervals over the 11 coefficients (simulation of the release and solve steps; noise levels set directly).}

    \toprule
    Scenario & $n$ & Ridge & Wald for $\btheta_\lambda$ (target 0.955) & Bias-aware for $\bbeta$ (target $\ge 0.95$) \\
    \midrule
    Ridge off & 4{,}000 & $\lambda = 0$ & 0.946--0.958 & 0.945--0.968 \\
    Ridge on, whole matrix & 1{,}500 & $\Psi = \bI$, median $\lambda = 477$ & 0.948--0.960 & 1.000 with a public bound on $\|\bbeta\|$; unbounded without \\
    Ridge on, $\Op$'s block only & 3{,}000 & $\Psi = \PiO$, median $\lambda = 439$ & 0.944--0.962 & unbounded without a public bound \\
    Small $n$, public bound & 200 & $\Psi = \bI$, median $\lambda = 1{,}021$ & 0.946--0.962 & 1.000 \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Real data with calibrated noise.} Table~\ref{tab:coverage} sets the noise levels directly. Table~\ref{tab:coverage_real} and Figure~\ref{fig:coverage_real} repeat the check for the Wald interval on real data with the noise of a real budget: setting I, $\sigma_1$ and $\sigma_2$ from Calibrate at $\delta = 10^{-9}$ and $\varepsilon \in \{1, 4, 8\}$, and block normalization with the reference rows of fold~0 (Section~\ref{sec:eval:adassp}). The data are fixed. The cohort is the first $n$ matched rows of fold~0, or all of them, so $\btheta_\lambda$ is a fixed function of the data and of each release's $\lambda$. Each cell has $1{,}000$ independent releases of the whole mechanism, with the implementation's bootstrap ($2{,}000$ antithetic pairs). Coefficients of one release share its noise, so each interval in the table is clustered by release.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item On protein, bike, elevators and pol, block normalization keeps the ridge on in every release at every size these datasets allow. Protein's $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ is about $80$k to $560$k for $\varepsilon = 8$ to $1$, and the block-normalized designs of bike and elevators are singular. We add 3droad ($p = 4$), where the ridge is off from $n = 1{,}000$ at $\varepsilon \ge 4$ and from $5{,}000$ at $\varepsilon = 1$, so that both regimes are checked on real data.
  \item The Wald interval covers $\btheta_\lambda$ $0.945$--$0.964$ of the time over the $48$ cells, and single coefficients $0.935$--$0.979$. On protein it covers $0.952$--$0.963$ in all twelve cells, and every protein interval contains the target or lies above it. The gate refused no release.
  \item While the ridge is on, the interval leans conservative: $26$ of the $40$ ridge-on cells lie above the target (elevators $0.959$--$0.964$). One cell lies below: 3droad at $n = 25{,}000$, $\varepsilon = 1$ ($0.945$). Rerun with $5{,}000$ fresh releases, it gives $0.955$ ($0.952$--$0.959$).
  \item Coverage of $\bbeta$ cannot be checked this way. With a fixed cohort, coverage of the full-data least-squares fit (the reference of Section~\ref{sec:eval:fws}) is conditional on one sample, whereas the bias-aware interval's guarantee holds over the sampling and the noise together. Where the mechanism noise is small against the sampling error, each coefficient is covered in almost every release or in almost none (3droad at $n = 25{,}000$: $0.67$--$0.85$ over three slopes). While $\hat L = 0$ the interval is unbounded anyway. Section~\ref{sec:eval:fws} checks the intervals for $\bbeta$ on real data with resampled cohorts.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Interval methods: the parametric bootstrap of Ferrando, Wang and Sheldon}
\label{sec:eval:fws}

Our Wald interval borrows the parametric bootstrap of Ferrando, Wang and Sheldon~\cite{ferrando2022parametric} (FWS). Their interval for differentially private least squares is also a competitor to our interval for $\bbeta$. This subsection runs it on the releases of setting I and compares coverage and width, estimand by estimand.

\paragraph{The FWS interval.} From released statistics $\hat\bG$, $\hat\bc$ and $\widehat{yy}$, FWS take the unregularized estimate $\hat\bbeta = \hat\bG^{-1}\hat\bc$, the scaled design $\hat Q = \hat\bG/n$, replaced by its projection with eigenvalues at least $0.1$ if it is not positive definite, and a residual variance $\hat s^2$. Each bootstrap draw simulates the sampling noise of $\by$ given the design and a fresh draw $(\bE^\ast_b, \bff^\ast_b)$ of the mechanism noise,
\[
  \bbeta^\ast_b = \bigl(\hat Q + \bE^\ast_b/n\bigr)^{-1}\bigl(\hat Q\hat\bbeta + \mathbf Z^\ast_b/\sqrt n + \bff^\ast_b/n\bigr), \qquad \mathbf Z^\ast_b \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf 0, \hat s^2\hat Q),
\]
and the interval is the percentile interval of the draws, coordinate by coordinate. We reimplemented it from their code (\texttt{DPCIs-OLS.py} of \texttt{github.com/ceciliaferrando/PB-DP-CIs}, commit \texttt{f0d2bc6}). Fed the same inputs and the same random numbers, the two give the same bootstrap draws to $10^{-15}$ (relative). On FWS's own simulation ($N = 1{,}000$, $d = 4$, $\varepsilon = 5$, Laplace noise, 400 trials) their pipeline covers $0.958$ and ours $0.950$, with median widths $0.581$ and $0.585$. We run two versions, both with $2{,}000$ draws at level $1 - \alpha_{\mathrm{CI}} = 0.955$, the per-coordinate level of our intervals under setting I.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{FWS on the stage-2 release:} the same $\hat\bG_2$ (with $\Rp$'s exact $\bA$), $\hat\bc_2$ and $\widehat{yy}_2$ from which our intervals are computed, and our noise law in the bootstrap: s.d.\ $\sigma_2$ on the $\Op$-dependent entries and zero on $\bA$. FWS release $s^2$ through a separate mechanism; here $\hat s^2 = (\widehat{yy}_2 - \hat\bc_2^\top\hat\bbeta)/(n - p)$, set to $0.1$ when negative, as FWS do with theirs. Stage~1 is not used.
  \item \emph{FWS with one release:} the whole budget on a single release of the stage-2 statistics, with no stage~1. This is the natural FWS mechanism under our adjacency and accountant. Its noise s.d.\ is $\sqrt{1 - f_1} = 0.87$ times $\sigma_2$ (for example $498$ against $575$ at $\varepsilon = 1$ on the synthetic population).
\end{itemize}
Both versions use only post-processing of statistics that the vertical protocol releases, so both could run in the vertical setting.

\paragraph{Estimands.} The methods target different quantities, so we report every method's coverage of every estimand.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item The ridge target $\btheta_\lambda = (\bG + \lambda\Psi)^{-1}\bc$ is a function of the matched data and of $\lambda$, and only the mechanism noise enters its uncertainty. Our Wald interval targets it. It is defined only for a release that chooses a ridge; with $\lambda = 0$ it is the sample least-squares coefficient $\hat\bbeta = \bG^{-1}\bc$.
  \item The regression coefficient $\bbeta$ of the working model for the clipped data (Section~\ref{sec:stats:estimands}). FWS target it, and so does our bias-aware interval; both intervals include the sampling variance. On the synthetic population we take the population least-squares coefficient of the clipped data, from 4 million rows. On real data we take the least-squares fit on all rows of the dataset outside the reference set.
  \item On the synthetic population, also the generating coefficients before clipping.
\end{itemize}
FWS's draws resample $\by$ given the design, so its interval is too wide for the sample coefficient $\hat\bbeta$. It has no ridge, so it has no $\btheta_\lambda$ of its own.

\paragraph{Synthetic population.} The cohorts, releases and intervals of setting I are those of Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim} ($\delta = 10^{-9}$, $R^2 = 0.5$, 200 cohorts per $n$ from $100$ to $10^5$, five values of $\varepsilon$, a fresh pair of releases per cohort and $\varepsilon$); our half-widths reproduce the published ones to $10^{-14}$. The ridge is on in every release in 41 of the 70 $(n, \varepsilon)$ cells, off in every release in 27, and mixed in 2. Table~\ref{tab:fws_cov} and Figure~\ref{fig:fws_cov} give coverage of the ten slopes and median half-widths.

\paragraph{Results on the synthetic population.}
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{The Wald interval and $\btheta_\lambda$.} The Wald interval covers $\btheta_\lambda$ $0.946$--$0.967$ in every cell, with the ridge on or off, and its half-width is $0.056$--$0.058$ while the ridge is on. FWS's interval is not centred on $\btheta_\lambda$: in the ridge-on cells where it is narrow enough to be informative it covers $\btheta_\lambda$ only $0.57$--$0.86$.
  \item \emph{Ridge off: FWS on our release is our bias-aware interval.} In the 27 cells where the ridge is off in every release, the two intervals for $\bbeta$ have the same median half-width to within $1\%$ ($0.0176$ against $0.0176$ at $n = 10^5$, $\varepsilon = 1$; $0.0053$ against $0.0054$ at $\varepsilon = 8$) and cover $0.943$--$0.958$ (FWS) and $0.946$--$0.962$ (bias-aware). With $\lambda = 0$ both add the same two variances, the mechanism's and the sampling variance, to the same estimate; one by a percentile bootstrap and the other by a Wald formula. FWS with one release covers $0.947$--$0.961$ and is $3$--$13\%$ narrower ($0.0154$ at $n = 10^5$, $\varepsilon = 1$), because it does not spend a quarter of the budget on stage~1; the difference shrinks as the sampling variance takes over ($2.6\%$ at $n = 10^5$, $\varepsilon = 8$). All three are wider than the Wald interval, which has no sampling term: read as an interval for $\bbeta$, the Wald interval covers only $0.62$ at $n = 10^5$, $\varepsilon = 8$.
  \item \emph{Ridge on: FWS is finite where the bias-aware interval is not.} While the private bound $\hat L$ is zero the bias-aware interval is unbounded: in every release at $n \le 10$k for $\varepsilon = 1$ and at $n \le 1$k for $\varepsilon = 8$. FWS uses no ridge and stays finite, and it covers $\bbeta$ in all 41 ridge-on cells ($0.951$--$1.000$). It is informative only once $\lambda_{\min}(\bG)$ is large against the noise. At $\varepsilon = 1$ its median half-width is $9.5$--$11.8$ for $n \le 2.5$k (where $\hat Q$ had to be projected in $93$--$100\%$ of releases), $5.3$ at $5$k, $0.40$ at $7.5$k, $0.21$ at $10$k, $0.12$ at $15$k and $0.070$ at $25$k ($n_{\mathrm{off}} = 27$k). At $\varepsilon = 8$ it is $8.8$--$11.1$ for $n \le 500$, $0.58$ at $1$k and $0.11$ at $2.5$k ($n_{\mathrm{off}} = 3.9$k). The change sits where $\vartheta_0 = \lambda_{\min}(\bG)/(2\sigma_2\sqrt p)$ passes $1$, near $6.4$k records at $\varepsilon = 1$ and $0.9$k at $\varepsilon = 8$. Between there and $n_{\mathrm{off}}$, FWS gives a valid finite interval for $\bbeta$ while ours is unbounded or, where $\hat L > 0$, wider (median $2.3$ at $15$k and $0.30$ at $25$k for $\varepsilon = 1$, finite ones only).
  \item \emph{FWS with one release} behaves like FWS on our release, with half-widths $13$--$41\%$ smaller in the ridge-on cells where both are informative ($0.28$ against $0.40$ at $7.5$k, $0.061$ against $0.070$ at $25$k for $\varepsilon = 1$), and coverage $0.948$--$1.000$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Real data.} We repeat the comparison on three of Wang's datasets with many rows (3droad, protein and bike), with block normalization and the reference rows of fold 0 as in Section~\ref{sec:eval:adassp}. The rows outside the reference set form a finite population, and the least-squares fit of the clipped $y$ on the clipped design over all of them is the reference coefficient $\bbeta$. Cohorts of $n$ rows are drawn from it without replacement, 200 per $n$, and on each the release, the intervals and both FWS versions are computed as above ($\delta = 10^{-9}$). A cohort is at most a sixth of the population, so the finite-population correction, which makes every interval for $\bbeta$ slightly conservative, is at most $17\%$ of the sampling variance. We also ran elevators but leave it out here: its block-normalized design is nearly singular, with a population coefficient of $5.4 \times 10^4$, so its least-squares fit is not a meaningful reference. Table~\ref{tab:fws_real} gives the results.

    protein & 1 & 1{,}000 & 1.00 & $0.962$ ($0.089$) & unbounded & $1.000$ ($16.2$) & $1.000$ ($15.9$) \\
     & 1 & 5{,}000 & 1.00 & $0.954$ ($0.087$) & unbounded & $0.998$ ($12.4$) & $0.999$ ($11.4$) \\
     & 8 & 1{,}000 & 1.00 & $0.958$ ($0.086$) & unbounded & $0.992$ ($10.4$) & $0.992$ ($9.54$) \\
     & 8 & 5{,}000 & 1.00 & $0.948$ ($0.074$) & unbounded & $0.994$ ($2.11$) & $0.983$ ($1.00$) \\
    \addlinespace
    bike & 1 & 500 & 1.00 & $0.960$ ($0.071$) & unbounded & $1.000$ ($16.3$) & $1.000$ ($16.7$) \\
     & 1 & 2{,}000 & 1.00 & $0.953$ ($0.071$) & unbounded & $1.000$ ($28.7$) & $1.000$ ($28.0$) \\
     & 8 & 500 & 1.00 & $0.967$ ($0.071$) & unbounded & $1.000$ ($24.4$) & $1.000$ ($21.5$) \\
     & 8 & 2{,}000 & 1.00 & $0.957$ ($0.078$) & unbounded & $0.940$ ($6.00$) & $0.943$ ($5.54$) \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{itemize}
  \item The Wald interval covers $\btheta_\lambda$ $0.938$--$0.978$ in every cell of the four datasets (elevators included), with the ridge on or off.
  \item On 3droad ($p = 4$) the ridge is off in 11 of the 15 cells. There FWS on our release and the bias-aware interval coincide again (for example half-widths $0.088$ and $0.089$ at $n = 5$k, $\varepsilon = 1$), and both cover the population coefficient $0.945$--$0.970$, although the real errors need not be homoskedastic. In the four ridge-on cells $\hat L$ is often positive and the bias-aware interval is finite but wider than FWS's ($2.17$ against $0.61$ at $n = 1$k, $\varepsilon = 1$).
  \item On protein and bike the ridge is on in every cell ($n_{\mathrm{off}}$ is $555$k on protein at $\varepsilon = 1$, Section~\ref{sec:stats:noff}), $\hat L = 0$, and the bias-aware interval is unbounded. FWS is finite but not informative: its median half-width is $2.1$--$29$ on coefficients whose population values lie within $\pm 2$, because $\lambda_{\min}(\bG)$ is far below the noise ($\vartheta_0 \ll 1$); $\hat Q$ had to be projected in every bike release and in most protein releases at $\varepsilon \le 2$. It covers $0.93$--$1.00$; the two cells below target are protein at $n = 2.5$k and bike at $n = 2$k, both at $\varepsilon = 8$ ($0.929$ and $0.940$).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Why the estimands matter.} The Wald interval is narrow because $\btheta_\lambda$ is a fixed function of the matched records: it answers what the protocol would have output without the privacy noise. FWS and the bias-aware interval answer a different question, what the coefficient of the working model is, and must add the sampling variance; with the ridge off they are the same interval. They differ in how they handle a poorly conditioned design. The bias-aware interval keeps the ridge and pays for its bias with a bound on $\|\bbeta\|$, which is infinite while $\hat L = 0$. FWS drops the ridge and lets the bootstrap measure the instability of $\hat\bG_2^{-1}$, which costs variance but no bound. Once $\vartheta_0 \gtrsim 1$ that variance is moderate, and FWS is the better interval for $\bbeta$; below it, neither gives a useful interval for $\bbeta$, and only $\btheta_\lambda$ has one.

\begin{remark}[Proposal, not proved: an FWS-type interval for $\bbeta$ while the ridge is on]
\label{rem:fwsprop}
While the ridge is on, $\Rp$ could also report an FWS-type percentile interval for $\bbeta$ from the unregularized stage-2 solve $\hat\bG_2^{-1}\hat\bc_2$, offered only when $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_2) \ge 2\sigma_2\sqrt p$, the gate with $\lambda = 0$. On the synthetic population it covers where the bias-aware interval is unbounded, from about $\vartheta_0 = 1$, roughly $0.25\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ (setting I). Its validity rests on the parametric bootstrap and is \emph{not proved} for the noise structure of v3.1, and its gate would use the same release as the interval. It is not part of the protocol.
\end{remark}

FWS release $s^2$ through their own mechanism; here it is the plug-in from the released $\by^\top\by$. Its residual sum of squares is biased downward by $\sigma_2^2\tr\bP + \hat\bbeta^\top\E[\bE\bP\bE]\hat\bbeta$ to second order, with $\bP = \bG^{-1}$ and an explicit remainder (proofs, Part~B, Proposition~S10a). Correcting it by that term changes FWS's coverage of the ten slopes by at most $0.0005$ in each of the $41$ ridge-on cells of Table~\ref{tab:fws_cov}, and its median half-width by at most $3\%$: where the ridge is on, the mechanism noise dominates FWS's width, and the plug-in is floored at $0.1$ in up to half of the releases.

\subsection{End-to-end runs of the implementation}
\label{sec:eval:e2e}

These runs use the full implementation: OPRF and labeled PSI, threshold CKKS upload and aggregation, two releases with exact discrete-Gaussian noise inside $\Op$'s share, and the solver, with $\delta = 10^{-9}$ and about $40$--$45$\,s per run on one core per party. They use the previous statistical setting ($f_1 = 0.1$, union-bound radius, $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$). They were made with a fixed flooding parameter, $\kappa = 40$ at $\varepsilon \in \{1, 4\}$ and $\kappa = 48$ at $\varepsilon = 8$, before the rule~\eqref{eq:kappa}, which gives $40$, $41$ and $46$ at the evaluation settings. The difference changes $\Omega_f$ but $\sigma_1$ and $\sigma_2$ by less than $0.2\%$.

\paragraph{Real data.} The protein dataset ($45{,}730$ rows, 9 features) is split into a public reference set of $10\%$ (standardization and transform constants, $99.5\%$ bounds), a test set of $20\%$, and $\Op$'s register ($32{,}011$ rows, padded to $32{,}768$). $\Rp$ holds 4 features and $\Op$ the other 5 and $y$. $\Rp$'s cohort is $19{,}206$ register members plus $1{,}000$ people $\Op$ does not hold. Table~\ref{tab:e2e_protein} gives five encrypted runs per setting. Every median lies inside the $95\%$ range of a plaintext simulation of the same mechanism, so the encryption does not change what the mechanism produces. Pooled over all 30 runs, the Wald interval covers $\btheta_\lambda$ for $286$ of $300$ coefficients (target $0.955$). A plaintext simulation of the same six configurations, with the noise levels of the encrypted runs and $10{,}000$ releases each, covers $0.955$--$0.958$, with release-clustered $95\%$ intervals inside $0.953$--$0.960$. The encrypted counts, $46$ to $50$ of $50$ per configuration, are what batches of five runs give: a plaintext batch covers no more in $21$--$100\%$ of draws (Figure~\ref{fig:coverage_repeat}).

\begin{table}[ht]
  \centering\small
  \caption{Protein, $19{,}206$ matched records: test MSE of the private estimate (median of 5 encrypted runs), the $95\%$ range of a plaintext simulation of the same mechanism (2{,}000 draws), non-private least squares under the same preprocessing, their ratio, and the median ridge. Predicting zero gives $1.010$.}
  \label{tab:e2e_protein}
  \begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccccr@{}}
    \toprule
    Preprocessing & $\Delta$ & $\varepsilon$ & Encrypted & Simulated range & Non-private & Ratio & $\lambda$ \\
    \midrule
    standardize & 87.3 & 1 & 0.883 & 0.871--0.896 & 0.728 & 1.21 & 29{,}678 \\
    standardize & 87.3 & 4 & 0.815 & 0.807--0.821 & 0.728 & 1.12 & 7{,}321 \\
    winsorize $|z| \le 2$ & 40.5 & 1 & 0.850 & 0.838--0.855 & 0.726 & 1.17 & 12{,}552 \\
    winsorize $|z| \le 2$ & 40.5 & 4 & 0.788 & 0.784--0.795 & 0.726 & 1.09 & 3{,}088 \\
    block unit norm & 8.4 & 1 & 0.793 & 0.786--0.795 & 0.745 & 1.06 & 1{,}385 \\
    block unit norm & 8.4 & 4 & 0.759 & 0.757--0.760 & 0.745 & 1.02 & 338 \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Block normalization costs a little in the non-private fit ($0.745$ against $0.728$) and gains much more in the private one: at $\varepsilon = 4$ its private error, $0.759$, is within $4\%$ of the best non-private model. The ridge is on in every setting, so the coefficients remain shrunk ($63$--$82\%$ of the way from least squares to zero with block normalization); prediction recovers faster than the coefficients do. Section~\ref{sec:stats:noff} predicts these ridges to within $1$--$19\%$ and traces them to a near-collinearity that keeps the ridge on until $186$k--$13$M matched records.

\paragraph{Synthetic data with the ridge off.} With the simulation population of Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim}, $20{,}000$ matched records in a $24{,}000$-row register and fixed data, 20 encrypted runs per setting gave $\lambda = 0$ every time. The estimate was within $4.0\%$ ($\varepsilon = 4$, $\kappa = 40$) and $2.5\%$ ($\varepsilon = 8$, $\kappa = 48$) of least squares (median relative distance), and the Wald interval covered $\btheta_\lambda$ for $0.950$ ($209/220$) and $0.927$ ($204/220$) of the coefficients, with standardized errors of mean $+0.04$ and $+0.03$ and s.d.\ $1.02$ and $1.05$.

A plaintext simulation of these two configurations on the same data, with the noise levels of the encrypted runs and $10{,}000$ releases each, covers $0.957$ ($0.956$--$0.958$, release-clustered) at $\varepsilon = 4$ and $0.955$ ($0.954$--$0.957$) at $\varepsilon = 8$. Single coefficients cover $0.951$--$0.961$, and the standardized errors have s.d.\ $0.993$ and $0.999$. So the coverage at $\varepsilon = 8$ is on target, and $204$ of $220$ is a low draw: a plaintext batch of 20 runs covers no more with probability $0.05$. Pooled over the eight configurations of this section, the encrypted runs covered $699$ of $740$ coefficients ($0.945$) against $707$ expected from the simulations (s.d.\ $6.1$; one-sided $p = 0.08$). The runs of Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim} test the encrypted coverage with many more runs.

  \caption{Wald coverage of $\btheta_\lambda$ in the configurations of the end-to-end runs of this section (previous setting): a plaintext simulation of the same mechanism on the same data with the noise levels of the encrypted runs ($10{,}000$ releases, release-clustered $95\%$ interval) against the encrypted runs (20 per synthetic and 5 per protein configuration; Wilson $95\%$ interval, which ignores the clustering and is therefore slightly too narrow). Horizontal line: the target $0.955$.}
  \label{fig:coverage_repeat}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Large-scale runs}
\label{sec:eval:large}

We ran the full implementation $78$ times at register sizes from $10^5$ to $10^6$ rows, in three regimes, each cell three times on one fixed dataset ($\delta = 10^{-9}$, $\kappa$ derived, a fresh single-run ledger per run, two felts per PSI item; Section~\ref{sec:cost} gives the cost). (A) The register grows and the cohort is fixed: $\bar n_O = 106{,}496$, $303{,}104$ and $1{,}007{,}616$ ($10^5$, $3 \times 10^5$ and $10^6$ rows of the synthetic population, padded), $n_R^{\max} = 20{,}000$ with $12{,}000$ matched records, settings I and P on the standardized population and P on the block-normalized one, $\varepsilon \in \{1, 4\}$. (B) A large matched set: $\bar n_O = 1{,}007{,}616$, $n_R^{\max} = 100{,}000$ with $60{,}000$ or $95{,}000$ matched records, setting I, $\varepsilon \in \{0.5, 1\}$. (C) 3droad: $\Op$'s register is its $304{,}413$ rows outside a $10\%$ reference set and a $20\%$ test set (padded to $311{,}296$), $\Rp$ holds the first feature, $\Op$ the other two and $y$, block normalization, $12{,}000$ matched records, settings I and P, $\varepsilon \in \{1, 4\}$. Before running we required, at each size of (A), exact linkage, the ridge and the estimates inside the $95\%$ range of a plaintext simulation of the same mechanism on the same data, a noise s.d.\ within $3\%$ of its calibrated value, and every phase within $25\%$ of the cost model; the last failed, as Section~\ref{sec:cost} describes.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{Linkage.} In all $78$ runs $\Rp$'s match set and every position agreed with the plaintext join. No felt-wise false match occurred, and the cuckoo insertion never needed its fallback.
  \item \emph{Noise.} Pooled over the $78$ runs, the $3.04$ million decrypted slot values outside the packed entries have mean $-0.0007$ (standard error $0.0006$) and s.d.\ $0.9997$ (Figure~\ref{fig:noise_large}). The released entries have s.d.\ $1.000$ (stage~1, $3{,}450$ values, Kolmogorov--Smirnov $p = 0.83$) and $1.012$ (stage~2, $4{,}224$ values, $p = 0.41$); per cell and release the s.d.\ lies within $0.994$--$1.007$.
  \item \emph{Against simulation.} The ridge lay inside the simulated $95\%$ range in all $78$ runs (under I at $12{,}000$ matched and $\varepsilon = 1$: $11.3$k--$11.8$k against $9.9$k--$12.9$k), and the coefficients in $0.939$--$0.975$ of cases per size of (A), with $|z| \le 3.4$ against the simulated law given the run's ridge. Under I the Wald interval covered $\btheta_\lambda$ for $344$ of $354$ coefficients ($0.972$); $400$ plaintext releases per cell on the same data cover $0.941$--$0.968$.
  \item \emph{Accuracy at $12{,}000$ matched.} The register size does not change the release, so (A) repeats the accuracy of Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim} at this $n$: the population test MSE relative to least squares on the same cohort is $1.26$--$1.33$ under I and $1.11$--$1.13$ under P at $\varepsilon = 1$ (standardized, ridge on; simulated medians $1.29$ and $1.13$--$1.14$), $1.008$--$1.018$ under P block-normalized, and at most $1.006$ at $\varepsilon = 4$.
  \item \emph{Regime B.} The ridge was off in all $12$ runs, as predicted: $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ is $52.5$k at $\varepsilon = 0.5$ and $27$k at $\varepsilon = 1$, and in simulation the ridge is on in $17\%$ of releases at $60$k and $\varepsilon = 0.5$, within the switch-off factor $1.2$, and in none elsewhere. The bias-aware interval for $\bbeta$ was finite in every run, with median half-widths $0.055$ and $0.029$ at $60$k ($\varepsilon = 0.5$, $1$) and $0.034$ and $0.018$ at $95$k, and in simulation it covers the true slopes $0.941$--$0.969$ of the time. Prediction is within $1.2\%$ of least squares; the coefficient RMSE is $9.1$, $4.7$, $4.7$ and $2.6$ times that of least squares.
  \item \emph{3droad.} The ridge was off in every release, and the test MSE lay within $0.9995$--$1.0014$ of the block-normalized non-private fit in every run. Block normalization reduces $\Rp$'s single feature to its sign, so that fit predicts little better than the mean ($0.973$ against $0.994$ on standardized $y$). The Wald interval covered $23$ of $24$ coefficients.
\end{itemize}
Figure~\ref{fig:accuracy_large} extends the simulation of Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim} to $10^6$ matched records, with the encrypted runs as spot checks. From $10^5$ matched records prediction is within $0.5\%$ of least squares at every $\varepsilon \ge 0.5$ in each setting, the ridge is off, the Wald interval covers $0.945$--$0.966$, and the coefficient RMSE relative to least squares falls to $1.45$ ($\varepsilon = 0.5$) and $1.15$ ($\varepsilon = 1$) at $10^6$ under I.

Hollow markers: the ridge is on in most releases. Crosses: the encrypted runs of regimes A ($12$k) and B ($60$k, $95$k).}

\begin{figure}[t]

  \caption{Decrypted noise of every release of the $78$ large runs, standardized by the calibrated $\sigma$ (all slots outside the packed entries, real and imaginary parts), against $\mathcal N(0, 1)$; one panel per stage of the runs ($10^5$, $3 \times 10^5$, $10^6$ rows, regime B, 3droad).}

\subsection{Coefficient accuracy against intersection size}
\label{sec:eval:sim}

\paragraph{Setup.} The population has $d_{R} = 4$ and $d_{O} = 6$ features on realistic raw scales (a score, a binary indicator, lognormal income and assets, a count and an age), Toeplitz-correlated with coefficient $0.3$. They are standardized with public population constants and clipped to the population's $99.5\%$ bounds ($B_{R} = 5.59$ with the intercept, $B_{O} = 6.24$, $B_{y} = 2.89$ at $R^2 = 0.5$). The generating slopes give $R^{2} \in \{0.3, 0.5, 0.9\}$. The weakest direction of the clipped design is $\ell = \lambda_{\min}(\Sigma) = 0.599$, estimated from $4$ million population rows, and $\lambda_{\min}(\bA)/n = 0.656$.

The mechanism is v3.1, simulated in plaintext with the implementation's own calibration and solver: a stage-1 release of $(\triu\bB, \bC)$ with s.d.\ $\sigma_1$, the stage-1 rule of Section~\ref{sec:stats:stage1}, a stage-2 release of every $\Op$-dependent entry with s.d.\ $\sigma_2$, the gate $\hat\vartheta \ge 1$, and the solve, with the bias correction off. The simulated noise is Gaussian in the slot domain with the calibrated s.d. The encrypted implementation's measured noise is $0.996\,\sigma_2$ and $0.976\,\sigma_1$ (Section~\ref{sec:eval:crypto}), and its estimates on protein lie inside the simulated range (Section~\ref{sec:eval:e2e}). A further encrypted check in the ridge-on regime is reported at the end of this subsection.

This subsection uses setting I (Table~\ref{tab:settings}) for the coefficients and the intervals, and adds setting P for prediction, with its first choice P$_0$ for reference. For P it also uses a block-normalized version of the population, on the same cohorts and noise draws. The previous setting is quoted for reference. Its runs used the same cohorts and the same unit noise draws, so the comparison is paired. All runs use $\delta = 10^{-9}$, $\by^\top\by$ in stage~2 (it is free for these bounds), and the fixed flooding parameter $\kappa = 40$ for $\varepsilon \le 2$, $44$ at $\varepsilon = 4$ and $48$ at $\varepsilon = 8$ (the rule~\eqref{eq:kappa} would give $41$ and $46$ at $\varepsilon = 4$ and $8$, and change $\sigma_1$ and $\sigma_2$ by less than $0.2\%$). With $\Delta = 86.2$ and $\Delta_G = 77.2$, setting I gives $\sigma_1 = 1737$, $892$, $461$, $240$, $128$ and $\sigma_2 = 1119$, $575$, $297$, $155$, $82$ for $\varepsilon = 0.5$, $1$, $2$, $4$, $8$ (Table~\ref{tab:noff_sim}). For each $n$ from $10^{2}$ to $10^{5}$ we draw 200 cohorts. For each cohort and each $\varepsilon$ we run an independent pair of releases, and we report the RMSE of the slopes against the true coefficients.

Lines: median over 200 runs, each with a new cohort and a new pair of releases. Bands: 2.5--97.5\% of runs. Hollow markers: the stage-1 ridge is on in most releases. Grey: non-private least squares on the true matches. Top: share of releases with $\lambda > 0$; dashed verticals at $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ of~\eqref{eq:noff}.}
  \label{fig:sim_rmse_v31}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Results.} Figure~\ref{fig:sim_rmse_v31} and Table~\ref{tab:sim_rmse_v31} show two regimes. Setting I moves the boundary between them and trades accuracy in one regime against the other.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{Ridge on.} While the stage-1 ridge is on, the estimate is shrunk towards zero, and its RMSE stays close to that of the all-zero estimate ($0.25$). The smaller ridge of setting I lowers the error here. At $n = 10^{4}$ the median RMSE is $0.197$, $0.154$ and $0.079$ for $\varepsilon = 0.5$, $1$ and $2$, against $0.213$, $0.184$ and $0.131$ at the previous setting. At $\varepsilon = 4$ the ridge is already off under I, and the RMSE is $0.023$ against $0.043$.
  \item \emph{Ridge off.} Once the ridge switches off, the DP error falls like $1/n$, faster than the sampling error, and merges into the floor. Here setting I is slightly worse, because $f_1 = 0.25$ leaves $9.5\%$ more noise s.d.\ in stage~2. The error reaches twice the floor at $n \approx 8.0$k ($\varepsilon = 8$), $27$k ($\varepsilon = 4$) and $88$k ($\varepsilon = 2$), against $7.3$k, $23$k and $79$k at the previous setting. At $n = 10^{5}$ the ratio is $3.2$ and $5.8$ for $\varepsilon = 1$ and $0.5$, against $2.9$ and $5.3$.
\end{itemize}
A stronger signal lowers the floor but not the DP error. At $R^2 = 0.9$ the crossings under I are at $25$k and $56$k ($\varepsilon = 8$, $4$; previous setting $23$k and $51$k), and none is reached by $10^5$ for $\varepsilon \le 2$. At $R^2 = 0.3$ they are at $5.9$k, $17$k and $67$k ($\varepsilon = 8$, $4$, $2$; previous setting $7.0$k, $15$k and $56$k). Only where the crossing lies close to $n_{\mathrm{off}}$, as at $R^2 = 0.3$ and $\varepsilon = 8$, does setting I reach it sooner.

The earlier single-release mechanism ($\delta = 10^{-5}$, committed eigenvalue bound) reached twice the floor at $3.2$k, $9.4$k and $34$k for $\varepsilon = 8$, $4$ and $2$. Under setting I these crossings are $2.5$--$2.9$ times larger ($2.3$--$2.5$ at the previous setting), close to the ratio of the noise variances, $(\sigma_2/\sigma)^2 = 2.5$--$3.0$ under I and $2.1$--$2.5$ at the previous setting.

\end{table}

\paragraph{When the ridge switches off.} Section~\ref{sec:stats:noff} predicts the switch-off under setting I at $n_{\mathrm{off}} = 53$k, $27$k, $14$k, $7.3$k and $3.9$k for $\varepsilon = 0.5$ to $8$, $35\%$ below the $81$k, $42$k, $22$k, $11$k and $6.0$k of the previous setting. The simulation agrees.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item On the grid of Figure~\ref{fig:sim_rmse_v31}, every point below $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ has the ridge on in every release, and every point from $1.3\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ has it off in every release. The two points just above $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ ($1.03$ and $1.08\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$) have it on in $91\%$ and $58\%$ of releases.
  \item Table~\ref{tab:noff_check_v31} puts the transition between $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ ($3$--$4\%$ of releases off) and $1.2\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ ($95$--$99.5\%$ off), as at the previous setting ($3$--$5\%$ and $94$--$99\%$).
  \item Below the threshold the rule chooses $1.5$--$1.6$ times the linear prediction, because $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)$ is biased downward.
  \item Near $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ the rule takes the $\Op$-block ridge $\Psi = \PiO$ in about half of the releases with $\lambda > 0$ ($51$--$54\%$ at $n_{\mathrm{off}}$), because $\lambda_{\min}(\bA)/n > \ell$. Below $0.8\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ it never does.
  \item Under setting P the switch-off is wider relative to its own $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ (Section~\ref{sec:stats:noff}): at most $1\%$ of releases are off at $n_{\mathrm{off}}$, $46$--$63\%$ at $1.2\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ and $98$--$99\%$ at $1.4\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$, and at $0.8\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ the rule chooses $2.0$--$2.3$ times the linear prediction. Under P$_0$ only $56$--$70\%$ were off at $1.4\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$, and the factor at $0.8\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$ was $3.0$--$3.4$.
\end{itemize}
While the ridge is on, setting I holds the validity ratio $\vartheta_\lambda = \lambda_{\min}(\bG + \lambda\bI)/(2\sigma_2\sqrt p)$ at a median of $5.0$--$5.2$ ($10$--$90\%$: $4.6$--$5.5$), against $7.9$--$9.2$ at the previous setting and $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$. Setting P holds it at $3.4$--$3.5$, and at $2.0$ on the block-normalized population (I: $3.2$; P$_0$: $2.9$--$3.0$ and $1.1$--$1.2$).

\paragraph{Prediction recovers first, and fastest under setting P.} On a held-out sample of the population (clipped design, unclipped $y$), setting I brings the test MSE within $10\%$ of that of non-private least squares at $n \approx 38$k, $20$k, $10$k, $5.3$k and $2.9$k for $\varepsilon = 0.5$ to $8$ ($0.72$--$0.76\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$, while the ridge is still on). The previous setting needed $58$k, $30$k, $15$k, $8.0$k and $4.2$k. Setting P, which was chosen for this, needs $26$k, $14$k, $7.1$k, $4.0$k and $2.1$k (Figure~\ref{fig:sim_pred_v31s}). That is $1.3$--$1.5$ times fewer records than I and $2.0$--$2.2$ times fewer than the previous setting. On this population the first choice P$_0$ needed $5$--$13\%$ fewer records than P ($23$k, $12$k, $6.2$k, $3.6$k and $2.0$k). Within $1\%$ the settings are close: $70$k to $5.0$k for P, $72$k to $5.0$k for I, and $91$k to $7.0$k for the previous setting. P's advantage over I shrinks with a stronger signal. At $R^2 = 0.9$ P needs $1.04$--$1.20$ times fewer records than I ($\varepsilon = 1$: $28$k against $32$k); at $R^2 = 0.3$ it needs $1.3$--$1.4$ times fewer. On this population the gate refused no release under I or P, at any $R^2$ and $n$. Under setting I, twice-the-floor accuracy on the coefficients needs $2.8$, $5.0$ and $8.8$ times as many records as prediction within $10\%$, for $\varepsilon = 8$, $4$ and $2$.

\paragraph{The block-normalized population.} The bottom row of Figure~\ref{fig:sim_pred_v31s} uses the same cohorts with each party's block of every row scaled to unit norm ($B_R^2 = 2$, $B_O = 1$, $B_y = 2.89$; $\Delta = 10.2$, $\Delta_G = 2.83$, $\ell = 0.105$). There $\sigma_1 = 0.46\,\sigma_2$ at $f_1 = 0.25$, against $1.55\,\sigma_2$ above, and P's $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ is $16$k to $1.2$k for $\varepsilon = 0.5$ to $8$.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item Setting P comes within $10\%$ at $9.4$k, $4.8$k, $2.5$k, $1.5$k and $729$ matched records, against $15$k, $7.5$k, $4.0$k, $2.1$k and $1.1$k under I ($1.4$--$1.6$ times fewer).
  \item Its gate refused at most $1\%$ of releases in any cell, $0.06\%$ on average, and also at most $1\%$ at $R^2 = 0.3$ and $0.9$.
  \item P$_0$'s gate refused $98$--$100\%$ of releases wherever its ridge was on, so its test MSE stays near that of predicting the mean until the ridge switches off.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[t]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/sim_pred_vs_n_v31p2.png}
  \caption{Test MSE on the population divided by that of non-private least squares on the same cohort, against $n$, $\delta = 10^{-9}$, $R^2 = 0.5$. Top row: the standardized population. Bottom row: the same cohorts with block-normalized features. Mean over 200 cohorts, a refused release counted as $\btheta = 0$. Blue: setting I. Orange: setting P. Grey dashed: P$_0$, the first choice of P, whose gate refuses the block-normalized releases while its ridge is on. Hollow markers: the stage-1 ridge is on in most releases. Dotted horizontal: within $10\%$.}
  \label{fig:sim_pred_v31s}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Coefficients on different scales.} Raw-scale errors cannot be compared across coefficients, so Figure~\ref{fig:sim_eff_v31} shows the \emph{efficiency ratio} $\mathrm{RMSE}_{\mathrm{DP},j}/\mathrm{SE}_{\mathrm{OLS},j}$ under setting I. It does not change when $x_j$ or $y$ is rescaled. A ratio $r$ means the release is about as accurate as least squares on $n/r^2$ records. While the ridge is on, the ratio grows with $n$ and is largest for the largest coefficients, which the ridge shrinks most. Once $\lambda = 0$ in every release, the ten slopes' ratios lie within $-21\%$ and $+25\%$ of their median, except at $\varepsilon = 8$ and $n \ge 50$k, where one coefficient reaches $+34\%$ (previous setting: $-20\%$ to $+26\%$, and $+36\%$). Neither block is consistently harder: the $\Op$-block average is $0.90$--$1.19$ times the $\Rp$-block average.

\begin{figure}[t]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/sim_efficiency_ratio_v31s.png}
  \caption{Efficiency ratio $\mathrm{RMSE}_{\mathrm{DP},j}/\mathrm{SE}_{\mathrm{OLS},j}$ per coefficient against $n$, setting I, $\delta = 10^{-9}$, $R^{2} = 0.5$. Top row: $\Rp$'s block, with the intercept. Bottom row: $\Op$'s block. Hollow markers: the stage-1 ridge is on in most releases.}
  \label{fig:sim_eff_v31}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Inference under setting I.} At $R^2 = 0.5$ every release also runs the intervals of Section~\ref{sec:stats:ci}, with a bootstrap of $2{,}000$ antithetic pairs and no public bound on $\|\bbeta\|$. Coverage holds in every regime we simulated.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{Wald interval for $\btheta_\lambda$.} It covers $0.945$--$0.966$ of the coefficients in each of the 70 $(n, \varepsilon)$ cells, pooled over the 200 releases and 11 coefficients, with the ridge on or off (target $0.955$). The previous setting gave $0.945$--$0.965$. The lowest single coefficient in any cell covers $0.91$ under both settings, which is what 770 coefficient-cells of 200 releases produce by chance.
  \item \emph{Bias-aware interval for $\bbeta$.} When the ridge is off in every release (27 cells under I, 20 at the previous setting), it is finite and covers the true slopes $0.930$--$0.964$ of the time and the clipped-population coefficients $0.946$--$0.962$ (previous setting: $0.930$--$0.964$ and $0.945$--$0.962$); at large $n$ clipping moves the target. While the ridge is on, $\hat L = 0$ in most releases and the interval is unbounded. Where $\hat L > 0$ it is finite but wide: median half-width up to $3.2$, against $0.058$ for the Wald interval.
  \item \emph{Naive reading.} Read as an interval for $\bbeta$, the Wald interval covers only $9$--$85\%$ in the 41 cells where the ridge is on in every release (previous setting: $2$--$60\%$ in 47 cells). The reports also give $0.088$--$0.950$ over the 43 cells where the ridge is on in most releases. These add two cells in which the ridge is off in $9\%$ and $42\%$ of releases ($n = 7{,}500$ at $\varepsilon = 4$, and $n = 15{,}000$ at $\varepsilon = 2$), which cover $0.90$ and $0.95$. The smaller ridge of setting I shrinks less, so the naive reading is less wrong, but it is still wrong.
\end{itemize}
The gate refused no release in any cell, at any $R^2$.

\paragraph{Encrypted check of settings I and P.} We ran the full OpenFHE implementation $450$ times on one synthetic dataset from this population: $n = 10{,}000$ matched records in a register of $12{,}000$ rows padded to $16{,}384$, $\delta = 10^{-9}$. There were three configurations. In two of them the ridge is on: setting I at $\varepsilon = 2$ and setting P at $\varepsilon = 1$, where the matched design's $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ is $14.1$k and $16.4$k. In the third it is off: setting I at $\varepsilon = 8$, where $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ is $3.9$k. The flooding parameter was $\kappa = 40$, $40$ and $48$. Each configuration got $100$ runs. Because I at $\varepsilon = 2$ came out low (below), $150$ more runs of it followed with fresh seeds. A run took $21$--$23$\,s (median; three runs at a time, one core per party). $\Rp$ read the exact radius from the cached quantile: $r_1 = 3{,}419$ under I at $\varepsilon = 2$ and $3{,}964$ under P.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item No release was refused, and $\bA$ was exact. We rebuilt each run's decrypted releases from its recorded noise and fed them to the plaintext solver with the run's seed. This gave the run's $\lambda$, estimate and half-widths to within $10^{-11}$ (relative), so an encrypted run differs from a plaintext one only through its noise.
  \item The ridge lay inside the $95\%$ range of a plaintext simulation of the same mechanism on the same data (4{,}000 draws). Under I at $\varepsilon = 2$ it did so in $95$ of $100$ runs and $144$ of $150$ runs of the repeat (range $2{,}578$--$4{,}232$). Under P it did so in $92$ of $100$ runs ($4{,}837$--$7{,}855$). Under I at $\varepsilon = 8$ it was $0$ in every run, as simulated. The encrypted coefficients lay inside the simulated $95\%$ ranges in $0.940$, $0.955$, $0.944$ and $0.957$ of cases (I at $\varepsilon = 2$, its repeat, P, I at $\varepsilon = 8$).
  \item Under I at $\varepsilon = 2$, the Wald interval covered $\btheta_\lambda$ for $1{,}037$ of $1{,}100$ coefficients ($0.943$; release-clustered $95\%$ interval $0.928$--$0.958$). The repeat covered $1{,}586$ of $1{,}650$ ($0.961$; $0.953$--$0.970$), and the two together $0.954$ ($0.946$--$0.962$). Under I at $\varepsilon = 8$ it covered $1{,}054$ of $1{,}100$ ($0.958$; $0.945$--$0.971$). The plaintext simulation of the same configurations covers $0.9585$ and $0.9555$ ($10{,}000$ releases each). The first $100$ runs at $\varepsilon = 2$ lie just below the $2.5\%$ quantile of the plaintext simulation for 100 runs ($1{,}040$). The repeat and the noise checks below put this down to chance.
  \item The raw decrypted noise was standardized by the calibrated $\sigma_1$ and $\sigma_2$: in stage~1, the $51$ $\Op$-dependent entries of $\bG$; in stage~2, those, $\bc$ and $y^\top y$. Pooled over all $450$ runs, the stage-1 noise has mean $+0.006$ (standard error $0.007$) and s.d.\ $0.995$ (Kolmogorov--Smirnov $p = 0.13$ against the calibrated law, $\mathcal N(0, 1)$ after standardization). The stage-2 noise has mean $+0.004$ ($0.006$) and s.d.\ $1.006$ ($p = 0.48$). For each configuration and release, every mean lies within $1.9$ standard errors of zero. The slots outside the packed entries carry the same noise on a plaintext zero. There are more than $1.6$ million of them per configuration and release. Their mean lies within $0.0011$ of zero (standard error $0.0006$--$0.0008$), and their s.d.\ is $0.999$--$1.001$, release by release. Over all $14.7$ million slot values the mean is $-0.0004$ (standard error $0.0003$).
  \item The negative means of the earlier five-run checks are not reproduced: $-0.17$ on the stage-1 noise under I, and $-0.10$ under P (Figure~\ref{fig:noise_more}). The same configurations now give $+0.03$ and $-0.001$ on the stage-1 noise under I ($5{,}100$ and $7{,}650$ entries), and $+0.006$ and $-0.003$ under P.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[t]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/noise_more.png}
  \caption{Raw decrypted noise of $450$ encrypted runs (settings I and P, $n = 10{,}000$, register $16{,}384$), standardized by the calibrated $\sigma_1$ and $\sigma_2$. Left: histogram of all released entries against $\mathcal N(0, 1)$. Middle: quantile plot. Right: mean with $95\%$ interval by configuration (I2: setting I at $\varepsilon = 2$; I2r: its repeat; P1: setting P at $\varepsilon = 1$; I8: setting I at $\varepsilon = 8$) and release, pooled, and for the earlier five-run checks (hollow).}
  \label{fig:noise_more}
\end{figure}

% ---------------------------------------------------------------- 12.6
\subsection{Does the bias correction matter?}
\label{sec:eval:bias}

Under v3.1 the ridge comes from stage~1, so it is independent of the stage-2 noise (Proposition~\ref{prop:indep}), and Theorem~\ref{thm:ridge} applies conditionally on $(\lambda, \Psi)$. We ask the question again with the earlier conditional design at the previous setting ($\delta = 10^{-9}$, $R^2 = 0.5$, fixed $\kappa = 40$, $40$, $40$, $44$ and $48$ for $\varepsilon = 0.5$ to $8$), and repeat it more briefly under setting I, with the same $\kappa$.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item For each $n \in \{500, 1{,}000, \dots, 128{,}000\}$ (doubling) and each $\varepsilon$ we draw five datasets.
  \item For each dataset we draw four stage-1 releases, each of which fixes $(\lambda, \Psi)$.
  \item For each of these we draw $10{,}000$ antithetic pairs $(\bE, \bff)$, $(-\bE, -\bff)$ of stage-2 releases.
\end{itemize}
The comparison against the true coefficients uses the cohort-level runs of Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim}.

\begin{table}[t]
  \centering
  \small
  \caption{Mechanism bias and the optional correction~\eqref{eq:bc} under v3.1 at the previous setting, $\delta = 10^{-9}$, $R^2 = 0.5$; 45 cells ($n$, $\varepsilon$), binned by $\vartheta_0 = \lambda_{\min}(\bG)/(2\sigma_2\sqrt p)$. Bias share and largest standardized bias: maxima over the cells of a bin. MSE ratio: corrected over uncorrected. $K$: smallest number of averaged releases of the same data at which the squared bias equals the variance of the average.}
  \label{tab:bias_v31}
  \setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
  \begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccccr@{}}
    \toprule
    & & & bias$^2$/MSE & $\max_j |b_j|/\mathrm{sd}_j$ & \multicolumn{2}{c}{MSE ratio against} & & \\
    \cmidrule(lr){6-7}
    $\vartheta_0$ & cells & ridge on & (uncorrected) & (uncorrected) & $\btheta_\lambda$ & $\hat\bbeta$ & & $K$ \\
    \midrule
    0--1 & 15 & 15 & 0.001\% & 0.009 & 0.9951--0.9954 & 1.0000--1.0005 & & 81{,}616 \\
    1--2.5 & 6 & 6 & 0.012\% & 0.024 & 0.9952--0.9954 & 1.0009--1.0021 & & 9{,}069 \\
    2.5--7 & 9 & 9 & 0.048\% & 0.044 & 0.9945--0.9952 & 1.0025--1.0109 & & 2{,}118 \\
    7--15 & 5 & 0 & 0.048\% & 0.037 & 0.9960--0.9972 & 0.9960--0.9972 & & 2{,}103 \\
    $\ge 15$ & 10 & 0 & 0.011\% & 0.018 & 0.9990--1.0000 & 0.9990--1.0000 & & 8{,}782 \\
    \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
\end{table}

No, not even as a variance reduction (Table~\ref{tab:bias_v31} and Figure~\ref{fig:bias_v31} for the previous setting; setting I below).
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{The bias is negligible.} The squared mechanism bias of Theorem~\ref{thm:ridge}, averaged over a cell's datasets and stage-1 releases, is at most $0.048\%$ of the mean squared error around $\btheta_\lambda$ in any of the 45 cells ($0.071\%$ for a single dataset and stage-1 release). The largest coefficient bias is at most $0.044$ of its standard deviation. The earlier mechanism gave $0.75\%$ and $0.14$. The reason is the stage-1 rule: while the ridge is on it holds $\vartheta_\lambda$ at $7.9$--$9.2$, well above $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$, because of the radius $r_1$; in these cells the ridge is off only from $\vartheta_0 = 9.6$. The bias peaks around the switch-off and decays like $1/\vartheta_0^2$ after it.
  \item \emph{The theorem holds.} Wherever $\vartheta_0 \ge 1$, the simulated bias is $0.997$--$1.010$ times the leading term $\sigma_2^2 M(\bP_\lambda)\btheta_\lambda$.
  \item \emph{Against $\btheta_\lambda$} the correction lowers the mean squared error by at most $0.55\%$: by about $0.5\%$ with the ridge on and by $0.4\%$ or less once it is off. At most $0.05$ percentage points of this come from removing bias; the rest is a variance reduction. The earlier mechanism gave $7$--$12\%$ with the ridge on, when $\vartheta_\lambda$ was near $2$.
  \item \emph{Against the sample least-squares estimate}, with the ridge on, the correction raises the mean squared error by up to $1.1\%$. Deterministic shrinkage (Proposition~\ref{prop:shrink}) is $92$--$99.5\%$ of that error, and the correction leaves it alone by design.
  \item \emph{Against the true coefficients} it changes the mean squared error by $-0.4\%$ to $+1.7\%$. The largest increase is with a moderate ridge just below the switch-off ($n = 75$k at $\varepsilon = 0.5$).
  \item \emph{Averaging.} The bias would matter only if at least about $2{,}100$ releases of the same data were averaged. The earlier mechanism gave $130$.
  \item \emph{Real data.} On the 29 datasets of Section~\ref{sec:eval:adassp} the correction changed the test MSE of v3.1 at the previous setting by $-0.01\%$ to $+0.39\%$, against $-3.7\%$ to $+2.3\%$ under the earlier mechanism.
\end{itemize}
\paragraph{Setting I.} The same design with $5{,}000$ instead of $10{,}000$ antithetic pairs per $(\text{dataset}, \lambda)$ gives the same answer, with a larger but still negligible bias.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item The squared mechanism bias, averaged over a cell's datasets and stage-1 releases, is at most $0.19\%$ of the mean squared error around $\btheta_\lambda$ in any of the 45 cells ($0.048\%$ at the previous setting). The largest share for a single dataset and stage-1 release in any cell is $0.24\%$ ($0.071\%$). The largest coefficient bias is $0.078$ of its standard deviation ($0.044$). The bias peaks around the switch-off, where $\vartheta_0 \approx 4.4$--$5.0$.
  \item The bias is larger because setting I holds $\vartheta_\lambda$ at $4.6$--$5.3$ while the ridge is on, against $7.9$--$9.2$ at the previous setting; the leading term scales like $1/\vartheta_\lambda^2$.
  \item Against $\btheta_\lambda$ the correction lowers the mean squared error by $1.4$--$1.7\%$ with the ridge on and by at most $1.4\%$ once it is off (previous setting: about $0.5\%$).
  \item Theorem~\ref{thm:ridge} holds: wherever $\vartheta_0 \ge 1$, the simulated bias is $0.994$--$1.017$ times the leading term.
  \item The bias would matter only if at least about $490$ releases of the same data were averaged ($2{,}100$ at the previous setting).
  \item The gate refused no release.
\end{itemize}
Leaving the correction off therefore costs nothing that matters under either setting.

\begin{figure}[t]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/bias_mse_ratio_v31.png}
  \caption{The optional correction under v3.1 at the previous setting, $\delta = 10^{-9}$, $R^2 = 0.5$, against $\vartheta_0 = \lambda_{\min}(\bG)/(2\sigma_2\sqrt{p})$, one line per $\varepsilon$; hollow markers: the stage-1 ridge is on in most releases. (a)~Squared mechanism bias as a share of the mean squared error around $\btheta_\lambda$ (uncorrected estimate). (b)~Mean squared error of the corrected estimate divided by that of the uncorrected one, against $\btheta_\lambda$. (c)~The same against the true coefficients, from the cohort-level runs of Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim}. Values below 1 favour the correction.}
  \label{fig:bias_v31}
\end{figure}

% ---------------------------------------------------------------- 12.7
\subsection{Benchmark against AdaSSP}
\label{sec:eval:adassp}

AdaSSP~\cite{wang2018revisiting} perturbs the same sufficient statistics and solves a ridge whose penalty adapts to a private estimate of $\lambda_{\min}(\bX^{\top}\bX)$. It assumes one curator holding the joined table, which our setting rules out. The comparison therefore measures the utility cost of never pooling the data.

\paragraph{Setup.} We use the 29 UCI datasets~\cite{dua2019uci} of Wang's published evaluation, with 10-fold cross-validation on the folds of the earlier comparison. This subsection uses $\delta = 10^{-6}$, as Wang's published evaluation does. There are 50 noise draws per fold and $\varepsilon \in \{0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8\}$. The v3.1 arms use the fixed $\kappa = 40$, $40$, $40$, $44$ and $48$; the rule~\eqref{eq:kappa} would give $40$ at every $\varepsilon$ at this $\delta$ and change $\sigma_1$ and $\sigma_2$ by less than $0.02\%$.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{Rows.} In each fold, $10\%$ of all rows, taken from the training folds, form a public reference set. Only the per-party preprocessing uses it, to fix its constants and bounds. Every arm trains on the remaining training rows ($80\%$ of the data) and is tested on the held-out fold.
  \item \emph{Split.} $\Rp$ holds the first $\lfloor d/2 \rfloor$ features and $\Op$ the rest and $y$. Every training row counts as matched.
\end{itemize}
We compare seven arms.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item[(a)] \emph{AdaSSP (published):} Wang's algorithm and noise constants, with his preprocessing: z-scored features, each whole row scaled to unit norm, $y/\max|y|$. Its guarantee covers the whole record $(\bx, y)$, not only $\Op$'s part, so it is nominally stronger than that of v3.1. Its published constants, however, are calibrated for add/remove adjacency, and under our replace-one adjacency they give an $\varepsilon$ up to $28\%$ above the nominal one (end of this subsection).
  \item[(b)] \emph{AdaSSP (matched):} AdaSSP's adaptive ridge under our adjacency and our accountant. $\bA$ is exact; $\rho_{\mathrm{run}}/3$ goes to $\lambda_{\min}$ and $2\rho_{\mathrm{run}}/3$ to one joint release of the $\Op$-dependent entries with $\Delta = \sqrt{10}$, with the tight conversion. It is idealized, because its private $\lambda_{\min}$ step needs $\bX^{\top}\bX$ in the clear.
  \item[(c)] \emph{v3.1 with joint row normalization, under settings I and P:} v3.1 on Wang's preprocessed data, without an intercept, with $B_R = B_O = B_y = 1$. It is idealized too. Wang's normalization scales each row $(\bx_R, \bx_O)$ by its joint norm, which needs both parties' features in one row, and no party has them in the vertical setting. The arm isolates the change of mechanism from the change of preprocessing.
  \item[(d)] \emph{v3.1 with block unit norm, under settings I and P:} each party normalizes its own block of every row (Section~\ref{sec:protocol:preproc}), so $B_R^2 = 2$ with $\Rp$'s intercept and $B_O = 1$. $B_y$ is the $99.5\%$ quantile on the reference set.
  \item[(e)] \emph{v3.1 with standardization, under setting I:} each party standardizes its own columns with reference-set constants; the bounds are the $99.5\%$ quantiles on the reference set.
\end{itemize}
The previous setting's arms (c)--(e) are taken from their earlier run on the same folds and matched rows, and are not rerun. AdaSSP was rerun with new noise draws, which changed its test MSE by a median of $0.0\%$ (at most $4.7\%$ on any dataset). The v3.1 arms run both releases, the stage-1 rule, the gate and the solve, with the bias correction off. A refused release outputs $\btheta = 0$. Settings I, P and P$_0$ of one preprocessing share their unit noise draws. P was run after the other two, on the same draws; rerunning I on them reproduced its test MSE to within $10^{-14}$ (relative). Arms with different preprocessing have different non-private baselines. Each arm is therefore reported in two ways:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item as a ratio of its test MSE to that of its own non-private fit, $(\bZ^\top\bZ + \bI)^{-1}\bZ^\top\by$ (Wang's non-private estimator), on the same matched rows with the same preprocessing; arms (a)--(c) share this fit;
  \item as an absolute test MSE on one common scale, Wang's $y/\max|y|$, which adds the cost of the preprocessing.
\end{itemize}
Methods are compared by paired tests over folds (95\% CI of the mean difference of absolute test MSE, $t$ with 9 degrees of freedom).

Section~\ref{sec:eval:baselines} adds two more baselines on the same folds and rows: vertical full-record SSP, which noises $\Rp$'s block too, and TukeyEM, which needs no bounds.

\begin{table}[t]
  \centering
  \small
  \caption{Test MSE at $\varepsilon = 1$, $\delta = 10^{-6}$, as a ratio to each arm's own non-private fit (Table~\ref{tab:adassp_abs_v31} gives the non-private test MSE). Last column: block unit norm at the previous setting, from its earlier run.}
  \label{tab:adassp_v31}
  \setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}

\paragraph{Results.} Tables~\ref{tab:adassp_v31}--\ref{tab:verdicts_v31} and Figure~\ref{fig:adassp_v31} separate the mechanism, the setting and the preprocessing.
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Setting I narrows the gap, but not the count.} Arm (c) has the same preprocessing as AdaSSP. Under setting I, at $\varepsilon = 1$, it has significantly higher test MSE than AdaSSP (published) on 23 of 29 datasets and lower on 5, with a median gap of $+19\%$; at the previous setting the counts were 20 and 6 and the gap $+28\%$. Against AdaSSP (matched), which has our guarantee, it is higher on 18 and lower on 6, with a median gap of $+12\%$ ($18$ and $8$, $+17\%$ at the previous setting). At $\varepsilon = 8$ the median gaps are $+11\%$ against (a) and $+6\%$ against (b). The ridge is still the cause. Setting I's median ridge is $2.8$ times that of AdaSSP (matched) (range $2.4$--$3.9$ over 26 datasets) and $4.1$ times that of AdaSSP (published), against $4.2$ times AdaSSP (matched) at the previous setting; for example $536$, $188$ and $127$ on housing and $372$, $150$ and $101$ on protein ($786$ and $559$ before). Setting I still certifies $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$ at confidence $1 - \alpha_L$ from a quarter of the budget.
  \item \emph{Setting P narrows the gap in the implementable arm.} Under block normalization, (d) under setting P has higher test MSE than AdaSSP (published) on 20 of 29 datasets at $\varepsilon = 1$ and lower on 5, the same counts as setting I, but the median gap is $+14\%$ instead of $+25\%$. At $\varepsilon = 8$ it is higher on 15 and lower on 3 (bike, kin40k and pol), with a median gap of $+4.3\%$ ($+10.7\%$ under I). Against AdaSSP (matched) the median gap is $+6.3\%$ at $\varepsilon = 1$ and $+1.4\%$ at $\varepsilon = 8$. Setting P has lower test MSE than setting I on 18--24 datasets and higher on at most 2 at every $\varepsilon$, by a median of $3$--$7\%$. Its ridge is $0.6$ times that of I. On protein the gap to AdaSSP (published) is $2.0\%$ ($0.122$ against $0.120$), and on bike it is $13\%$. Its gate refused $0.2\%$ of releases on average over the 29 datasets and all $\varepsilon$, more than $1\%$ on at most 3 datasets at any $\varepsilon$, and at most $7.2\%$ (solar at $\varepsilon = 1$, where $\sigma_1 = 0.25\,\sigma_2$).
  \item \emph{Setting P under joint normalization.} Arm (c) under P has a median gap of $+4.9\%$ against AdaSSP (matched) at $\varepsilon = 1$ (lower test MSE on 6 datasets, higher on 18) and $+2.6\%$ at $\varepsilon = 8$ (1 and 18). Against AdaSSP (published) the gaps are $+8.8\%$ and $+7.8\%$. Its ridge is $1.6$--$1.9$ times that of AdaSSP (matched), against $2.6$--$2.9$ times under I, and it beats (c) under I on 18--21 datasets at every $\varepsilon$. Its gate refused $0.03\%$ of releases on average, and at most $2.4\%$ (servo, $\varepsilon = 8$).
  \item \emph{The first choice P$_0$ failed under block normalization.} P$_0$ ($\vartheta^\star = 0.5$, $\alpha_L = 0.5$, $f_1 = 0.2$) matched AdaSSP (matched) to within about $1\%$ under joint normalization (a median gap of $+0.2\%$ to $+1.1\%$ at every $\varepsilon$, with a ridge $1.1$--$1.4$ times AdaSSP's). Under block normalization, however, its gate refused $65\%$ of releases on average over the 29 datasets and all $\varepsilon$: at least a quarter on 25 of them, and all of them on elevators, gas and solar. A refused release outputs $\btheta = 0$, so (d) under P$_0$ was worse than AdaSSP (published) by a median of $+36\%$ at $\varepsilon = 1$ and $+81\%$ at $\varepsilon = 8$. Under joint normalization setting P is worse than P$_0$ on 18--20 datasets, by a median of $0.3$--$2.9\%$; that is the price of a gate that passes under block normalization, where P beats P$_0$ on 18--20 datasets. Section~\ref{sec:eval:sweep} explains why block normalization made P$_0$'s gate fail.
  \item \emph{The preprocessing.} Under setting I, block normalization (d) and joint normalization (c) differ significantly on 17 of 29 datasets in absolute test MSE ($\varepsilon = 1$; 5 lower, 12 higher), and their ratios to their own non-private fits are similar. Their differences come mostly from the non-private fits: on elevators, block normalization's non-private test MSE is $2.6$ times that of Wang's preprocessing, while on concrete it is $0.86$ times. Plain standardization (e) is worse than block normalization on 18--25 datasets, because heavy tails inflate the bounds.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{AdaSSP's published noise constants.} AdaSSP adds noise of s.d.\ $s = \sqrt{\log(6/\delta)}/(\varepsilon/3)$ to each of three statistics: $\lambda_{\min}$, $\bX^\top\bX$ (symmetrized, so off-diagonal s.d.\ $s/\sqrt2$) and $\bX^\top\by$.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item Under add/remove adjacency each of the three has sensitivity $1$ for unit-norm rows. The three releases then cost $\rho = 3/(2s^2)$, which the tight conversion turns into $(0.31, 0.64, 1.35, 2.87, 6.24)$-DP at $\delta = 10^{-6}$ for nominal $\varepsilon = (0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8)$, which is valid, with room.
  \item Under replace-one adjacency, which is ours, the sensitivities of $\bX^\top\bX$ (the Frobenius norm of $\bx\bx^\top - \bx'\bx'^\top$, which matches the symmetrized noise) and of $\bX^\top\by$ are $\sqrt2$ and $2$. The same constants then give $(0.48, 1.01, 2.14, 4.60, 10.2)$-DP: above the nominal $\varepsilon$ for $\varepsilon \ge 1$, by up to $28\%$ (at $\varepsilon = 8$).
  \item Recalibrated to exactly $(\varepsilon, \delta)$ under replace-one adjacency, with the tight conversion and an equal split, AdaSSP's test MSE rises by a median $2$--$3\%$ (range $-5\%$ to $+28\%$).
\end{itemize}
The published column therefore overstates AdaSSP's advantage only slightly, and its stronger guarantee holds only nominally: it protects the whole record, but at our adjacency it exceeds its nominal $\varepsilon$ for $\varepsilon \ge 1$. The gap to v3.1 lies in the ridge rule.

\tocheck{AdaSSP's adjacency: the published constants are consistent with add/remove adjacency (sensitivity 1 for each statistic); confirm against the statement in Wang (2018) before citing it.}

\subsection{Two further baselines: full-record SSP and TukeyEM}
\label{sec:eval:baselines}

Two more baselines run on the folds, reference rows and matched rows of Section~\ref{sec:eval:adassp}, at $\delta = 10^{-6}$ with 50 noise draws per fold, $\varepsilon \in \{0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8\}$ and the same fixed $\kappa$, and are reported in the same two ways. They answer two questions that AdaSSP does not: what keeping $\Rp$'s block exact buys, and what the clipping bounds and the per-party preprocessing cost. They differ in what they need.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{Full-record SSP} could run in the vertical setting: it needs a secure aggregation of the whole Gram matrix, as in the protocols below, and output noise. With block normalization it is implementable; with joint normalization it is idealized, like arm (c).
  \item \emph{TukeyEM} needs the joined table: each of its least-squares fits uses whole rows. It is an idealized central baseline, like AdaSSP.
  \item The FWS intervals of Section~\ref{sec:eval:fws} post-process our own release, so they could run in the vertical setting.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Full-record SSP.} Karr et al.~\cite{karr2009privacy}, Hall et al.~\cite{hall2011secure} and Gasc\'on et al.~\cite{gascon2017privacy} compute the whole Gram matrix securely, $\bA$ included. Adding noise to every released entry gives the natural competitor that combines secure regression with output privacy. We keep everything else of v3.1: the two releases with the split $f_1$, the tight zCDP conversion with the same slack, the stage-1 rule, the gate, and settings I and P. The releases now also contain $\triu\bA$ (every entry but the constant $n$), and the adjacency replaces a whole record $(\bx_R, \bx_O, y)$. Two things change. The sensitivity is that of Proposition~\ref{prop:sens_full}. The exact radius is the quantile of $\lambda_{\max}$ of a unit noise matrix without the zero block on $\bA$, computed by the rule of Section~\ref{sec:stats:stage1}: at $p = 11$, $q_{0.9975} = 7.87$ instead of $7.42$ (setting I) and $q_{0.25} = 4.87$ instead of $4.44$ (setting P). Each full-record release adds noise on $\bA$, from a separate stream, to the same unit noise on the $\Op$-dependent entries as the v3.1 release it is compared with, so the comparison is paired draw for draw. The v3.1 arms recomputed on these streams reproduce the published fold values to $10^{-14}$. The question is therefore only what the exact $\bA$ buys.

\begin{proposition}[Sensitivity of the full-record release]
\label{prop:sens_full}
Let a record be $(\bxd, \bx_O, y)$ with $\bxd = (1, \mathbf u)$, $\|\bxd\| \le B_R$, $\|\bx_O\| \le B_O$ and $|y| \le B_y$, and let the release be the upper triangle of $\sum_i \mathbf w_i\mathbf w_i^\top$ with $\mathbf w_i = (\bxd_i, \bx_{O,i}, y_i)$, without its constant entry $n$, every entry with the same noise. For the blocks $k \in \{\mathbf u, \bx_O, y\}$ of a record and of its replacement write $n_k$ and $n'_k$ for their squared norms and $g_k$ for their inner product. Then the replace-one $\ell_2$ sensitivity is
\begin{equation}
  \Delta_{\mathrm{full}}^2 = \sup\Bigl[\sum_k (n_k + n'_k - 2g_k) + \sum_k (n_k^2 + n_k'^2 - 2g_k^2) + \sum_{k < l} (n_k n_l + n'_k n'_l - 2g_k g_l)\Bigr],
  \label{eq:sens_full}
\end{equation}
over the norms allowed by the bounds and $|g_k| \le \sqrt{n_k n'_k}$, with $|g_k| = \sqrt{n_k n'_k}$ for a block of dimension one ($y$ among them). For a block of dimension at least two the supremum is attained with both norms at the bound. Without an intercept the first sum is dropped; without $\by^\top\by$ (separate mode) the term $(n_y - n'_y)^2$ is dropped; for stage~1 every term with $y$ is dropped.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
The first sum is the change of the first moments (the intercept row), the others that of $\triu(\mathbf w\mathbf w^\top - \mathbf w'\mathbf w'^\top)$ block by block. For each diagonal block $\|\triu \bS\|^2 \le \|\bS\|_F^2$, which gives the bound. In two coordinates, for any norms and inner product, the two vectors can be chosen so that $\mathbf x\mathbf x^\top - \mathbf x'\mathbf x'^\top$ is diagonal, so the bound is attained for blocks of dimension at least two. The bracket is a concave quadratic in the $g_k$, every other coefficient is nonnegative, and the admissible set of $g_k$ grows with the norms, so its maximum is nondecreasing in $n_k$ and $n'_k$. For blocks of dimension at least two what remains is a maximization over $t = yy' \in [-B_y^2, B_y^2]$ of an explicit piecewise quartic, attained at $t = \pm B_y^2$, at a breakpoint of the pieces or at a real root of a cubic, so the supremum has a closed form computed by exact enumeration, as for Proposition~\ref{prop:sens} (proofs, Part~B, Theorem~S10c); for a block of dimension one that value is a valid, conservative bound. The values below use the earlier numerical maximization (a dense grid refined by local search, rounded up by a relative $10^{-12}$), which agrees with the enumeration to $10^{-12}$ over $19$ bound vectors. With $\Rp$'s row held fixed the same computation reproduces Proposition~\ref{prop:sens} to $10^{-12}$, and a direct maximization over vectors with $d_R, d_O \in \{1, 2, 3\}$ from many starting points never exceeds~\eqref{eq:sens_full} and attains it.
\end{proof}

Without an intercept and at unit bounds (arm (c), $d_R, d_O \ge 2$), $\Delta_{\mathrm{full}}^2 = 32/3$ (at $\cos\angle(\mathbf u, \mathbf u') = \cos\angle(\bx_O, \bx'_O) = 1/3$ and $y' = -y$) against $\Delta^2 = 10$, and $\Delta_{G,\mathrm{full}}^2 = 6$ against $\Delta_G^2 = 4.5$. On the simulation population (standardized, $B_R = 5.59$ with the intercept, $B_O = 6.24$, $B_y = 2.89$), $\Delta$ rises from $86.2$ to $92.7$ ($+7.5\%$) and $\Delta_G$ from $77.2$ to $85.8$ ($+11\%$). Under block normalization the sensitivity of the stage-2 release without $\by^\top\by$, which sets $\sigma_2$ there because $\by^\top\by$ is released separately, does not change at all ($10.00$ at $B_y = 2.89$), and $\Delta_G$ rises from $2.83$ to $3.27$ ($+15\%$). On the 29 datasets (fold 0, $\varepsilon = 1$), $\sigma_1$ rises by $12$--$16\%$ and $\sigma_2$ by $0$--$7\%$ under block normalization, and by $0$--$16\%$ and $0$--$3\%$ under joint normalization.
