\paragraph{Results for full-record SSP.}
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Synthetic population, standardized} (Table~\ref{tab:full_sim}, Figure~\ref{fig:full_sim}). Noising $\bA$ moves every threshold up. The ridge switches off $12\%$ later under setting I and $13\%$ later under P ($30.2$k against $27.0$k records at $\varepsilon = 1$ under I). Prediction within $10\%$ of non-private needs $7$--$16\%$ more records ($22.1$k against $20.1$k under I and $15.8$k against $13.7$k under P at $\varepsilon = 1$). Twice the OLS floor on the slopes needs $17$--$18\%$ more ($31.6$k against $26.6$k at $\varepsilon = 4$, $9.4$k against $8.0$k at $\varepsilon = 8$), and at $\varepsilon = 2$ it is not reached by $10^5$ records ($88$k for v3.1). Once the ridge is off the slopes stay less accurate: at $n = 10^5$ their median RMSE is $3.54$ against $3.20$ times the floor at $\varepsilon = 1$, and $1.40$ against $1.31$ at $\varepsilon = 4$.
  \item \emph{Synthetic population, block-normalized.} Here $\sigma_2$ is unchanged and only stage~1 and the noise on $\bA$ cost. Prediction within $10\%$ needs $3$--$7\%$ more records.
  \item \emph{The 29 datasets} (Table~\ref{tab:full_verdicts}, Figure~\ref{fig:full_real}). Full-record SSP has significantly higher test MSE than v3.1 on most datasets and lower on few, but the gap is small. Under block normalization and setting I it is higher on $21$--$24$ of $29$ datasets and lower on at most $2$, at every $\varepsilon$, with a median gap of $+0.4\%$ to $+1.0\%$; under setting P, higher on $19$--$23$ and lower on at most $2$, by $+0.6\%$ to $+1.2\%$. The largest gaps are on energy and sml ($+5.1\%$ and $+4.1\%$ under I at $\varepsilon = 1$, $+7.3\%$ and $+6.3\%$ under P). Under joint normalization the median gap is $+1.0\%$ to $+1.7\%$; full-record SSP is lower on $7$ datasets at $\varepsilon = 0.5$ and $5$ at $\varepsilon = 1$ under setting I. Six of the seven have little linear signal (non-private test MSE within $25\%$ of that of predicting the mean: fertility, forest, pendulum, pumadyn32nm, solar and tamielectric), where its larger ridge shrinks a noisy estimate further; the seventh is concreteslump ($103$ rows). Its median ridge is $8$--$10\%$ larger than v3.1's under block normalization and $14$--$17\%$ under joint normalization. Its gate refused $0.14\%$ of releases on average under block normalization and setting P at $\varepsilon = 1$ (at most $2.4\%$, solar).
  \item \emph{Against AdaSSP} the verdicts do not change: under block normalization and setting I full-record SSP is worse than AdaSSP (published) on $20$ of $29$ datasets at $\varepsilon = 1$ with a median gap of $+26\%$, against $+25\%$ for v3.1.
  \item \emph{Cryptographic cost} (measured; single thread, $d_R = 4$, $d_O = 6$, $n_R^{\max} = 2{,}000$). The releases also hold $\triu\bA$ without $n$: $(d_R + 1)(d_R + 2)/2 - 1 = 14$ more entries. The four sums $\sum_t b_t x_{R,j,t}$ come from $\Rp$'s existing uploads. The ten products need ten more encrypted upload vectors $b \cdot x_j x_k$ from $\Rp$, on top of the six of v3.1. We implemented this extra work as a benchmark path of the implementation, not as a protocol mode: $\Rp$ encrypts and $\Op$ converts the ten vectors, $\Op$ aggregates all $77$ entries under encryption (the new ones against a vector of ones), and each packed release is made and decoded; a noiseless test decryption returns $\triu\bA$ to $10^{-11}$. At $16$k and $64$k register rows, $\Op$'s aggregation grows by a factor $1.21$ and $1.15$ ($1.5$\,s more than v3.1's $7.4$ and $10.2$\,s; the cost model's estimate was $77/63 = 1.22$), and a whole run by $1.08$ and $1.09$ over a measured v3.1 run of $21$ and $33$\,s (estimate: $1.16$). The upload grows by $10$ and $40$\,MB, exactly as estimated, which multiplies the traffic by $1.15$ and $1.46$ (estimate: $1.19$ and $1.55$, against a v3.1 baseline with one felt per PSI item). The releases cost the same, since each is one ciphertext whose noise is sampled per ring coefficient. With the larger entry counts, Calibrate raises $\log_2 B_{\mathrm{tot}}$ by $0.15$--$0.18$, with the same $\kappa$ and $\sigma$.
\end{itemize}
Keeping $\Rp$'s block exact therefore buys about $1\%$ of test MSE on real data with per-party preprocessing, consistently but little, and $7$--$18\%$ of the records needed on standardized synthetic data, where $\Rp$'s bound is large ($B_R = 5.59$). The gain grows with $\Rp$'s share of the sensitivity: under block normalization $B_y$ dominates $\Delta$, so the exact $\bA$ saves only the noise on $\bA$ itself and part of $\Delta_G$. Its other benefits are that no budget and no circuit work are spent on statistics that $\Rp$ already holds, and that $\Rp$ can use the exact $\bA$ for diagnostics that cost no budget (Section~\ref{sec:stats:noff}).

\paragraph{TukeyEM.} Amin et al.~\cite{amin2023easy} split the rows at random into $m$ disjoint groups, fit least squares on each, and release a point of high approximate Tukey depth among the $m$ fits. A propose-test-release (PTR) step first checks, with half the budget, that the regions of high depth are stable; if the check fails, nothing is released, which we score as $\btheta = 0$, as a refused release of v3.1. The exponential mechanism on depth uses the other half. A record changes one fit, so the guarantee is $(\varepsilon, \delta)$-DP for a change of one whole record, at the nominal $(\varepsilon, \delta) = (\varepsilon, 10^{-6})$ and with no zCDP conversion. TukeyEM needs no bound on the features or the labels, so it runs on the z-scored features (reference-set constants, with an intercept column) and the standardized $y$, without clipping; its own non-private fit is the ridge fit on that design. We reimplemented it from the reference code (\texttt{dp\_regression/tukey.py} of \texttt{github.com/google-research/google-research}, commit \texttt{ba728e2}). Replaying the reference code's random numbers, the two give the same fits, PTR distances and outputs in 60 of 60 trials. We changed three details of the reference code:
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item the Laplace noise of the PTR test has scale $1/(\varepsilon/2)$, which its threshold $\ln(1/(2\delta))/(\varepsilon/2)$ requires, instead of $\varepsilon/2$;
  \item the released point is drawn from the depth level that the exponential mechanism chose, not from the next deeper one;
  \item an empty set of depths in the PTR check counts as passing instead of raising an error.
\end{itemize}
On the reference code's own example ($n = 22{,}000$, $d = 10$) the first two leave the pass rates unchanged and raise the median error of the released model by $0.1$--$5\%$. The number of groups trades the PTR test against the accuracy of each fit. We fixed it in advance to groups of $2p$ rows, $m = \lfloor n/(2p) \rfloor$, close to the reference example ($m = 1{,}000$ to $2{,}000$ at $n = 22{,}000$, $p = 11$). We also ran $m = \lfloor n/(kp) \rfloor$ for $k \in \{1.5, 3, 5, 10, 25\}$; choosing the best $k$ per dataset and $\varepsilon$ on the test folds, an optimistic oracle, changes the counts against v3.1 below by at most one dataset.

\paragraph{Where TukeyEM fails.} The PTR distance is a lower bound on the number of fits that would have to change to make the release unsafe. It cannot exceed $t - 1$ with $t = \lfloor\lfloor m/2\rfloor/2\rfloor$, and the test passes only if the distance plus Laplace noise exceeds $\ln(1/(2\delta))/(\varepsilon/2)$, which is $26.2$ at $\varepsilon = 1$ and $3.3$ at $\varepsilon = 8$ for $\delta = 10^{-6}$. So TukeyEM needs about $m \ge 110$ groups at $\varepsilon = 1$ and $m \ge 20$ at $\varepsilon = 8$, that is $n \ge 220p$ and $n \ge 40p$ matched rows with groups of $2p$. With our split, $21$ of the $29$ datasets have $m < 110$ and $12$ have $m < 20$. Enough groups are necessary but not sufficient: the regions of high depth are stable only if the fits agree, which fits on $2p$ rows rarely do. Table~\ref{tab:tukey} and Figure~\ref{fig:tukey} give the results.
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{The test fails on every dataset with fewer than about $3{,}500$ rows per coefficient, unless $\varepsilon$ is large.} At $\varepsilon \le 2$ it passes in every run on kin40k, protein, tamielectric and 3droad ($n/p \ge 3{,}556$, $m \ge 1{,}777$) and in no run on the other $25$ datasets. There its distance bound takes its smallest value, $-1$, even where $m$ would allow more (pol, elevators and bike, $m = 222$--$386$). At $\varepsilon = 8$ it also passes on pol, elevators, bike and airfoil ($98\%$), and fails on the other 21 datasets in every run.
  \item \emph{Where it passes, it is accurate on the large datasets.} Its test MSE is within $0.4\%$ of its own non-private fit on kin40k, tamielectric and 3droad, and $12\%$ ($\varepsilon = 1$) and $8\%$ ($\varepsilon = 8$) above it on protein. At $\varepsilon = 8$ it is $1.2\%$ above on bike, $6\%$ on airfoil and $15\%$ on elevators. On pol it passes at $\varepsilon = 8$ but releases a poor model: $5.3$ times its non-private test MSE and $7.4$ times that of v3.1. Its fits on groups of $54$ rows vary widely (pol's features are discrete), and the released model predicts worse than the mean ($0.983$ against $0.345$ on Wang's scale).
  \item \emph{Against v3.1.} TukeyEM has significantly higher absolute test MSE than v3.1 with block normalization under setting P on $25$ of $29$ datasets at $\varepsilon = 1$ and lower on $2$ (median gap $+26\%$), and higher on $24$ and lower on $3$ at $\varepsilon = 8$ ($+31\%$). Against setting I the counts are $25$ and $1$ ($+18\%$) and $24$ and $3$ ($+30\%$). It is lower on 3droad, where the unclipped z-scored fit beats block normalization ($0.02649$ against $0.02689$ non-private), on pumadyn32nm, where the test fails and predicting the mean beats any fit of these noise-only data, and at $\varepsilon = 8$ on elevators, where block normalization's own non-private fit is $2.6$ times worse than the z-scored one.
\end{itemize}
TukeyEM therefore answers the question about bounds only where it passes. There, the bounds and the clipping of v3.1 cost little: on protein, kin40k and tamielectric v3.1 is as accurate or more so. Per-party block normalization can cost more than the privacy noise (elevators), and a method without bounds avoids that cost, but at the sizes of most of Wang's datasets TukeyEM releases nothing.

\tocheck{TukeyEM: the adjacency (a change of one record changes one of the m fits; the PTR distance is a Hamming distance between the sets of fits) and the PTR noise scale 2/epsilon are taken from the reference code and the PTR threshold, because the paper of Amin et al. was not accessible when this was written. Confirm both against the paper, and the choice of m against its recommendation.}

\tocheck{Full-record SSP: the measured cost assumes that R uploads the products b x-j x-k. The U-side bound of those vectors (at most B-R squared) enters the derivation of B-tot, which was only re-evaluated with the larger entry counts, not re-derived. A secure-aggregation protocol of the kind of Karr et al. or Gascon et al. would have its own cost.}

\tocheck{Full-record SSP: the closed form of the proofs (Part B, Theorem S10c) covers blocks of dimension at least two; for tamielectric, whose block for R has dimension one, it is a conservative bound and the reported value comes from the numerical maximization. Decide whether the baseline code should switch to the enumeration.}

% ---------------------------------------------------------------- 12.8
\subsection{Intersection size on real data}
\label{sec:eval:sweep}

Figure~\ref{fig:sweep_v31} repeats the experiment of Figure~\ref{fig:sim_rmse_v31} on the four datasets with at least 15{,}000 rows and a real linear signal: bike, elevators, pol and protein. It runs v3.1 with block normalization under settings I and P, and AdaSSP (published), all at $\delta = 10^{-9}$. The previous setting is taken from its earlier run on the same folds and matched sets.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item In each fold the reference set is set aside as in Section~\ref{sec:eval:adassp}. The remaining training rows are shuffled once, and the first $n$ form the matched set, nested across $n$. The held-out fold is the test set.
  \item There are 40 noise draws per fold and $n$. The v3.1 runs of settings I and P share their unit noise draws and use the fixed $\kappa = 40$, $40$, $40$, $44$ and $48$ for $\varepsilon = 0.5$ to $8$.
  \item Each arm is compared with its own non-private fit at the same $n$.
\end{itemize}
Table~\ref{tab:sweep_v31} gives the $n$ at which each arm comes within $10\%$ of its own non-private test MSE.

\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Protein, setting I.} v3.1 comes within $10\%$ of non-private at $16$k, $8.2$k, $4.1$k, $2.1$k and $985$ matched records for $\varepsilon = 0.5$ to $8$, $18$--$24\%$ fewer than the previous setting ($20$k, $10$k, $5.2$k, $2.6$k, $1.3$k). AdaSSP needs $3.6$--$5.9$ times fewer records than setting I ($4.4$--$7.8$ times fewer than the previous setting). The earlier single-release mechanism needed $5.4$k, $2.8$k, $1.4$k and $619$ for $\varepsilon = 0.5$ to $4$.
  \item \emph{Bike, elevators and pol, setting I.} Bike now comes within $10\%$ at $\varepsilon = 4$ ($13.6$k) and $\varepsilon = 8$ ($7.3$k); the previous setting did so only at $\varepsilon = 8$ ($8.3$k). Elevators does at $8.0$k and $4.1$k for $\varepsilon = 4$ and $8$ (previous: $8.9$k and $4.7$k), relative to block normalization's own non-private fit, which is $2.8$ times worse than that on standardized features. Pol does not, at the $12$k records available: at that size its test MSE is $1.63$ and $1.22$ times non-private at $\varepsilon = 1$ and $4$ (previous: $1.74$ and $1.27$).
  \item \emph{Setting P.} Its gate refused at most $1\%$ of releases at every $n$ and $\varepsilon$ on all four datasets: at most $0.3\%$ on bike, $1.0\%$ on elevators, and none on pol and protein. P comes within $10\%$ sooner than setting I wherever either does. On protein it needs $10$k, $5.0$k, $2.5$k, $1.2$k and $599$ matched records for $\varepsilon = 0.5$ to $8$, $1.6$--$1.7$ times fewer than I. On bike it needs $8.6$k and $4.5$k for $\varepsilon = 4$ and $8$ (I: $13.6$k and $7.3$k). On elevators it needs $9.5$k, $5.1$k and $2.5$k for $\varepsilon = 2$, $4$ and $8$, where I needed $8.0$k and $4.1$k for $\varepsilon = 4$ and $8$ only. On pol it gets there at $\varepsilon = 8$ only ($10.5$k). AdaSSP still needs $2.2$--$3.6$ times fewer records than P on protein. At the largest $n$ and $\varepsilon = 1$ the median stage-1 ridge under P is $600$ on protein and $1{,}204$ on bike, against $1{,}056$ and $1{,}936$ under I.
  \item \emph{Why the first choice P$_0$ failed.} While the ridge was on, the gate refused $96$--$100\%$ of P$_0$'s releases on bike, $100\%$ on elevators and $82$--$97\%$ on protein, at every $n$ and $\varepsilon$. A refused release outputs $\btheta = 0$ and still spends the budget, so P$_0$'s test MSE stayed near that of predicting the mean: at the largest $n$ it was $3.9$, $1.6$ and $1.34$ times non-private. With $\vartheta^\star = 0.5$ the gate passes only if $r_1$ exceeds $\sigma_2\sqrt p$ by more than the extra downward shift that the stage-2 noise causes in $\lambda_{\min}$ (Section~\ref{sec:stats:stage2}). On the standardized synthetic population, where $\Delta_G \approx 0.9\Delta$, P$_0$ had $\sigma_1 = 1.79\,\sigma_2$ and $r_1 = 8.9\,\sigma_2$, far above $\sigma_2\sqrt{11} = 3.3\,\sigma_2$. Block normalization bounds $\bx$ tightly but leaves $B_y$ as large as before, so $\Delta_G/\Delta \approx 0.34$ on protein. Then $\sigma_1 \approx 0.67\,\sigma_2$ and $r_1 = q_{0.5}\sigma_1 \approx 4.53 \times 0.67\,\sigma_2 \approx 3.1\,\sigma_2$ ($p = 10$), below $\sigma_2\sqrt{10} = 3.2\,\sigma_2$ even before the stage-2 noise, the larger of the two, lowers $\lambda_{\min}$ further. P$_0$'s selection population never entered this regime. Setting P's selection included a block-normalized population with $\sigma_1 = 0.46\,\sigma_2$, and with $\vartheta^\star = 1.5$ its gate does not depend on $r_1$ to pass. On pol, where $\sigma_1 = 0.83\,\sigma_2$ under P, the gate refused at most $2\%$ of P$_0$'s releases, and there P$_0$ remains the best v3.1 arm: within $10\%$ at $12.0$k ($\varepsilon = 4$) and $6.4$k ($\varepsilon = 8$).
  \item \emph{The ridge stays on throughout.} At the largest $n$ and $\varepsilon = 1$ the median stage-1 ridge under setting I is $1{,}056$ on protein and $1{,}936$ on bike (previous setting: $1{,}334$ and $2{,}226$). Section~\ref{sec:stats:noff} puts the switch-off for protein at $555$k matched records at $\varepsilon = 1$ under setting I.
\end{itemize}

Setting P was chosen on synthetic populations whose noise ratio does not go below $\sigma_1 = 0.46\,\sigma_2$; on the 29 benchmark datasets its gate refused up to $7.2\%$ of releases, on solar ($\sigma_1 = 0.25\,\sigma_2$). Both are limitations of the setting (Section~\ref{sec:params}).

\subsection{Where the utility goes}
\label{sec:eval:lessons}

\paragraph{Prediction is not interpretation.} Test MSE includes the irreducible noise, so it is forgiving. On the simulation population, setting I brings prediction within $10\%$ of non-private at about $0.73\,n_{\mathrm{off}}$, while the ridge is still on; coefficients within twice the non-private error take $2.8$--$8.8$ times as many records (Section~\ref{sec:eval:sim}). Intervals for $\bbeta$ are informative only once the ridge is off or a public bound on $\|\bbeta\|$ is available.

\paragraph{The gap to AdaSSP is the ridge; the two settings shrink it only in part.} At the same $(\varepsilon, \delta)$ and unit bounds, the stage-2 noise of v3.1 is lower than that of AdaSSP under our guarantee: $\sigma_2 = 16.5$ at settings I and P (both $f_1 = 0.25$) and $15.1$ at the previous setting, against $17.6$, at $\varepsilon = 1$ and $\delta = 10^{-6}$. The difference lies in the ridge, which the stage-1 rule sizes to certify $\vartheta^\star$ at confidence $1 - \alpha_L$.
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{Setting I keeps the interval guarantee.} The exact radius and $f_1 = 0.25$ cut the ridge by about a third ($2.8$ instead of $4.2$ times that of AdaSSP (matched)), hold $\vartheta_\lambda$ near $5$ instead of $8$--$9$, and lower $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ by $35\%$ and the records needed for prediction within $10\%$ by a third on the simulation population; its intervals cover in every simulated regime. On real data it improves on the previous setting on 18--24 of 29 datasets, but it does not change the verdict against AdaSSP: with block normalization it is still worse on 20 of 29 at $\varepsilon = 1$, and the median gap falls only from $28\%$ to $25\%$. Once the ridge is off, its larger $\sigma_2$ costs $9.5\%$ in noise s.d.
  \item \emph{Setting P gives up the interval guarantee.} It holds the validity ratio at about $3.5$ instead of $5$ ($2.0$ on the block-normalized population), its ridge is about $0.6$ times that of setting I, and its gate still passes: it was chosen under a bound on refusals on standardized and block-normalized synthetic data, and it refused $0.2\%$ of block-normalized releases on average over the 29 datasets. With block normalization, the preprocessing a vertical deployment can use, it is the best v3.1 arm. It is better than setting I on 18--24 datasets at every $\varepsilon$, and the median gap to AdaSSP (published) falls from $+25\%$ to $+14\%$ at $\varepsilon = 1$ and from $+11\%$ to $+4\%$ at $\varepsilon = 8$. It is still worse than AdaSSP on 20 of 29 datasets at $\varepsilon = 1$. Under joint normalization its ridge is $1.8$ times that of AdaSSP (matched). The first choice P$_0$ ($\vartheta^\star = 0.5$) came within about $1\%$ of AdaSSP (matched) there, with a ridge $1.3$ times AdaSSP's, but was refused on most block-normalized releases. With $\vartheta^\star < 1$ the gate passes only when the stage-1 noise is not much smaller than the stage-2 noise ($\sigma_1 = 1.34\,\sigma_2$ for unit bounds, about $0.67\,\sigma_2$ for block-normalized protein), and block normalization makes $\Delta_G$ about a third of $\Delta$.
\end{itemize}
Per-party block normalization costs little against Wang's joint normalization, which no party can apply in the vertical setting; under setting I the two differ significantly on 17 of 29 datasets, mostly through their non-private fits.

\paragraph{What the other baselines add.} Three comparisons bound the cost of the design choices (Sections~\ref{sec:eval:fws} and~\ref{sec:eval:baselines}).
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item \emph{The exact $\bA$ is a modest accuracy gain.} Noising $\Rp$'s block as well, with the protocol otherwise unchanged, raises test MSE by a median of $0.4$--$1.7\%$ on the 29 datasets (significantly on $19$--$24$ of them, at every $\varepsilon$, setting and preprocessing), and the records needed on the standardized synthetic population by $7$--$18\%$. The exact $\bA$ also means that no budget and no circuit work are spent on statistics that $\Rp$ already holds.
  \item \emph{Dropping the bounds does not help at these sizes.} TukeyEM, on the joined table and without bounds, fails its stability test on $25$ of $29$ datasets at $\varepsilon \le 2$ and has higher test MSE than v3.1 under setting P on $24$--$25$ of them. Where it passes, it shows that block normalization can cost more than the privacy noise (elevators), and that clipping costs little (protein, kin40k).
  \item \emph{For $\bbeta$, a bootstrap without the ridge helps where ours gives no interval.} The interval of Ferrando et al.\ on our stage-2 release equals our bias-aware interval once the ridge is off, and stays finite and covers while the ridge is on, from about $\vartheta_0 = 1$, roughly a quarter of $n_{\mathrm{off}}$ on the synthetic population. Below that, only $\btheta_\lambda$ has a useful interval.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{How well the data fill their bounds matters as much as $n$.} The noise is sized for the committed bounds, so headroom the data do not use is lost signal. After scaling by $\max|y|$, the typical $|y|$ is only $0.19$ of its bound on elevators and $0.24$ on wine. Choosing bounds that typical records come close to, and units or transforms that make this possible (for example log income), is part of using the protocol well.

\paragraph{Conditioning decides when the coefficients become usable.} The ridge is off from about $(g^\star + r_1)/\ell$ matched records (Section~\ref{sec:stats:noff}). On protein a near-collinearity involving a single feature keeps it on until $555$k records with block normalization and $8.3$M without, at $\varepsilon = 1$ under setting I ($691$k and $13$M at the previous setting). Removing that feature, a decision that public reference data can inform, lowers the block-normalized threshold about sevenfold ($102$k at the previous setting).

\paragraph{Budget.} Every release, including a refused one, is charged to $\Op$'s ledger. Model search and exploratory re-runs spend the budget quickly; the protocol suits a pre-specified model per research question.

\section{Cost}
\label{sec:cost}
% ==============================================================

\centering\small

\label{tab:cost}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X l@{}}
\toprule
Step & Communication & $\Op$'s computation & Rounds \\
\midrule
Link & $n_R^{\max}$ OPRF calls; PSI query and response (sublinear in $\bar n_O$) & per-run preprocessing $O(\bar n_O)$; per-query evaluation & 2--3 \\
Upload & $(d_R + 2)\,m$ seeded ciphertexts (half size) & $(d_R + 2)\,m$ key conversions & 1 \\
Aggregate & -- & $|\mathcal E|\,m$ ciphertext--plaintext products; $|\mathcal E|\log_2 N_s$ rotations; $|\mathcal E|$ mask products & -- \\

\begin{proposition}[Work linear in the scope]
\label{prop:lower}
Consider any two-party protocol in which $\Op$ reads its register row by row and whose decrypted statistic is within $\tau_{\mathrm{dec}} < B_O^2/2$ of $\mathbf z(D)$ except with probability $p_{\mathrm{err}}$, for every input in the clipped domain. Suppose that $\Op$'s views for any two cohorts of the same size are distinguishable with advantage at most $\mathrm{adv}$. Let $n_{\mathrm{Sc}} \le \bar n_O$ be the number of non-dummy rows in scope. Then $\Op$ reads at least $(1 - 2p_{\mathrm{err}} - \mathrm{adv})\,n_{\mathrm{Sc}}$ rows in expectation. In v3.1, $\mathrm{adv}$ is the slack of G2, so $\Op$'s work is $\Omega(n_{\mathrm{Sc}})$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
The proofs (Part~A, Proposition~9.5) prove this in a cell-probe model, following the companion book on the protocol (Chapter~20 there). If $\Op$ never reads a matched row, changing that row's $\bx_O$ from $\mathbf 0$ to $B_O\mathbf e_1$ does not change $\Op$'s computation but moves $B_{11}$ by $B_O^2 > 2\tau_{\mathrm{dec}}$, so a matched row is skipped with probability at most $2p_{\mathrm{err}}$. Whether a row is skipped is a function of $\Op$'s view, so an unmatched row is skipped with probability at most $\mathrm{adv}$ more: otherwise $\Op$ could tell a cohort that matches the row from one that does not. Summing over the non-dummy rows gives the bound. $\Op$ knows its dummy rows and may skip them, so the bound is in $n_{\mathrm{Sc}}$, not $\bar n_O$; $\bar n_O$ is chosen close to $n_{\mathrm{Sc}}$.
\end{proof}

The measured runs use the chain of Section~\ref{sec:params}, with $\Omega_U = q_2$ and $\kappa = 40$; the operations, and so the timings, are the same at the evaluation settings.

\paragraph{Measured cost.} We ran the full implementation at $\bar n_O = 106{,}496$, $303{,}104$ and $1{,}007{,}616$ register rows with $n_R^{\max} = 20{,}000$ padded queries ($p = 11$, two felts per PSI item, four OpenMP threads per party and four aggregation entries in parallel at $\Op$, on a 4-core 2.8\,GHz Xeon with 15\,GB of memory), and at $10^6$ rows with $n_R^{\max} = 100{,}000$ (Section~\ref{sec:eval:large}). Table~\ref{tab:cost_large} gives the cost of one run; the communication equals the model's exact count. In these runs, at $10^6$ rows, $\Op$ spent $80$ of its $152$\,s on the OPRF evaluation of its whole register (fresh key every run), which was then single-threaded, $19$\,s on PSI binning and interpolation, $18$\,s on the CKKS aggregation, $15$\,s on the PSI evaluation and $12$\,s on \textsc{SanitizeBFV}; $369$ of the $475$\,MB of traffic is $\Rp$'s upload. With $100{,}000$ queries the PSI evaluation and sanitization grow with the ten cuckoo tables ($74$ and $62$\,s) and the traffic to $756$\,MB.

\paragraph{Parallel OPRF.} $\Op$'s OPRF evaluation now runs in parallel over the rows, with the same key and the same group operations, and with outputs bit-identical to the single-threaded evaluation. On four threads it takes $1.8$ instead of $7.8$\,s at $10^5$ rows and $17.7$ instead of $72.7$\,s at $10^6$ rows ($4.1\times$). We re-measured $\Op$'s total by full runs, not by substituting the OPRF time: one run each at $10^5$ and $10^6$ rows (setting I, $\varepsilon = 1$, $n_R^{\max} = 20{,}000$, release padding on). At $10^6$ rows $\Op$ took $91$\,s instead of $152$\,s, of which $18.6$\,s was the OPRF, and the run took $120$\,s end to end instead of $179$\,s, with the same peak memory ($2.3$\,GB) and traffic; at $10^5$ rows, $28.6$ and $36$\,s (Table~\ref{tab:cost_large}). The OPRF is now as costly as the PSI preprocessing ($18.5$\,s) and the aggregation ($16.8$\,s). Both runs linked exactly, and in each the second release reached $\Rp$ $2$\,ms after its deadline of $0.49$ and $0.51$\,s (the first also waits for the aggregation).

\begin{figure}[t]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/cost_large.png}
  \caption{Left: $\Op$'s wall time per phase at $10^5$, $3 \times 10^5$ and $10^6$ register rows ($n_R^{\max} = 20{,}000$, setting I, $\varepsilon = 1$, four threads, single-threaded OPRF), against the cost model with its published inputs (filled diamonds) and the refreshed model (open triangles). Stars: $\Op$'s total in one run with the parallel OPRF; open circles: the refreshed model for it. Right: traffic per run.}
  \label{fig:cost_large}

The cost model, built from per-operation microbenchmarks and exact operation counts, failed with the inputs published before the runs (Figure~\ref{fig:cost_large}): it overestimated $\Op$'s total by $25$--$52\%$ at every size, and single phases by up to a factor $2.8$, which fails the $25\%$ agreement we had required before running. Three causes were found. The microbenchmarks behind it ran $1.4$--$2.2$ times faster on the idle machine than in the published files. Its interpolation term assumed full partitions, whereas the fill is $0.15$ to $0.58$. Its aggregation speedup was measured with one thread per entry, not with the four threads per entry of these runs. We refreshed the model accordingly. All microbenchmarks were re-measured on the idle machine. The interpolation is split into the membership polynomials, whose degree is always $D$, and the label interpolation, whose cost is scaled by the expected sum of squared partition counts under the binomial bin loads; on four threads the model also accounts for the static schedule, which gives the fullest partitions to one thread. It now predicts the measured interpolation to within $0.88$--$0.98$ at $10^5$ to $10^6$ rows. The aggregation uses the speedup of the configuration that is run ($2.83$ for four threads $\times$ four entries, against $3.51$ for one thread per entry). Evaluated for the code of the $78$ runs, the refreshed model gives $\Op$'s total to within a factor $0.95$--$1.12$ in every run, against $0.94$--$1.25$ with the earlier re-measured inputs. Every phase of at least $2$\,s at $\Op$ lies within $25\%$ in every run, except the upload conversion at $10^6$ rows ($1.11$--$1.40$: the isolated microbenchmark misses the memory traffic of $738$\,MB of ciphertexts). On $\Rp$'s side, its OPRF takes $1.12$--$1.31$ times the model. For the runs with the parallel OPRF the model gives $1.07$ ($10^6$ rows) and $1.10$ ($10^5$ rows) of $\Op$'s total. The earlier extrapolation ($260$\,s of $\Op$'s CPU on one core and $76$\,s on four cores at $10^6$) assumed one felt and $2{,}000$ queries; with two felts and $20{,}000$ queries the refreshed model predicts $85$\,s on four cores, and $91$\,s were measured.

% ==============================================================
\section{Extensions and limitations}
\label{sec:ext}
% ==============================================================

\paragraph{A deviating researcher.} v3.1 makes no claim against a malicious $\Rp$. Making output privacy hold against one requires (i) zero-knowledge proofs at upload that the ciphertexts are well formed with short errors, that $b_t \in \{0, 1\}$, $\|\bxd_t\| \le B_R$, $(1 - b_t)\bxd_t = \mathbf 0$ and $\sum_t b_t \le n_R^{\max}$, together with a coarse norm bound that rules out wraparound (candidates include lattice-based and VOLE-based proofs); (ii) key generation with $\Rp$ committing first, plus proofs that its shares are short; and (iii) OPRF inputs bound to a certified cohort. A homomorphic check that relies on $\Rp$'s own decryption share cannot do this job.

\begin{remark}[Hiding membership]
\label{rem:modeM}
Replacing labeled PSI with circuit-PSI (for example VOLE-PSI with payloads~\cite{rindal2021vole}) gives both parties shares of $\bb$ and $\dXR$ in $\tau$-order, so $\Rp$ no longer learns $I$. The aggregation would move to BFV or use wide statistical masking, because shares modulo $2^\ell$ do not reduce inside CKKS. $\bA$ must then be computed under encryption and noised, and the adjacency extends to changing a record's match status, which raises the sensitivity.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Prediction after training (future work).}
\label{sec:inference}
After a run $\Rp$ holds $\tilde\btheta = (\tilde\btheta_R; \tilde\btheta_O)$. Predicting $y_i$ for a new individual needs $\bx_{O,i}^\top\tilde\btheta_O$, and only $\Op$ holds $\bx_{O,i}$. Split inference, in which $\Op$ evaluates $\bx_{O,i}^\top\tilde\btheta_O$ under encryption and releases it as in Algorithm~\ref{alg:release}, is not part of v3.1. Three things are unspecified: the sensitivity of a prediction release, its noise, and its charge to the ledger. Each decrypted prediction is a projection of $\bx_{O,i}$ in a direction $\Rp$ knows, so repeated queries or model versions reconstruct $\bx_{O,i}$ unless every one is charged.

What v3.1 supports is distillation to an $\Rp$-only model. The student coefficient that regresses the joint model's fitted values on $\Rp$'s features is $\hat\bbeta_R^{\mathrm{st}} = \bA^{-1}\bcR = \bbeta_R + \bA^{-1}\bC\bbeta_O$. $\Rp$ computes $\bA^{-1}\hat\bc_R$ from its exact $\bA$ and the released $\hat\bc_R$; this is post-processing, costs no budget and needs no interaction at test time. Its loss against the joint fitted values is $\bbeta_O^\top\bS\bbeta_O$, with $\bS = \bB - \bC^\top\bA^{-1}\bC$ the Schur complement of $\bA$ in $\bG$: when it is small, distillation suffices; when it is large, $\Op$'s features carry signal that $\Rp$'s cannot proxy.

\paragraph{Further limitations.}
\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
  \item The two evaluation settings were not fully re-tuned: setting I keeps $\vartheta^\star = 2.5$ and $\kappa_\Psi = 2$, and setting P keeps $\kappa_\Psi = 2$, from earlier variants of the rule, and setting I's selection criterion averages over $n$ (Section~\ref{sec:params}).
  \item Setting P is the best candidate of a grid on whose edge it lies, under a refusal bound that binds, and it was chosen after its first choice failed. Its selection covered $\sigma_1/\sigma_2 \ge 0.46$; 8 of the 29 benchmark datasets lie below, and on solar its gate refused up to $7.2\%$ of releases (Section~\ref{sec:params}).
  \item Exact-identifier linkage only.
  \item Linear models only; generalized linear models via approximate sufficient statistics are future work.
  \item The variance is model-based. A sandwich variance would need a third release of $\sum_i r_i^2\bx_i\bx_i^\top$, a ciphertext--ciphertext computation with its own sensitivity.
  \item No verifiability of $\Op$'s computation.
\end{itemize}

% ==============================================================
\section{Summary and open items}
\label{sec:summary}
% ==============================================================

Protocol v3.1 links records with an OPRF and labeled PSI, aggregates under two-party threshold CKKS, and makes each release a threshold decryption in which $\Op$'s share carries the DP noise and floods the encryption error. $\Op$ never decrypts, $\Rp$ never sees a noiseless statistic that depends on $\Op$'s data, and the budget is enforced by $\Op$'s control of its share. Two releases separate the choice of the ridge from the estimate, which makes the bias theory and the intervals valid. The protocol and its guarantees are stated for any admissible parameter vector (Section~\ref{sec:params}); \textsc{Calibrate} derives the flooding parameter, the scale and the noise from it, and the ledger composes runs, fixed or adaptive, under a per-register cap: it charges a run's full cost at admission and accounts the smudging step in hockey-stick divergence, so that across runs it no longer raises the flooding parameter. The evaluation uses two settings of the same protocol, chosen on synthetic data by a pre-registered rule. Setting I keeps the interval guarantee: with an exact Monte Carlo quantile for the stage-1 radius and $f_1 = 0.25$ it lowers the ridge threshold by $35\%$, and its intervals cover in every simulated regime. Setting P gives up the intervals for a smaller ridge. It was chosen subject to a bound on gate refusals, after a first choice failed on block-normalized real data. With block normalization it beats setting I on 18--24 of 29 datasets at every $\varepsilon$ and nearly halves the median gap to AdaSSP at $\varepsilon = 1$ ($+14\%$ against $+25\%$), but AdaSSP on the joined table still predicts better on 20 of them.

\paragraph{Remaining obligations.}
\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep, label=O\arabic*.]
  \item The worst-case error bound: proven for the circuit as written, as an explicit function of the parameters, for centred key switching ($B_{\mathrm{tot}} \approx 2^{25.3}$ at the proofs' example) and for OpenFHE's uncentred key switching in the model it defines ($2^{26.6}$ at the evaluation settings), with decoupled scales; that OpenFHE~1.5.1 realizes the model rests on reading its source, and (M5) for its sampler on a numerical check. Proofs for the restructured circuit, which is not used, are open.
  \item Smudging for the discrete Gaussian, for every $\sigma_c \ge 2^{42}$ in statistical distance and with no floor in hockey-stick divergence (proven), and a concrete constant-time exact sampler (open). Releases are now padded to a deadline fixed from public parameters, which removes the dependence of their latency on $\eta$ except in deadline overruns, whose probability is measured, not bounded.
  \item Simulation security of threshold key generation against a semi-honest $\Rp$: proven, by a reduction to plain RLWE, since $\Op$ sends only its public-key share (hybrid H4 of Theorem~\ref{thm:output}).
  \item The two-stage rule, the RSS correction and the coverage of both intervals, checked by simulation (Sections~\ref{sec:eval:coverage} and~\ref{sec:eval:sim}, the latter at setting I) and, for the Wald interval, also on fixed cohorts of five real datasets with calibrated noise (Section~\ref{sec:eval:coverage}); on real data the intervals for $\bbeta$ are checked only against a full-data reference (Section~\ref{sec:eval:fws}).
  \item PSI parameters with explicit failure probabilities (correctness only, not charged to $\delta$). The item length now meets them at $\bar n_O = 10^6$: two felts per item and a label-consistency test give $p_{\mathrm{pass}} = 2^{-67.5}$ there with $n_R^{\max} = 20{,}000$ (Appendix~\ref{app:psi}), and \textsc{Setup} checks the bound. The cuckoo-hashing failure probability is now bounded for an insertion that finds an assignment whenever one exists, which the implementation's augmenting-path fallback provides, and the worst-case bound $B_\psi$ on the evaluation noise of the PSI replies, which \textsc{SanitizeBFV} floods, is proved for the textbook BFV model; checking OpenFHE's BFVrns against that model remains.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Other open items.} Whether the rule for runs fixed in advance stays valid when requests are chosen adaptively (Proposition~\ref{prop:compose}); a proof of the subgaussianity of OpenFHE's error sampler, which is checked only numerically, and of the match between OpenFHE's key switching and the model in which the error bound is proved, which rests on reading its source; the free-set boundary for $\by^\top\by$ and a closed form for $d_O = 1$; explicit constants for the bootstrap RSS correction; a guarantee for the data-driven variance of the bias-aware interval when clipping binds (a public variance bound gives a valid interval); a bound on the downward bias of $\lambda_{\min}(\hat\bG_1)$ sharp enough to turn the switch-off factors $1.2$ and $1.4$ into bounds; and sixteen citation details flagged for verification in the proofs. The no-wraparound condition at the earlier levels, which earlier drafts listed here, is proved, and \textsc{Calibrate}, which sizes the modulus chain, is proved sound.

\appendix

% ==============================================================
\section{Mechanism bias and the optional correction}
\label{sec:bias}
% ==============================================================

Throughout this section $\sigma$ denotes the stage-2 slot s.d.\ $\sigma_2$, and $(\lambda, \Psi)$ is fixed independently of the stage-2 noise (Proposition~\ref{prop:indep}).

\subsection{Noise model}

Write the stage-2 statistics as $\hat\bG_2 = \bG + \bE$ and $\hat\bc_2 = \bc + \bff$, with
\begin{equation}
  \bE = \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{0} & \bE_{C} \\ \bE_{C}^{\top} & \bE_{B} \end{bmatrix}, \qquad
  \bff = \begin{bmatrix} \bff_{R} \\ \bff_{O} \end{bmatrix}.
  \label{eq:noise_structure}
\end{equation}
The top-left block of $\bE$ is identically zero, since $\bA$ is exact. Each independent entry on and above the diagonal of the $\Op$-dependent blocks is drawn with variance $\sigma^{2}$ and mirrored, so every entry, the diagonal included, has variance $\sigma^{2}$. With $\chi_{ij} = 0$ iff $i, j$ both lie in $\Rp$'s block and $\chi_{ij} = 1$ otherwise,
\begin{equation}
  \E[E_{ij}E_{kl}] = \sigma^{2}\chi_{ij}\bigl(\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl} + \delta_{il}\delta_{jk} - \delta_{ij}\delta_{ik}\delta_{il}\bigr).
  \label{eq:noise_cov}
\end{equation}
The last term removes the diagonal double-count of the GOE convention. $\bE$ and $\bff$ occupy disjoint entries of the release, are independent, and are symmetric about zero. (The lattice noise induces slot-domain noise with these first and second moments; the CKKS error is flooded and of lower order.)

Condition on the realized sample, so $\hat{\bbeta} = \bG^{-1}\bc$ is fixed, and set $\bP = \bG^{-1}$,
\begin{equation}
  \vartheta_0 = \frac{\lambda_{\min}(\bG)}{2\sigma\sqrt{p}}, \qquad
  \mathcal{E}_{\eta} = \bigl\{\|\bG^{-1/2}\bE\bG^{-1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le \eta\bigr\} \quad (0 < \eta < 1).
  \label{eq:event}
\end{equation}
$\mathcal{E}_{\eta}$ is invariant under $\bE \mapsto -\bE$. Define
\begin{equation}
  M(\bP) = \bigl(\bP^{2} + \tr(\bP)\bP - \bP\diag(\bP)\bigr)\PiO + \bigl(\bP\PiO\bP + \tr(\PiO\bP)\bP\bigr)\PiR,
  \label{eq:M}
\end{equation}
where $\diag(\bP)$ is the diagonal matrix formed from the diagonal of $\bP$.

\begin{remark}[The unconditional mean does not exist]
With Gaussian $\bE$, $(\bG + \bE)^{-1}(\bc + \bff)$ has no finite mean: already for $p = 1$, $\E[1/(a + e)]$ diverges. Statements about its bias must therefore be conditional, or about a truncated estimator. We condition on $\mathcal{E}_{\eta}$, whose complement has exponentially small probability.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Result}

\begin{theorem}[Leading-order conditional bias]
\label{thm:bias}
Fix $\eta \in (0,1)$ with $\eta\vartheta_0 > 1$. Then, for $\tilde\bbeta = (\bG + \bE)^{-1}(\bc + \bff)$,
\[
  \E\bigl[\tilde{\bbeta} \mid \mathcal{E}_{\eta}\bigr] - \hat{\bbeta}
  = \sigma^{2} M(\bP)\,\hat{\bbeta} + O(\sigma^{4}) + O\bigl(e^{-p(\eta\vartheta_0 - 1)^{2}/2}\bigr),
\]
where the constants are explicit: in the norm $\|\mathbf v\|_{\bG} = (\mathbf v^\top\bG\mathbf v)^{1/2}$ the remainder is at most $\Pr(\mathcal E_\eta)^{-1}\bigl[\kappa_p^4\vartheta_0^{-4}(1 - \eta)^{-1}(\|\hat\bbeta\|_{\bG} + \sigma\sqrt{\tr\bP}) + 2\sqrt2\,\kappa_p^2\vartheta_0^{-2}e^{-p(\eta\vartheta_0 - 1)^2/2}\|\hat\bbeta\|_{\bG}\bigr]$, with $\kappa_p = (2\sqrt p + 2.512 + 2\sqrt2)/(2\sqrt p)$, which is $1.81$ at $p = 11$ (proofs, Part~B, Theorem~S9). The moment-vector noise $\bff$ contributes nothing at this order; it is variance-only. The term $-\bP\diag(\bP)\PiO$ is the single-draw correction to the GOE contraction.
\end{theorem}

The proof is given in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}. Setting $\PiO \to \bI$ and $\PiR \to \mathbf{0}$ recovers the homogeneous-Gram bias, the correct check when every block is noised.

\begin{remark}[When the expansion is valid]
\label{rem:validity}
$\bP\bE$ is similar to $\bG^{-1/2}\bE\bG^{-1/2}$, so the Neumann series for $(\bI + \bP\bE)^{-1}$ converges iff $-\bG \prec \bE \prec \bG$; on $\mathcal{E}_{\eta}$ it converges geometrically with ratio $\eta$. For the largest eigenvalue, $\E\lambda_{\max}(\bE) \le 2\sigma\sqrt{p}$, and $\lambda_{\max}(\bE)$ has sub-Gaussian tails above its mean; $\lambda_{\max}(-\bE)$ has the same law. Bounding the two one-sided tails of $\|\bE\|_{\mathrm{op}} = \max\{\lambda_{\max}(\bE), \lambda_{\max}(-\bE)\}$ separately gives $\Pr(\mathcal{E}_{\eta}^{c}) \le 2\exp(-p(\eta\vartheta_0 - 1)^{2})$ for $\eta\vartheta_0 > 1$. Whether $\E\|\bE\|_{\mathrm{op}} \le 2\sigma\sqrt p$ also holds is not proved. Since $\lambda_{\min}(\bG)$ grows linearly in $n$ while $\sigma$ does not depend on $n$, the condition holds with high probability once $n$ is large enough; below that, a ridge is required.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Schur-complement reading}

Let $\bS = \bB - \bC^{\top}\bA^{-1}\bC$ be the Schur complement of $\bA$ in $\bG$. Block inversion gives $\PiO\bP\PiO = \bS^{-1}$, hence
\begin{equation}
  \tr(\PiO\bP) = \tr(\bS^{-1}), \qquad
  \tr(\bP) = \tr(\bA^{-1}) + \tr\bigl(\bA^{-1}\bC\bS^{-1}\bC^{\top}\bA^{-1}\bigr) + \tr(\bS^{-1}).
  \label{eq:traces}
\end{equation}
The two trace weights governing the leading bias are $\tr(\bG^{-1})$ for the $\Op$-coefficients and $\tr(\bS^{-1})$ for the $\Rp$-coefficients. When $\Op$'s features carry little signal beyond $\Rp$'s, $\bS$ is near-singular and the private estimator is worst-biased in $\Rp$'s own coefficients.

\subsection{Ridge}

Let $\bG_{\lambda} = \bG + \lambda\Psi$, $\bP_{\lambda} = \bG_{\lambda}^{-1}$, and let $\vartheta_{\lambda}$ and $\mathcal{E}_{\eta,\lambda}$ be as in~\eqref{eq:event} with $\bG$ replaced by $\bG_{\lambda}$.

\begin{theorem}[Ridge mechanism bias]
\label{thm:ridge}
Suppose $(\lambda, \Psi)$ is independent of $(\bE, \bff)$ and $\eta\vartheta_{\lambda} > 1$. Then
\[
  \E\bigl[\tilde{\btheta} \mid \mathcal{E}_{\eta,\lambda}\bigr] - \btheta_{\lambda}
  = \sigma^{2} M(\bP_{\lambda})\,\btheta_{\lambda} + O(\sigma^{4}) + O\bigl(e^{-p(\eta\vartheta_{\lambda} - 1)^{2}/2}\bigr),
\]
with the moment-vector noise $\bff$ again variance-only.
\end{theorem}

The proof is that of Theorem~\ref{thm:bias} under $\bG \mapsto \bG_{\lambda}$, which leaves $\bE$, its covariance~\eqref{eq:noise_cov} and $\bff$ untouched.

\begin{proposition}[Total bias against least squares: shrinkage split]
\label{prop:shrink}
If the estimand is $\hat{\bbeta} = \bG^{-1}\bc$, the total bias of the private ridge splits as
\[
  \E[\tilde{\btheta} \mid \mathcal{E}_{\eta,\lambda}] - \hat{\bbeta}
  = \underbrace{\btheta_{\lambda} - \hat{\bbeta}}_{\text{deterministic}}
  + \underbrace{\E[\tilde{\btheta} \mid \mathcal{E}_{\eta,\lambda}] - \btheta_{\lambda}}_{\text{mechanism}},
  \qquad
  \btheta_{\lambda} - \hat{\bbeta} = -\lambda\bP_{\lambda}\Psi\hat{\bbeta}.
\]
The deterministic term grows in $\lambda$. The leading mechanism term decays in $\lambda$: for $\Psi = \bI$, $\|\sigma^2 M(\bP_\lambda)\btheta_\lambda\| \le (3 + p + d_O)\,\sigma^2\|\bc\|/(\lambda_{\min}(\bG) + \lambda)^3$, which is $O(\sigma^2/\lambda^3)$; for $\Psi = \PiO$ with $\bA$ invertible it is $O(\sigma^2/\lambda)$.
\end{proposition}

For $\Psi = \bI$, $\lambda_{\min}(\bG_{\lambda}) = \lambda_{\min}(\bG) + \lambda$ grows without bound. For $\Psi = \PiO$, $\lambda_{\min}(\bG + \lambda\PiO) \to \lambda_{\min}(\bA)$ as $\lambda \to \infty$, so the $\Op$-block ridge cannot validate the expansion when $\lambda_{\min}(\bA)$ is too small, which is why the stage-1 rule falls back to $\Psi = \bI$.

\subsection{Why the ridge must not depend on the estimation noise}
\label{sec:bias:lambda}

Earlier drafts chose $(\lambda, \Psi)$ from the same noisy Gram matrix used for estimation. That choice is post-processing and costs no privacy, but it makes $\lambda$ a function of $\bE$ at first order, so Theorem~\ref{thm:ridge} no longer applies. In simulation, with $\lambda$ fixed independently the correction below removed $87$--$98\%$ of the mechanism bias, whereas under the adaptive rule it removed essentially none: the residual after correction was $1.0$--$1.6$ times the uncorrected bias. The two-stage rule of Section~\ref{sec:stats:stage1} keeps the adaptivity and restores independence.

\subsection{Optional zero-budget correction}
\label{sec:bias:correction}

Since $\sigma$, $d_{R}$, $d_{O}$ and $\PiO$ are public and $\hat\bG_2$ is already private, the bias is estimable by post-processing:
\begin{equation}
  \tilde{\btheta}_{\mathrm{bc}} = \tilde{\btheta} - \sigma^{2}M(\tilde{\bP})\,\tilde{\btheta}.
  \label{eq:bc}
\end{equation}
Substituting $\hat\bG_2$ for $\bG$ inside a term already of order $\sigma^{2}$ injects only $O(\sigma^{3})$ fluctuation and $O(\sigma^{4})$ bias. One must not additionally invert the deterministic shrinkage, which would re-amplify precisely the directions the ridge protects. In practice the bias removed is small (Section~\ref{sec:eval:bias}). The squared mechanism bias of one run is at most $0.05\%$ of the mean squared error around $\btheta_\lambda$ at the previous setting and $0.19\%$ under setting I (cell averages). The correction lowers that error by about $0.5\%$ at the previous setting and by $1.4$--$1.7\%$ under setting I while the ridge is on, mostly by reducing variance. The correction is therefore an option of the protocol, off in our evaluation.


% ==============================================================
\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:bias}}
\label{app:proof}
% ==============================================================

Throughout, condition on the sample so that $\bG$, $\bc$ and $\hat{\bbeta} = \bG^{-1}\bc$ are fixed, and write $\bP = \bG^{-1}$ and $\bH = \bP\bE$.

\paragraph{Step 1: Neumann expansion on $\mathcal{E}_{\eta}$.}
On $\mathcal{E}_{\eta}$ the spectral radius of $\bH$ is at most $\eta < 1$ (Remark~\ref{rem:validity}), so
\[
  \tilde{\bbeta} = (\bG + \bE)^{-1}(\bc + \bff) = (\bI + \bH)^{-1}\bP(\bc + \bff)
  = \bigl(\bI - \bH + \bH^{2} - \bH^{3} + \bH^{4}(\bI + \bH)^{-1}\bigr)\bigl(\hat{\bbeta} + \bP\bff\bigr),
\]
and $\|\bH^{4}(\bI + \bH)^{-1}\|$ is bounded by a constant times $\|\bE\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{4}/(1 - \eta)$ in the $\bG$-norm.

\paragraph{Step 2: odd terms and the moment noise vanish.}
$\mathcal{E}_{\eta}$ is invariant under $\bE \mapsto -\bE$, $\bE$ is symmetric about zero, and $\bff$ is independent of $\bE$ and symmetric about zero. Hence $\E[\bH^{m} \mid \mathcal{E}_{\eta}] = 0$ for odd $m$, and every term containing $\bP\bff$ has zero conditional expectation. Therefore
\[
  \E[\tilde{\bbeta} \mid \mathcal{E}_{\eta}] = \hat{\bbeta} + \E[\bH^{2} \mid \mathcal{E}_{\eta}]\,\hat{\bbeta} + O(\sigma^{4}).
\]

\paragraph{Step 3: removing the conditioning.}
\[
  \E[\bH^{2} \mid \mathcal{E}_{\eta}] - \E[\bH^{2}] = \bigl(\E[\bH^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\eta}}] - \E[\bH^{2}]\Pr(\mathcal{E}_{\eta})\bigr)/\Pr(\mathcal{E}_{\eta}),
\]
whose norm is at most $2\bigl(\E\|\bH\|^{4}\bigr)^{1/2}\Pr(\mathcal{E}_{\eta}^{c})^{1/2}/\Pr(\mathcal{E}_{\eta}) = O\bigl(\sigma^{2}e^{-p(\eta\vartheta_0 - 1)^{2}/2}\bigr)$ by Cauchy--Schwarz and Remark~\ref{rem:validity}.

\paragraph{Step 4: the second-moment contraction.}
Write $\mathbf{p} = (P_{11}, \dots, P_{pp})^{\top}$ for the diagonal of $\bP$ and $\mathbf{X} = (\chi_{ij})$.

\begin{lemma}
For any fixed symmetric $\bP$, with $\circ$ the Hadamard product,
$\E[\bE\bP\bE] = \sigma^{2}\bigl(\mathbf{X} \circ \bP + \diag(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{p}) - \PiO\diag(\mathbf{p})\bigr)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Entrywise, $\E[(\bE\bP\bE)_{ab}] = \sum_{i,j} P_{ij}\E[E_{ai}E_{jb}]$, and~\eqref{eq:noise_cov} gives $\E[E_{ai}E_{jb}] = \sigma^{2}\chi_{ai}(\delta_{aj}\delta_{ib} + \delta_{ab}\delta_{ij} - \delta_{ai}\delta_{aj}\delta_{ab})$. Summing against the three terms gives $\sigma^{2}\chi_{ab}P_{ab}$, $\sigma^{2}\delta_{ab}\sum_{i}\chi_{ai}P_{ii}$, and $-\sigma^{2}\delta_{ab}\chi_{aa}P_{aa}$. With $\chi_{aa} = \mathbf{1}[a \in O]$ these are the $(a,b)$ entries of $\mathbf{X} \circ \bP$, $\diag(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{p})$ and $-\PiO\diag(\mathbf{p})$.
\end{proof}

Writing $\chi_{ij} = 1 - (\PiR)_{ii}(\PiR)_{jj}$ turns the first two terms into projector form: $\mathbf{X} \circ \bP = \bP - \PiR\bP\PiR$ and $\diag(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{p}) = \tr(\bP)\bI - \tr(\PiR\bP)\PiR$. Left-multiplying by $\bP$ and using $\PiO\diag(\mathbf{p}) = \diag(\bP)\PiO$,
\[
  \E[\bP\bE\bP\bE] = \sigma^{2}\bigl[\bP^{2} - \bP\PiR\bP\PiR + \tr(\bP)\bP - \tr(\PiR\bP)\bP\PiR - \bP\diag(\bP)\PiO\bigr].
\]

\paragraph{Step 5: regrouping.}
Substituting $\PiO = \bI - \PiR$, the first four terms collapse to $(\bP^{2} + \tr(\bP)\bP)\PiO + (\bP\PiO\bP + \tr(\PiO\bP)\bP)\PiR$, which gives $\E[\bH^{2}] = \sigma^{2}M(\bP)$. Combining with Steps 2 and 3 proves the theorem. \qed

\paragraph{Degenerate check.}
With $\PiO = \bI$ and $\PiR = \mathbf{0}$ the formula gives $\sigma^{2}(\bP^{2} + \tr(\bP)\bP - \bP\diag(\bP))$; for $p = 1$ this is $\sigma^{2}a^{-2}\hat{\bbeta}$, matching a direct expansion of $c/(a + e)$ with $\mathrm{Var}(e) = \sigma^{2}$.

\paragraph{Extension to ridge (Theorem~\ref{thm:ridge}).}
Replacing $\bG$ by $\bG_{\lambda} = \bG + \lambda\Psi$ with $(\lambda, \Psi)$ independent of $(\bE, \bff)$ leaves $\bE$, its covariance and $\bff$ untouched, and Steps 1--5 hold verbatim with $\bP \mapsto \bP_{\lambda}$. If $\lambda$ depended on $\bE$, Step 2 would fail: $\bP_{\lambda}$ would no longer be fixed under the conditional expectation.

% ==============================================================
\section{Labeled PSI with an OPRF and record alignment}
\label{app:psi}
% ==============================================================

$\Rp$ must learn $I$ and the alignment $\pi$, while $\Op$ learns only public sizes. We use labeled PSI in the style of Chen, Laine and Rindal~\cite{chen2017fast,chen2018labeled,cong2021labeled} on OPRF values. The construction runs under BFV with $\Rp$'s own key, whose exact arithmetic modulo $t$ is needed for the zero test and label recovery. Algorithm~\ref{alg:link} gives the steps; the toy example deck works them through with numbers.

\paragraph{Pre-processing at $\Op$ (fresh every run).} $\Op$ pads its scope register to $\bar n_O$, draws a uniformly random permutation $\tau$ of its rows and a fresh OPRF key $k$. Each row $j$ becomes the item $F_k(\mathrm{id}_j)$, mapped into $\Z_t^F$ as $F$ field elements (\emph{felts}, as in APSI) that each avoid the padding root $t - 1$, with label $\tau(j)$ encrypted under $F$ independent keys derived from $F'_k(\mathrm{id}_j)$, one per felt ($F = 2$ in the evaluation, so items have $64$ bits). Felt $f$ of a bin occupies slot $f N_{\mathrm{bins}} + b$, that is, ciphertext $f$; each slot has its own membership and label polynomial, and the items of a partition differ in every felt. Each item is hashed into all $h$ candidate bins; each bin is split into $\alpha$ partitions of at most $D$ items, where $D$ and $\alpha$ are public functions of $(\bar n_O, h, N_{\mathrm{bins}}, d_{\mathrm{cap}})$ chosen so that overflow has probability at most $\delta_{\mathrm{psi}}$. Every partition's membership polynomial is padded with the root $t - 1$ to degree exactly $D$, and its label polynomial interpolates (item, encrypted label) pairs.

\paragraph{Query at $\Rp$.} $\Rp$ pads its identifiers to $n_R^{\max}$, obtains $F_k$ and $F'_k$ of each through the OPRF, cuckoo-hashes~\cite{pagh2004cuckoo} the items into $N_{\mathrm{bins}}$ bins with the same $h$ hash functions (at most one item per bin), and sends BFV encryptions of the slot-wise powers $\mathbf{w}^{2^{i}}$, $i < W$, with $W = \lceil \log_{2} D \rceil + 1$.

\paragraph{Evaluation and reply at $\Op$.} $\Op$ forms the encryptions of $\mathbf{w}^{d}$, $d \le D$, as products of window ciphertexts, evaluates the membership and label polynomials of each partition as plaintext-coefficient linear combinations, masks with $\mathbf{r}_{1}, \mathbf{r}_{2}$ uniform in $(\Z_{t}^{\ast})^{N_{\mathrm{bins}}}$ from a CSPRNG, and returns
\[
  \textsc{SanitizeBFV}\bigl(\mathbf{r}_{1} \odot P(\mathbf{w})\bigr), \qquad
  \textsc{SanitizeBFV}\bigl(\mathbf{r}_{2} \odot P(\mathbf{w}) + L(\mathbf{w})\bigr).
\]
The first is $0$ in a bin iff $\Rp$'s item there is in $\Op$'s set, and the second is then that item's encrypted label; otherwise both are uniform. The mask $\mathbf r_1$ hides $P(\mathbf w)$ at non-matches, which would otherwise carry information about $\Op$'s items. \textsc{SanitizeBFV} and its parameters $(\kappa_\psi, B_\psi, q')$ are discussed under G4 in Section~\ref{sec:input}.

\paragraph{Recovery at $\Rp$.} For each occupied bin and partition, $\Rp$ declares a match only if the flag is zero in all $F$ felt slots of the bin \emph{and} the $F$ label felts, each opened under its own key derived from $F'_k$ of $\Rp$'s item, give the same position; that position is $\pi(i) = \tau(j)$. A true match always passes both tests. Flags that are zero in every felt but whose labels disagree come from felts matched by different items of the partition, which the zero test alone allows, and $\Rp$ treats them as no match, without an abort. $\Rp$ aborts the run if an identifier gets two different positions (ambiguous), if a position lies outside $[1, \bar n_O]$, or if two identifiers get the same position; an honest $\Op$ assigns distinct positions, so only a false match causes any of these. When a bin has more than one partition ($\alpha > 1$; $\alpha = 5$ at $\bar n_O = 10^6$ in the evaluation parameters), $\Rp$ also learns which partition flags its match. That index depends on the loads and insertion order of $\Op$'s items in the bin, which are pseudorandom to $\Rp$ outside $I$, and the simulator of G4 reproduces it (Section~\ref{sec:input}).

\begin{remark}[Collision probability]
A false match requires a query and the items of one partition of its bin to share all $F$ felt values, possibly from different items. Felts are uniform on $t - 1$ values (up to the bias of reducing a $256$-bit hash, which the implementation includes), independent across felts, and the items of a partition differ in every felt, so with partition loads $c_a \le D$ summing to at most the public load bound $B$,
\[
  p_{\mathrm{felt}} = \frac{n_R^{\max}\, S_F}{(t - 1)^F}, \qquad S_F = \lfloor B/D \rfloor D^F + (B \bmod D)^F,
\]
bounds the probability that some query has all $F$ flags zero in some partition. Such an event passes the label test only if $F - 1$ independent keys of the query hit given values, and only an event that passes can abort the run (ambiguous, out of range, or repeated position) or change the output:
\[
  p_{\mathrm{pass}} = p_{\mathrm{felt}}\, t^{-(F - 1)}, \qquad p_{\mathrm{silent}} = p_{\mathrm{pass}}\, \bar n_O / t .
\]
$p_{\mathrm{pass}}$ is the false-match term $p_{\mathrm{false}}$ of the slack of G1 and G2 (Section~\ref{sec:input}), and \textsc{Setup} refuses unless $p_{\mathrm{pass}} \le \delta_{\mathrm{psi}}$. With $F = 1$ there is no label test, $p_{\mathrm{pass}} = p_{\mathrm{felt}}$, and $t \approx 2^{32}$ fails the constraint at every evaluation size ($p_{\mathrm{pass}} = 2^{-18.8}$ at $\bar n_O = 4{,}096$, $n_R^{\max} = 600$; the earlier per-item estimate $n_R^{\max}\bar n_O h/(N_{\mathrm{bins}}(t-1))$ gave $8.5 \times 10^{-5}$ at $10^6$ and $n_R^{\max} = 2{,}000$). The evaluation uses $F = 2$, $N_{\mathrm{bins}} = 2^{14}$, $h = 3$, $D = 64$. At $\bar n_O = 10^6$ ($B = 308$, $\alpha = 5$) and $n_R^{\max} = 20{,}000$: $p_{\mathrm{felt}} = 2^{-35.5}$, which alone exceeds $\delta_{\mathrm{psi}} = 2^{-40}$ and is why the label test is part of the match rule; $p_{\mathrm{pass}} = 2^{-67.5}$ and $p_{\mathrm{silent}} = 2^{-79.6}$. The per-item formula with $F$ felts, $n_R^{\max}\bar n_O h/(N_{\mathrm{bins}}(t-1)^2)$, which counts only collisions of a whole item, gives $2^{-42.2}$. At $n_R^{\max} = 100{,}000$, $p_{\mathrm{pass}} = 2^{-65.2}$. Two felts double the query, the powers and the replies per cuckoo table at the same $N_{\mathrm{bins}}$; \textsc{SanitizeBFV}'s conditions are unchanged, since every reply ciphertext is as before.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Pre-shuffle necessity]
Without $\tau$, the positions $\pi(i)$ recovered by $\Rp$ would reflect $\Op$'s original database ordering (e.g.\ by region or registration date), leaking attributes outside the DP guarantee. The fresh shuffle makes positions uniformly random. It must be applied by $\Op$ inside the protocol, not assumed of the input files.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Linkage privacy]
$\Op$ sees $n_R^{\max}$ uniformly random group elements in the OPRF and BFV ciphertexts under $\Rp$'s key in the PSI, so it learns neither $I$ nor $n_R$.
\end{remark}

The companion proofs document~\cite{vfrproofs} (Part~A: cryptography and DP~\cite{vfrproofsA}; Part~B: sensitivity and statistics~\cite{vfrproofsB}; and a composition appendix on composition across runs and the hockey-stick ledger~\cite{vfrproofsC}) labels every step as exact, first order, asymptotic, numerical or open. Several results were first written in the companion book on the protocol and added to the proofs after a second reading that checked every step and constant (Part~A, Sections~4, 7 and~9; Part~B, Sections~S7--S10). Table~\ref{tab:status} summarizes it.

Worst-case $B_{\mathrm{tot}}$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:btot}) & proven, explicit & $\approx 2^{25.3}$ for the circuit as written with centred key switching; $\approx 2^{26.6}$ for OpenFHE's key switching, proven for the model it defines, whose match with OpenFHE~1.5.1 rests on reading its source; assumes (M5) and honestly sampled errors; measured errors $7.8$--$8.7$ bits below it \\

Output privacy (Theorem~\ref{thm:output}) & proven (hybrids) & RLWE for the re-randomizer and plain RLWE for $\Op$'s public-key share; $\Rp$'s errors honestly sampled; slack $\gamma$ statistical, not negligible in $\lambda_{\mathrm{sec}}$; standard simulation jointly with $\Rp$'s output \\
\textsc{Calibrate} & proven & its outputs meet the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:output} and the no-wraparound condition \\
Input and cohort privacy (G1, G2) & proven & $\Op$'s view only; simulators, up to the slack $p_{\mathrm{cuckoo}} + p_{\mathrm{false}}$ of $\Rp$'s observable aborts; $p_{\mathrm{cuckoo}} \le 2^{-49.1}$ at $n_R^{\max} = 2{,}000$ and $\le 2^{-41.1}$ up to $100{,}000$ (balanced tables) for an insertion that finds an assignment whenever one exists; $p_{\mathrm{false}} = p_{\mathrm{pass}} \le 2^{-65}$ with two felts \\

G4 with \textsc{SanitizeBFV} & proven (random oracles, CDH) & first layer without circuit privacy; second layer proven for the textbook BFV model (evaluation noise $\le 2^{-31}$ after the switch), with OpenFHE's BFVrns not checked against the model \\
